Wave turbulence for a semilinear Klein-Gordon system
Anne-Sophie de Suzzoni, Annalaura Stingo and Arthur Touati

Abstract
In this article we consider a system of two Klein-Gordon equations, set on the d d -dimensional box of size L L , coupled through quadratic semilinear terms of strength ε \varepsilon and evolving from well-prepared random initial data. We rigorously derive the effective dynamics for the correlations associated to the solution, in the limit where L → ∞ L\rightarrow\infty and ε → 0 \varepsilon\rightarrow 0 according to some power law. The main novelty of our work is that, due to the absence of invariances, trivial resonances always take precedence over quasi-resonances. The derivation of the nonlinear effective dynamics is justified up time to δ ​ T \delta T , where T = ε − 2 T=\varepsilon^{-2} is the appropriate timescale and δ \delta is independent of L L and ε \varepsilon . We use Feynmann interaction diagrams, here adapted to a normal form reduction and to the coupled nature of our real-valued system. We also introduce a frequency decomposition at the level of the diagrammatic and develop a new combinatorial tool which allows us to work with the Klein-Gordon dispersion relation.



1
Introduction

1.1
Background and motivations

In this article, we study the nonlinear evolution of correlations associated to a semilinear Klein-Gordon system, which models a wave turbulence phenomenon. We begin by reviewing the relevant literature and then highlight the main novelty of our work: the impact of trivial resonances on the effective dynamics.

1.1.1
Wave turbulence

Wave turbulence studies how the distribution of a solution to a wave equation evolves when the initial data are random variables or when the equation includes a random forcing term. The concept was first introduced in
[
Pei29
,
BP56
]
and later, in the 1960s, emerged in the context of plasma physics
[
Ved67
,
ZS67
]
and fluid mechanics
[
Has62
,
Has63
,
BSB66
]
. Around the same time, it was popularized by Zakharov, one of his major contributions being the introduction of what is now called Kolmogorov–Zakharov spectrum, which models an energy transfer across spatial scales, see the article
[
Zak65
]
. Wave turbulence is a very active field in mathematical physics, as reflected in the work of Zakharov and others
[
ZLF92
,
Naz11
,
Gal23
]
.

Starting in the late 2000s, the mathematical community began to take an interest in weak turbulence, a subfield of wave turbulence where the initial data are assumed to be small. A major discovery by the aforementioned physicists was that the statistics of certain solutions to wave equations could be described using the so-called kinetic equations. This led to the derivation and study of kinetic equations that emerge from the statistical properties of wave equations.
The pioneer work
[
LS11
]
describes a mixing effect for the dynamics of discrete Schrödinger equations. This was followed by works on atmospheric primitive equations
[
dST14
,
dS15
]
and the Schrödinger equation
[
BGHS21
,
CG25
,
DH21
,
dS22b
,
HSZ24
]
. In parallel, a stochastic approach was developed in
[
DK20
,
DK23
,
DK21
]
. This line of research culminated in the works
[
DH24
,
DH23a
,
DH23b
,
DH23c
]
, where the authors rigorously derive the kinetic equation governing the evolution of correlations associated to the cubic Schrödinger equation and extend the derivation up to the time where the equation remains well-posed. The ideas developed in these papers were then applied to the long-time derivation of the Boltzmann equation in both Euclidean space and on the torus
[
DHM24
,
DHM25
]
. Meanwhile, in
[
ST21
]
, the authors derive a kinetic equation for atmospheric primitive equations.

The aforementioned mathematical works have been strongly influenced by the study of the Cauchy problem for various equations with random data, in particular the works
[
Bou94
,
BT08a
,
BT08b
,
DNY22
,
BDNY24
]
; the derivation of collisional and diffusive regimes for the Schrödinger equation with random potentials, in particular by
[
EY00
,
ESY08
]
; and the derivation theorem for the Boltzmann equation for classical particles, for instance
[
Lan76
]
.

In addition, several recent works address related issues. For example, we mention
[
ACG21
,
HRST22
,
FM24a
]
in inhomogeneous settings,
[
Ma22
,
dS23
]
in the context of fluids,
[
GH24
]
with damping and forcing and
[
DGHG23
]
for toy models in finite dimension. The study of the kinetic equation itself has been the subject of
[
EMV07
,
EMV08
]
,
[
EV15
]
,
[
CDG24
,
Men24
]
, and
[
Fao20
]
for a stochastic version. For a linear turbulence model, we mention the work of
[
ABC+23
]
. Related to the present work, we also mention the derivation of the continuous resonant system
[
FGH16
]
. Finally, some questions related to propagation of chaos were adressed in
[
dS22a
]
; some related to graph theory in
[
BT24
]
.

1.1.2
Trivial resonances and discrete wave turbulence

In the physics literature, many equations studied through the prism of wave turbulence are quadratic and real-valued, such as fluid primitive equations, see for instance the pioneer work on capillary waves by Zakharov and Filonenko
[
ZF67
]
. In the present work, we investigate the derivation of effective equations for the statistical dynamics of weakly interacting nonlinear waves starting from a
system
of equations with
real-valued
solutions. We believe that, in the generic case, there are not enough symmetries in the (system of) equation(s) to ensure that the effective dynamics is
kinetic
. We believe that, instead, they behave more like in the
discrete
wave turbulence or finite-box effects regime (see
[
DLN07
,
Kar94
,
Kar91
,
DKMV23
,
dS22b
]
) because trivial (exact) resonances do not cancel each other and play a significant part in the effective dynamics.

To better illustrate the significance of invariances for the correlations’ dynamics, let us come back to the well-understood case of the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation. In
[
DH23b
,
DH23a
]
, Deng and Hani explain the different roles played by exact resonances versus quasi-resonances in the derivation of the effective dynamics. The idea is to compare the “size” of the sets

E=\left\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}^{d}~\middle|~\omega(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3})-\omega(k_{1})+\omega(k_{2})-\omega(k_{3})=0\right\}

and

Q=\left\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}^{d}~\middle|~0\neq|\omega(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3})-\omega(k_{1})+\omega(k_{2})-\omega(k_{3})|\leq\varepsilon^{2}\right\},

which represent respectively exact resonances and quasi-resonances. Here,
L
is the size of the box,
\varepsilon
is the strength of the nonlinearity and
\omega(k)=|k|^{2}
is the dispersion relation of the Schrödinger equation. We remark that if
L
is fixed, the size of
E
is larger than that of
Q
, which is actually empty for small enough
\varepsilon
. This explains why the precedence of exact resonances over quasi-resonances is called finite-box effects. Set
E
always contains trivial resonances, that is
k_{1}=k_{2}
or
k_{2}=k_{3}
. As it happens, due to the
U(1)
invariance of the Schrödinger equation, the contributions of these trivial resonances cancel out and do not affect the effective dynamics. This is seen in the fact that the equation may be “Wick-renormalized” (see Section 2.1 in
[
DH23b
]
). In this case, the right set to consider is

\tilde{E}=\left\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}^{d}~\middle|~\omega(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3})-\omega(k_{1})+\omega(k_{2})-\omega(k_{3})=0\,\wedge\,k_{2}\neq k_{1}\,\wedge\,k_{2}\neq k_{3}\right\}.

It is then possible to find regimes between
\varepsilon
and
L
for which the size of
\tilde{E}
is smaller than the size of
Q
and the effective dynamics is of kinetic type. Note that in the case of fluid primitive equations, the invariance under
U(1)
is substituted by the invariance of the average (or
0
-mode) of the solution.

In the framework we consider here and which we describe in the next sections, these invariances no longer hold. Therefore, the set of exact resonances
E
(or rather its analogue in our context) has to be considered instead of
\tilde{E}
. Due to the trivial resonances,
there are no regimes in which
Q
takes precedence over
E
.
This impacts dramatically the effective dynamics, which in particular is not of kinetic type. Still, this effective dynamics is
nonlinear
and driven by the
trivial
resonances.

Remark 1.1
.

Note that in the context of the Schrödinger equation, exact resonances also play a role in the long time dynamics of the correlations but on timescales longer than the kinetic one, see the works on the so-called CR equation
[
FGH16
,
FM24b
]
.

1.1.3
The toy model

One possibility to investigate how the absence of invariance can affect the effective dynamics of correlations would be to consider two coupled nonlinear Schrödinger equations. However, we have chosen a different route motivated by the theoretical description in
[
GN17
]
of a turbulent behaviour of gravitational waves in the early age of the universe. Gravitational waves are famous vacuum solutions of the field equations of general relativity, the so-called Einstein equations. Despite the energy cascade being potentially mitigated by the expansion of the early universe (see
[
CN18
]
), deriving an effective dynamics for such waves is a mathematical challenge due to the nature of the Einstein equations.

In some coordinates, the Einstein vacuum equations rewrite as a system of quasilinear wave equations for the metric of the spacetime, with quadratic semilinear interaction terms. A good toy model for the Einstein vacuum equations is thus a system of wave equations on Minkowski spacetime with quadratic nonlinearities. Nevertheless, such a wave system presents low-frequency singularities, which induce specific analytic difficulties on which we do not wish to focus.
With this in mind, we instead study a system of two coupled quadratic Klein–Gordon equations, which may be viewed as a low-frequency regularization of wave equations. More precisely, we consider the following system

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta+m\right)u

\displaystyle=\varepsilon Q^{0}(v,v),

(1.1)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta+m\right)v

\displaystyle=\varepsilon Q^{1}(u,u),

set on the
d
-dimensional torus of size
L
and with
m>0
(see Section
1.2
below for the precise assumptions on
Q^{0}
and
Q^{1}
and on the initial data). System (
1.1
) obviously misses the geometric nature of the Einstein equations and understanding how general covariance might affect the effective dynamics of turbulent gravitational waves would be of great interest.

Remark 1.2
.

We chose to consider this specific class of nonlinearities because the resulting effective dynamics exhibit a particularly convenient structure. While other choices were possible, we selected this formulation to maintain clarity and avoid unnecessary complexity.

1.1.4
Presentation of the result

Our main result (see Theorem
1.1
for a precise statement) is the rigorous derivation of the effective dynamics for the correlations associated with (
1.1
), in the limit
L\to\infty
,
\varepsilon\to 0
and where the relation between
L
and
\varepsilon
is given in Theorem
1.1
. The Klein-Gordon system (
1.1
) does not display exact resonances and a normal form argument allows us to transform it into an equivalent system with cubic nonlinearities. This new cubic system does display exact resonances and moreover trivial resonances drive the nonlinear effective dynamics. As explained above, this discrete regime is ultimately linked to the fact that we consider a system and not a scalar equation (as opposed for instance to
[
Spo06
]
, where a single Klein-Gordon equation is considered on a lattice).

Remark 1.3
.

Note that our result does not compare to

•

[
GN17
]
, where
kinetic
wave turbulence is predicted for gravitational waves. This is mainly due to the gauge choice for the Einstein vacuum equations made in
[
GN17
]
, which reduces the dynamics to a scalar wave equation;

•

[
ACG21
]
, where, although there is no invariance under
U(1)
, kinetic wave turbulence is again observed. This is due to their setting being the Euclidean space, in which the set of exact resonances has zero measure.

After the normal form procedure, the correct timescale
T
to observe the effective dynamics can be proved to be
\varepsilon^{-2}
. One of the strength of our result is that we justify the approximation of the correlations by the effective dynamics up to times of order
\delta T
, where
\delta>0
is a small quantity
independent of
L
and
\varepsilon
. In other words, our derivation extends the validity of the effective dynamics up to a nontrivial timescale. In the context of wave turbulence, such a rigorous derivation has only been obtained for the Schrödinger equation in the pioneer work
[
DH23b
]
(and subsequently improved in
[
DH23a
]
), where the authors reach the so-called kinetic timescale.

An overview of our proof will be given in Section
1.4
, but we can already say that our result follows from a precise diagrammatic representation of the solution to the Klein-Gordon system, as in the aforementioned articles on other equations. However, the new features of the system we consider force us to develop a new diagrammatic adapted to the normal form procedure and an analysis adapted to the Klein-Gordon dispersion relation (which, as opposed to the Schrödinger one, forbids the application of number theory results). For this last aspect, we rely on a low/high-frequency analysis and we develop new combinatorial tools that take this decomposition into account.

Remark 1.4
.

Note that a low/high-frequency decomposition is also utilized in
[
LS11
]
and in the work on inhomogeneous settings
[
ACG21
]
, but in both cases it does not show up in the diagrammatic. See also Remark
1.9
below.

1.2
The Klein-Gordon system

In this section we present the system of two coupled Klein-Gordon equations we study in this article and introduce all the notations that are required to state Theorem
1.1
.

The system.

We consider the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} \left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta+m\right)u&=\varepsilon Q^{0}(v,v)\\
\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta+m\right)v&=\varepsilon Q^{1}(u,u)\\
(u,\partial_{t}u)_{|_{\{t=0\}}}&=(u_{\rm in},u^{\prime}_{\rm in})\\
(v,\partial_{t}v)_{|_{\{t=0\}}}&=(v_{\rm in},v^{\prime}_{\rm in})\end{aligned}\right.

(KG)

posed on the torus
L\mathbb{T}^{d}
of size
L>0
, with
m>0
and
\Delta=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{i}^{2}
the flat Laplacian. The solutions
u
and
v
are real-valued and the small parameter
\varepsilon>0
will be ultimately linked to
L
, see Theorem
1.1
.

The nonlinearities.

For
\eta\in\{0,1\}
, the nonlinearity
Q^{\eta}
is a symmetric bilinear map defined in Fourier space by

\displaystyle\widehat{Q^{\eta}(f,g)}(k)=\frac{1}{(2\pi L)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}Q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})\hat{f}(k_{1})\hat{g}(k_{2}),

where we consider the following definition of the Fourier transform for complex-valued functions defined on
L\mathbb{T}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\hat{u}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi L)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{L\mathbb{T}^{d}}e^{-ik\cdot y}u(y)\mathrm{d}y,\qquad u(x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi L)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}e^{ik\cdot x}\hat{u}(k),

where
x\in L\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
(with
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
).

Remark 1.5
.

Note that, with this convention, convolution and Parseval formulas read

\displaystyle\widehat{uv}(k)=\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\frac{\hat{u}(k_{1})\hat{v}(k_{2})}{(2\pi L)^{\frac{d}{2}}},\qquad\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(L\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\left\|\hat{u}\right\|_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d})}.

The functions
Q^{\eta}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
defined on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
are assumed to be symmetric, bounded together with their gradient and satisfy

\displaystyle Q^{\eta}(-\xi_{1},-\xi_{2})=\overline{Q^{\eta}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})},

so that
Q^{0}(v,v)
and
Q^{1}(u,u)
in (
KG
) are real-valued. We also assume

\displaystyle Q^{\eta}(\xi,-\xi)=0,

(1.2)

to ensure that the zero-mode of the solution is not affected by the nonlinearities.

Remark 1.6
.

We impose the assumption that nonlinearities do not affect the zero-mode to avoid additional technical complications that do not contribute to a deeper understanding of the underlying phenomenon. Without this assumption, the effective dynamics and the final stages of the proof become significantly more complex (see Remark
5.3
). Moreover, the main comparison estimates (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) fail to hold for
k=0
.
Nevertheless, the general analysis remains valid without (
1.2
) (see Remark
4.1
).

The initial data.

The initial data for
u
and
v
in (
KG
) are defined as follows. We consider
\left(\left(\mu^{0}_{k},\mu^{1}_{k}\right)\right)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}
a family of independent Gaussians variables in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that, for all
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mu^{\eta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mu^{\eta}_{k}\mu^{\eta^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mu^{\eta}_{k}\overline{\mu^{\eta^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{k-k^{\prime}}M^{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}(k),

(1.3)

where
M^{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and satisfy
M^{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=\overline{M^{\eta^{\prime},\eta}}
and
\left|M^{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\right|^{2}\leq\left|M^{\eta,\eta}\right|\left|M^{\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}\right|
. We also assume that functions
M^{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}
are supported in the ball
B(0,R)
centered at the origin of radius
R>0
. We then define
(u_{\rm in},u^{\prime}_{\rm in})
and
(v_{\rm in},v^{\prime}_{\rm in})
in Fourier by

\displaystyle\widehat{u_{\rm in}}(k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2\left\langle k\right\rangle}\left(\mu^{0}_{k}+\overline{\mu^{0}_{-k}}\right),

\displaystyle\widehat{u^{\prime}_{\rm in}}(k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2i}\left(\mu^{0}_{k}-\overline{\mu^{0}_{-k}}\right),

(1.4)

\displaystyle\widehat{v_{\rm in}}(k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2\left\langle k\right\rangle}\left(\mu^{1}_{k}+\overline{\mu^{1}_{-k}}\right),

\displaystyle\widehat{v^{\prime}_{\rm in}}(k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2i}\left(\mu^{1}_{k}-\overline{\mu^{1}_{-k}}\right),

where the Japanese bracket is defined by
\langle k\rangle\vcentcolon=\sqrt{m+|k|^{2}}
. This choice of initial data is such that
u_{\rm in}
,
u^{\prime}_{\rm in}
,
v_{\rm in}
and
v^{\prime}_{\rm in}
are real-valued.

First order formulation.

In order to transform the second-order system (
KG
) into a first-order one, we introduce
H\vcentcolon=\sqrt{-\Delta+m}
and

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=Hu+i\partial_{t}u,\qquad V\vcentcolon=Hv+i\partial_{t}v.

(1.5)

These new unknowns satisfy the following half-Klein-Gordon system

\left\{\begin{aligned} i\partial_{t}U&=HU-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}Q^{0}\left(H^{-1}\left(V+\bar{V}\right),H^{-1}\left(V+\bar{V}\right)\right),\\
i\partial_{t}V&=HV-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}Q^{1}\left(H^{-1}\left(U+\bar{U}\right),H^{-1}\left(U+\bar{U}\right)\right),\\
(U,V)_{|_{\{t=0\}}}&=(U_{\rm in},V_{\rm in}).\end{aligned}\right.

(1.6)

From (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) we deduce that the Fourier transforms of
U_{\rm in}
and
V_{\rm in}
are simply given by

\displaystyle\widehat{U_{\rm in}}(k)=\mu_{k}^{0},\qquad\widehat{V_{\rm in}}(k)=\mu_{k}^{1}.

In order to screen the linear evolution in system (
1.6
), we introduce

\displaystyle X^{0}(t)\vcentcolon=e^{itH}U(t),\qquad X^{1}(t)\vcentcolon=e^{itH}V(t).

Moreover, in order to get the most concise system for
X^{0}
and
X^{1}
, we make use of two notations: for
\eta\in\{0,1\}
we set
\bar{\eta}=1-\eta
and if
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\iota\in\{\pm\}
then
z^{\iota}
denotes
z
if
\iota=+
or
\bar{z}
if
\iota=-
. Note that in Fourier space this translates to
\widehat{u^{\iota}}(k)=\widehat{u}(\iota k)^{\iota}
. Using these notations, system (
1.6
) for
(U,V)
rewrites as the following integral equation

\widehat{X^{\eta}}(t,k)=\mu_{k}^{\eta}+\frac{i\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})e^{i\tau\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta}}}(\tau,\iota_{1}k_{1})^{\iota_{1}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta}}}(\tau,\iota_{2}k_{2})^{\iota_{2}}\mathrm{d}\tau,

(1.7)

where we defined

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}_{k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\langle k_{1}+k_{2}\right\rangle-\iota_{1}\left\langle k_{1}\right\rangle-\iota_{2}\left\langle k_{2}\right\rangle,

(1.8)

\displaystyle q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{Q^{\eta}\left(k_{1},k_{2}\right)}{4(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}\langle k_{1}\rangle\langle k_{2}\rangle}.

(1.9)

Normal form.

The choice of the Klein-Gordon dispersion relation with a non-zero mass implies the absence of first order resonances as shown in the next lemma.

Lemma 1.1
.

For all
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}_{a,b}\right|}\lesssim\langle a\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle b\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(1.10)

Proof.

If
(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})=(-,-)
then (
1.10
) simply follows from
\langle a\rangle\geq\sqrt{m}
, which implies both
\Delta^{(-,-)}_{a,b}\geq 3\sqrt{m}
and
\langle a\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle b\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\sqrt{m}
. For
(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})=(+,+)
, we first note the identity

\displaystyle-\Delta^{(+,+)}_{a,b}\Delta^{(-,-)}_{a,b}

\displaystyle=m+2\left(\left\langle a\right\rangle\left\langle b\right\rangle-a\cdot b\right).

(1.11)

Using also the triangle inequality
\left\langle a+b\right\rangle\lesssim\left\langle a\right\rangle+\left\langle b\right\rangle
to get
\Delta^{(-,-)}_{a,b}\lesssim\left\langle a\right\rangle+\left\langle b\right\rangle
, (
1.11
) implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\Delta^{(+,+)}_{a,b}\right|}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\left\langle a\right\rangle+\left\langle b\right\rangle}{\left\langle a\right\rangle\left\langle b\right\rangle-|a||b|}.

Multiplying by the conjugate quantity and using
|a||b|\leq\left\langle a\right\rangle\left\langle b\right\rangle
we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\Delta^{(+,+)}_{a,b}\right|}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\left(\left\langle a\right\rangle+\left\langle b\right\rangle\right)\left\langle a\right\rangle\left\langle b\right\rangle}{\left\langle a\right\rangle^{2}\left\langle b\right\rangle^{2}-|a|^{2}|b|^{2}}\lesssim\frac{\left(\left\langle a\right\rangle+\left\langle b\right\rangle\right)\left\langle a\right\rangle\left\langle b\right\rangle}{\left\langle a\right\rangle^{2}+\left\langle b\right\rangle^{2}},

where we also used
\left\langle a\right\rangle^{2}+\left\langle b\right\rangle^{2}\leq 2(m+|a|^{2}+|b|^{2})
. Using now
\left(\left\langle a\right\rangle+\left\langle b\right\rangle\right)^{2}\leq 2(\left\langle a\right\rangle^{2}+\left\langle b\right\rangle^{2})
and
2\left\langle a\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle b\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\left\langle a\right\rangle+\left\langle b\right\rangle
we finally obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\Delta^{(+,+)}_{a,b}\right|}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\left\langle a\right\rangle\left\langle b\right\rangle}{\left\langle a\right\rangle+\left\langle b\right\rangle}\lesssim\left\langle a\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle b\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}},

which proves (
1.10
) in the case
(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})=(+,+)
. Now for
(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})=(-,+)
, we distinguish two cases. First if
|b|\leq|a|
, then
\Delta^{(-,+)}_{a,b}\geq\left\langle a+b\right\rangle\geq\sqrt{m}
so that (
1.10
) holds. If
|a|\leq|b|
then we use
\Delta^{(-,+)}_{a,b}=-\Delta^{(+,+)}_{a+b,-a}
and (
1.10
) in the case
(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})=(+,+)
, which gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\Delta^{(-,+)}_{a,b}\right|}\lesssim\left\langle a+b\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle a\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left\langle b\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle a\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The case
(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})=(+,-)
is deduced from the case
(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})=(-,+)
using
\Delta^{(+,-)}_{a,b}=\Delta^{(-,+)}_{b,a}
. This concludes the proof of (
1.10
).
∎

The above lemma allows us to perform a normal form procedure on (
1.7
). More precisely, using integration by parts
one can show that (
1.7
) is equivalent to the fixed point problem

\displaystyle\widehat{X^{\eta}}(t,k)

(1.12)

\displaystyle=\widehat{X^{\eta}}_{\rm in}(k)

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})}{\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\left(e^{it\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,\iota_{1}k_{1})^{\iota_{1}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,\iota_{2}k_{2})^{\iota_{2}}-\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta}}}_{\rm in}(\iota_{1}k_{1})^{\iota_{1}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta}}}_{\rm in}(\iota_{2}k_{2})^{\iota_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{i\varepsilon^{2}}{L^{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=k\\
\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}e^{i\tau\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}}q^{\eta}_{\iota_{2}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\widehat{X^{\eta}}(\tau,\iota_{1}k_{1})^{\iota_{1}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta}}}(\tau,\iota_{2}k_{2})^{\iota_{2}}\widehat{X^{\eta}}(\tau,\iota_{3}k_{3})^{\iota_{3}}\mathrm{d}\tau,

where
\widehat{X^{\eta}}_{\rm in}(k)\vcentcolon=\mu^{\eta}_{k}
and where we defined

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\langle k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}\right\rangle-\iota_{1}\left\langle k_{1}\right\rangle-\iota_{2}\left\langle k_{2}\right\rangle-\iota_{3}\left\langle k_{3}\right\rangle,

(1.13)

\displaystyle q^{\eta}_{\iota}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{4\left\langle k_{1}+k_{3}\right\rangle}{m+2\left((k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3})\cdot k_{2}-\iota\left\langle k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}\right\rangle\left\langle k_{2}\right\rangle\right)}q^{\overline{\eta}}(k_{1},k_{3})q^{\eta}(k_{1}+k_{3},k_{2}).

(1.14)

Remark 1.7
.

Lemma
1.1
shows that there are no exact first order resonances but also that there might be high-frequency first order quasi-resonances. In this article they play no role since they are compensated by the decay of
q^{\eta}(a,b)
, which follows from the boundedness of
Q^{\eta}(a,b)
in (
1.9
).

1.3
Statement of the main theorem

Our main result concerns the derivation of an effective dynamics for the correlations associated to (
1.12
). This dynamics is governed by the following system satisfied by functions
\rho^{\eta}
(for
\eta=0,1
) and
\rho^{\times}
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\rho^{0}(t,\xi)&=4\rho^{0}(t,\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{0}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,\zeta)}\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta,\\
\partial_{t}\rho^{1}(t,\xi)&=4\rho^{1}(t,\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{1}(\xi,\zeta)\rho^{\times}(t,\zeta)\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta,\\
\partial_{t}\rho^{\times}(t,\xi)&=2i\rho^{\times}(t,\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(P_{+}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,\zeta)}+P_{-}(\xi,\zeta)\rho^{\times}(t,\zeta)\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta,\\
(\rho^{0},\rho^{1},\rho^{\times})_{|_{\{t=0\}}}&=\left(M^{0,0},M^{1,1},M^{0,1}\right),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.15)

where the kernels
N^{\eta}
and
P_{\pm}
are defined by

\displaystyle N^{\eta}(\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=q^{\eta}_{+}(\xi,\zeta,-\zeta)-\overline{q^{\eta}_{-}(\xi,-\zeta,\zeta)},

(1.16)

\displaystyle P_{\pm}(\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\overline{q^{1}_{\pm}(\xi,\pm\zeta,\mp\zeta)}-q^{0}_{\pm}(\xi,\pm\zeta,\mp\zeta).

(1.17)

We now state the main result of our article.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
s>\frac{d}{2}
,
\beta>2
and
A>0
. There exist
\delta_{0},L_{0}>0
such that if
0<\delta\leq\delta_{0}
,
L\geq L_{0}
and
\varepsilon\vcentcolon=L^{-\frac{1}{\beta}}
, the following holds:

(i)

There exists a set
\mathcal{E}_{L,A}
of probability larger than
1-L^{-A}
such that, if the initial datum
(U_{\rm in},V_{\rm in})
introduced in the previous subsection belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{L,A}
, equation (
1.12
) admits a unique solution
X^{\eta}\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,\delta\varepsilon^{-2}],H^{s}(L\mathbb{T}^{d})\right)
;

(ii)

There exists a unique solution
\left(\rho^{\eta},\rho^{\times}\right)\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,\delta],W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)
of (
1.15
);

(iii)

We have

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\left|\widehat{X^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|^{2}\right)-\rho^{\eta}(t,k)\right|

\displaystyle=0,

(1.18)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\widehat{X^{0}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\overline{\widehat{X^{1}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\right)-\rho^{\times}(t,k)\right|

\displaystyle=0.

(1.19)

Remark 1.8
.

We remark that, although
U_{\rm in}
and
V_{\rm in}
belong to any Sobolev space, they are homogeneous. In particular, their
H^{s}
norms are of order
L^{\frac{d}{2}}
. Consequently, relying solely on deterministic methods would only allow us to solve (
1.12
) up to the time scale
\varepsilon^{-2}L^{-d}
, which vanishes as
L\to\infty
, in contrast to the timescale achieved in Theorem 1.1.

We now make some comments on Theorem
1.1
.

The correlations of the Klein-Gordon system.

We can deduce from (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) the behavior of the correlations associated to the initial system (
KG
). First, note that the proof of (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) can be easily adapted to show that, for all
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\sup_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\widehat{X^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\widehat{X^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=0.

(1.20)

Using (
1.18
)-(
1.19
)-(
1.20
) and (
1.5
) we can obtain in the limit
L\to\infty
the following:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\left|\hat{u}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\rho^{0}(t,k)+\rho^{0}(t,-k)}{4\left\langle k\right\rangle^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\left|\hat{v}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\rho^{1}(t,k)+\rho^{1}(t,-k)}{4\left\langle k\right\rangle^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\hat{u}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\overline{\hat{v}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\rho^{\times}(t,k)+\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,-k)}}{4\left\langle k\right\rangle^{2}},

where all the above limits are uniform in
t\in[0,\delta]
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
. Note that the exact same limits hold if we replace
\hat{u}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
(resp.
\hat{v}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
) by
\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\widehat{\partial_{t}u}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
(resp.
\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\widehat{\partial_{t}v}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
). We also obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\hat{u}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\overline{\widehat{\partial_{t}u}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\rho^{0}(t,-k)-\rho^{0}(t,k)}{4i\left\langle k\right\rangle},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\hat{v}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\overline{\widehat{\partial_{t}v}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\rho^{1}(t,-k)-\rho^{1}(t,k)}{4i\left\langle k\right\rangle},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\hat{u}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\overline{\widehat{\partial_{t}v}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,-k)}-\rho^{\times}(t,k)}{4i\left\langle k\right\rangle},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\hat{v}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\overline{\widehat{\partial_{t}u}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\rho^{\times}(t,-k)-\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,k)}}{4i\left\langle k\right\rangle}.

The effective dynamics.

System (
1.15
) has a triangular structure since the third equation for
\rho^{\times}
does not involve
\rho^{0}
and
\rho^{1}
, and
\rho^{\times}
only appears in the source term of the first and second equations. We can actually solve these equations and obtain
(\rho^{0},\rho^{1})
in terms of
\rho^{\times}
:

\displaystyle\rho^{0}(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=M^{0,0}(\xi)\exp\left(4\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{0}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{\rho^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)}\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\tau\right),

(1.21)

\displaystyle\rho^{1}(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=M^{1,1}(\xi)\exp\left(4\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{1}(\xi,\zeta)\rho^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\tau\right),

where using (
1.9
), (
1.14
) and (
1.16
) one can show that

\displaystyle N^{\eta}(\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4(2\pi)^{d}\langle\xi\rangle\langle\zeta\rangle^{2}}\left(\frac{Q^{\overline{\eta}}(\xi,-\zeta)Q^{\eta}(\xi-\zeta,\zeta)}{m+2(\xi\cdot\zeta-\langle\xi\rangle\langle\zeta\rangle)}-\frac{\overline{Q^{\overline{\eta}}(\xi,\zeta)Q^{\eta}(\xi+\zeta,-\zeta)}}{m-2(\xi\cdot\zeta-\langle\xi\rangle\langle\zeta\rangle)}\right).

The expressions (
1.21
) suggests exponential decay or growth for
\rho^{0}
and
\rho^{1}
, which might be seen as an obstruction to a kinetic regime.

Regarding the equation for
\rho^{\times}
, we note that the kernels
P_{\pm}
are generically non-trivial since, using (
1.9
), (
1.14
) and (
1.17
), one can show that

\displaystyle 4(2\pi)^{d}P_{\pm}(\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\langle\zeta\rangle^{-2}}{m\pm 2\left(\xi\cdot\zeta-\left\langle\xi\right\rangle\left\langle\zeta\right\rangle\right)}\left(\overline{Q^{0}\left(\xi,\mp\zeta\right)}\overline{Q^{1}\left(\xi\mp\zeta,\pm\zeta\right)}-Q^{1}\left(\xi,\mp\zeta\right)Q^{0}\left(\xi\mp\zeta,\pm\zeta\right)\right).

Definitions (
1.16
)-(
1.17
) actually imply the relation
N^{0}-\overline{N^{1}}=\overline{P_{-}}-P_{+}
, which in turn yields that any solution
(\rho^{0},\rho^{1},\rho^{\times})
of (
1.15
) satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{\left|\rho^{\times}\right|}{\sqrt{\rho^{0}\rho^{1}}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

(1.22)

on the support of
M^{0,0}M^{0,1}
. This suggests that, despite the exponential decay or growth for
\rho^{0}
and
\rho^{1}
, the statistical correlation between
\widehat{X^{0}}
and
\widehat{X^{1}}
has constant modulus.

The expressions (
1.21
) and the identity (
1.22
) crucially rely on the triangular structure of (
1.15
), which would not hold without assumption (
1.2
) (see Section
1.4.4
below) or if we had considered different coupling terms in (
KG
) such as
(Q^{0}(u,v),Q^{1}(u,v))
. In these other situations, the effective dynamics one obtains in the limiting process is much more involved than (
1.15
).

1.4
Sketch of proof

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is based on a diagrammatic representation of the solution
X^{\eta}
to (
1.12
) in terms of trees. It is spread over four sections:

•

In Section
2
we define a sequence of approximate solutions to (
1.12
) and introduce our main combinatorial tools, that is signed coloured trees, couplings and bushes;

•

In Section
3
we prove the main estimate for the sequence of approximate solutions;

•

In Section
4
we construct an exact solution to (
1.12
) as an appropriately truncated Dyson series plus a remainder term;

•

In Section
5
we solve (
1.15
) and prove (
1.18
)-(
1.19
).

In Sections
1.4.1
,
1.4.2
,
1.4.3
and
1.4.4
below we give an overview of the proof and provide a heuristic derivation of the (
1.15
).

1.4.1
Approximate solution

We start by presenting the construction of a sequence of approximate solutions to (
1.12
), thus covering the general diagrammatic from Section
2
and the estimates from Section
3
.

The standard way to exploit the randomness of the initial data, and in particular (
1.3
), is to formally expand the solution
X^{\eta}
to (
1.12
) and obtain the so-called Dyson series
\sum_{n\geq 0}X^{\eta}_{n}
, which is a formal solution to (
1.12
). In order to estimate each term
X^{\eta}_{n}
and prove convergence of the Dyson series, we write each
X^{\eta}_{n}
as a sum over trees representing the combinatorial structure of the nonlinear terms in (
1.12
) when iterating Duhamel’s formula. Compared to the case of the cubic Schrödinger equation, we need to introduce new features to the diagrammatic in order to capture the peculiarities of our system:

(i)

our trees are made of binary and/or ternary nodes, as (
1.12
) contains both quadratic (coming from the normal form) and cubic interaction terms;

(ii)

we consider colour maps
c
that associate “colours” in
\{0,1\}
to each node. This is because we started with a
system
of equations and need to distinguish between
X^{0}
and
X^{1}
in (
1.12
);

(iii)

we consider sign maps
\varphi
that associate a sign
\{\pm\}
to each node. This is because we started with a system with real-valued solutions and need to take complex conjugation into account when passing to the first order half-Klein-Gordon system (
1.6
) and to (
1.12
).

In conclusion, the natural combinatorial objects representing
X^{\eta}_{n}
are
signed coloured trees with binary and ternary nodes
, and in Proposition
2.1
we prove that

\displaystyle X^{\eta,\iota}_{n}=\sum_{(A,\varphi,c)\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\eta,\iota}}F_{(A,\varphi,c)},

where
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\eta,\iota}
is the set of signed coloured trees of size
n
whose root has colour
\eta
and sign
\iota
, and where
F_{(A,\varphi,c)}
is defined recursively in Definition
2.7
. The estimate for
X^{\eta}_{n}
is inferred from the estimate of the correlations
\mathbb{E}\left(\big|\widehat{X^{\eta}_{n}}\big|^{2}\right)
, which are computed from the representation formula written above. In fact, the structure of the initial data and the Wick formula (see Equation (
2.42
) or
[
Jan97
]
) for the product of Gaussians allow us to represent the correlations as a sum over
couplings
, i.e. ordered pairs of signed coloured trees, with the property that the number of overall positive leaves equals that of overall negative leaves, equipped with a coupling map
\sigma
pairing leaves of different sign. In Proposition
2.3
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\left|\widehat{X^{\eta}_{n}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta,\eta,+,-}_{n,n}}\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k),

where
\mathcal{C}^{\eta,\eta,+,-}_{n,n}
is a set of couplings. Naively estimating
\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
over the time interval
t\in[0,\delta]
by
\delta^{n}
would lead to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\left|\widehat{X^{\eta}_{n}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|^{2}\right)\lesssim\delta^{n}n!,

(1.23)

where the factorial comes from counting the number of couplings of size
n
. This factorial growth, which is now a standard obstruction in the wave turbulence literature, cannot be beaten by the geometric factor
\delta^{n}
and this bound is useless to prove the convergence of the Dyson series
\sum_{n\geq 0}X^{\eta}_{n}
over relevant timescales.

To get a more refined estimate than (
1.23
), with
\delta
still independent of
L
and
\varepsilon
, is the hardest part of the proof. Similarly to
[
DH23b
]
, our goal is to identify the contributions that lead to a systematic
\varepsilon
gain in the estimate for
\widehat{F_{C}}
. As for trees, couplings have binary and/or ternary nodes. The quadratic terms in (
1.12
) show that each binary node naturally contributes to an
\varepsilon
gain in the estimate for
\widehat{F_{C}}
. This is
a priori
not the case for ternary nodes and we employ instead a low/high-frequency argument based on a cut-off function located at
\varepsilon^{\gamma}
(for some
\gamma\in(0,2)
). Introduced at the level of the diagrammatic, it distinguishes between
low and high ternary nodes
. For instance, the first node in the example below is low while the second is high:
low
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
|k_{1}+k_{2}|\leq\varepsilon^{\gamma}
k_{3}
high
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}
k_{1}
k_{2}
|k_{1}+k_{2}|>\varepsilon^{\gamma}
k_{3}

Contributions of high and low nodes are estimated differently:

•

on the one hand, we estimate high nodes following the strategy of
[
LS11
]
, namely via oscillating integrals and spanning trees. We show that most high node contributes to
\widehat{F_{C}}
with a gain of
\varepsilon^{2-\gamma}
. Note that when estimating oscillating integrals, we cannot rely on number theory arguments as in
[
BGHS21
]
and subsequent works since the Klein-Gordon dispersion relation is inhomogeneous;

•

on the other hand, we estimate low nodes using the constraint
|k_{1}+k_{2}|\leq\varepsilon^{\gamma}
, which in most cases yields a gain of
\varepsilon^{\gamma d}
. However, when the pair of trees below the children contributing to the low-frequency interaction forms a coupling,
k_{1}+k_{2}
vanishes structurally and the low-frequency constraint does not provide any gain. It is then crucial to identify the non-trivial and independent low-frequency constraints. This is done with a new combinatorial object we call
bush
(see Section
2.8
), which moreover replaces the notion of skeleton from
[
DH23b
]
. Low ternary nodes with a self-coupled bush – i.e. a bush where all leaves are paired together – do not produce any gain in
\varepsilon
and are thus the main obstruction to the convergence of the Dyson series. Nevertheless, such nodes induce structural constraints on the coupling and do not contribute to the factorial loss of (
1.23
).

Using these ideas, we obtain the following key estimate for
\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
(see Proposition
3.1
):

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|\lesssim\delta^{n}\varepsilon^{\alpha\left(n-n_{\mathrm{scb}}\right)},

(1.24)

where
n
is the number of nodes,
n_{\mathrm{scb}}
is the number of self-coupled bushes and
\alpha>0
is universal. The refined estimate (
1.24
) holds for
n\leq|\ln\varepsilon|^{3}
and ultimately removes the factorial growth in the estimate for the correlations. This leads to the following estimate with high probability for
X^{\eta}_{n}
(see Corollary
3.1
):

\left\|X^{\eta}_{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,\varepsilon^{-2}\delta],H^{s}(L\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\lesssim\delta^{\frac{n}{2}}L^{\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{\beta}+A},

where
\beta
and
A
are as in Theorem
1.1
. This estimate shows that truncating the Dyson series
\sum_{n\geq 0}X^{\eta}_{n}
at some
N\sim\ln L
indeed produces an approximate solution of (
1.12
).

Remark 1.9
.

In
[
LS11
]
, the authors use a low/high-frequency cut-off similar to ours, except that it is not integrated in the diagrammatic but only in the equation. This is only possible because in
[
LS11
]
low-frequency interactions always yield a gain in
\varepsilon
, due to the continuous nature of the phase space there.

1.4.2
Exact solution

Here we give an overview of Section
4
, which is concerned with the resolution of (
1.12
) thus proving the first part of Theorem
1.1
.

We look for a solution
X^{\eta}
of (
1.12
) of the form

\displaystyle X^{\eta}=X_{\leq N}^{\eta}+v^{\eta},\quad\eta=0,1

where
X_{\leq N}\vcentcolon=\sum_{n\leq N}X_{n}
and
v
is a remainder that solves an equation of the form

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Id}-\mathfrak{L})(v)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{S}+\mathfrak{N}(v).

In the above equation,
\mathfrak{L}
is a linear operator,
\mathfrak{S}
is shown to be a small source term and
\mathfrak{N}
contains nonlinear terms, all depending on
X_{\leq N}
. The norm of the linear operator
\mathfrak{L}
is of order
N^{s+d}L^{\frac{1}{4}+d+A}
, which
a priori
forbids the inversion of
\mathrm{Id}-\mathfrak{L}
. This obstruction was already present in
[
DH23b
]
and we use the same strategy to overcome it, namely we show that the operator norm of the
N
-th iterates
\mathfrak{L}
can be made arbitrarily small if the truncation threshold
N
grows logarithmically with
L
. More precisely, we can show (see Proposition
4.10
) that for an arbitrary truncation threshold
N
, for
m\geq 0
and
s>0
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{L}^{m}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim{\delta}^{\frac{m}{2}}m^{s+d}N^{s+d}L^{\frac{1}{4}+d+A},

(1.25)

where the operator norm refers to the action of
\mathfrak{L}
on
\mathcal{C}([0,\varepsilon^{-2}\delta],H^{s}(L\mathbb{T}^{d}))
. Therefore, if both
N
and
m
are of order
\ln L
, we can choose
\delta
small enough to have
\left\|\mathfrak{L}^{m}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim L^{-\mu}(\ln L)^{2(s+d)}
for some
\mu>0
. This allows us to invert
\mathrm{Id}-\mathfrak{L}
for
L
large enough and to solve for
v
. We thus get a solution
X^{\eta}
to (
1.12
).

The proof of (
1.25
) is the key part of Section
4
, and it is once again based on a tree expansion of the iterates of
\mathfrak{L}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{n}(v)^{\eta,\iota}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(A,\varphi,c)\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{lin}}_{n,\eta,\iota}}F^{\mathrm{lin}}_{(A,\varphi,c)}.

Here,
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{lin}}_{n,\eta,\iota}
is a set of signed coloured
linear
trees (see Definition
2.6
), i.e. trees where the contribution of one of the leaves is
v
. The above representation formula encapsulates the transition between the recursive structure naturally associated to the iterates of
\mathfrak{L}
to the recursive structure of mixed trees with binary and ternary nodes. We adapt the diagrammatic to linear trees and estimate
\mathfrak{L}^{n}(v)^{\eta,\iota}
similarly to
X^{\eta,\iota}_{n}
.

1.4.3
Dynamics of the correlations

Finally, we discuss the content of Section
5
, which is concerned with the last two parts of Theorem
1.1
.

Our first task is to identify the correct resonant structures in the correlations that ultimately dominate in the limit
L\to\infty
. For this, we define
resonant nodes
and
resonant couplings
. On the one hand, resonant nodes are low ternary nodes whose bush is self-coupled and whose children satisfy particular sign conditions (we have already introduced them in Section
1.4.1
as obstructions to the convergence of the Dyson series). On the other hand, resonant couplings are couplings containing only resonant nodes. These are the relevant couplings to look at since couplings having at least one non-resonant node always allow an
\varepsilon
gain in the estimates, see Proposition
5.1
, and their contribution becomes negligible in the limit. Up to symmetries and cancellations, all nodes in a resonant coupling
C=(A,A^{\prime},\sigma)
have the following form
low
\iota_{1}
A_{1}
\iota_{2}
A_{2}
-\iota_{2}
A_{1}
\iota_{1}

where
\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
are signs and where the node’s bush is self-coupled. The triplet
(A_{1},A_{2},\sigma^{\prime})
, where
\sigma^{\prime}
is the restriction of
\sigma
to the leaves of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, is itself a coupling (see Lemma
5.1
), which we represent with a blue arrow in the above figure. In Lemma
5.6
and Proposition
5.2
we show that resonant coupling have almost a recursive structure. Namely, a resonant coupling is either trivial or of one the following non-exclusive forms:

low
\iota_{1}
A_{1}
\iota_{2}
A_{2}
-\iota_{2}
A_{3}
\iota_{1}
A^{\prime}
-\iota_{1}
low
\iota_{1}
A_{1}
\iota_{2}
A_{2}
-\iota_{2}
A_{3}
\iota_{1}
A^{\prime}
-\iota_{1}

where
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{3},A^{\prime})
together with the restriction of coupling map to their leaves form resonant couplings. To get a truly recursive structure, we show in Section
5.2.2
that resonant couplings with ordered nodes are in bijection with binary trees with ordered nodes. As a consequence, resonant couplings can be endowed with the recursive structure of binary trees. This structure is the one of quadratic equations, which explains the quadratic nature of the effective dynamics described by (
1.15
). Finally, note that the quantities in (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) are proved to converge to zero polynomially with respect to
L
.

1.4.4
Heuristic derivation of the effective dynamics

We conclude this introduction by providing a heuristic derivation of the effective dynamics (
1.15
). We look at the solution to (
1.12
) at the appropriate timescale and introduce
\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)\vcentcolon=\widehat{X^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
together with

\displaystyle\rho^{\eta}(t,k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left(\left|\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)\right|^{2}\right),\qquad\rho^{\times}(t,k)\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{Z^{0}}(t,k)\overline{\widehat{Z^{1}}(t,k)}\right).

We write
\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)
as the sum of the following three terms:

\displaystyle\widehat{Z^{\eta}}_{0}(t,k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\widehat{Z^{\eta}}_{\rm in}(k)

\displaystyle\widehat{Z^{\eta}}_{1}(t,k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})}{\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\left(e^{i\varepsilon^{-2}t\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,\iota_{1}k_{1})^{\iota_{1}}\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,\iota_{2}k_{2})^{\iota_{2}}-\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}_{\rm in}(\iota_{1}k_{1})^{\iota_{1}}\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}_{\rm in}(\iota_{2}k_{2})^{\iota_{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\widehat{Z^{\eta}}_{2}(t,k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=-\frac{i}{L^{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=k\\
\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}e^{i\varepsilon^{-2}\tau\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}}q^{\eta}_{\iota_{2}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(\tau,\iota_{1}k_{1})^{\iota_{1}}\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(\tau,\iota_{2}k_{2})^{\iota_{2}}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(\tau,\iota_{3}k_{3})^{\iota_{3}}\mathrm{d}\tau.

In the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\widehat{Z^{\eta}}_{1}(t,k)
becomes negligible and can be discarded. The same can be said for the contributions to
\widehat{Z^{\eta}}_{2}
for which the resonant factor
\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}
is non-zero, since integration by parts produces a factor
\varepsilon^{2}
. While quasi-resonances would only be observed at times of order
\varepsilon^{-4}
(since the strength of the cubic nonlinearities is
\varepsilon^{2}
), the dynamics of
\widehat{Z^{\eta}}
is lead by the exact resonances
\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}=0
, so we can assume that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{L^{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=k\\
\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3}\in\{\pm\}\\
\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}=0\end{subarray}}q^{\eta}_{\iota_{2}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,\iota_{1}k_{1})^{\iota_{1}}\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,\iota_{2}k_{2})^{\iota_{2}}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,\iota_{3}k_{3})^{\iota_{3}}.

Furthermore, Wick formula and propagation of chaos suggest that only trivial resonances contribute to the dynamics of the correlations
\rho^{\eta}
and
\rho^{\times}
. Trivial resonances are such that
k_{a}+k_{b}=0
,
k_{c}=k
,
\iota_{a}=-\iota_{b}
and
\iota_{c}=+
where
a,b,c\in\{1,2,3\}
are all distinct. Therefore, the above sum can be split into three terms and we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{L^{d}}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)\sum_{\ell,\iota}q^{\eta}_{\iota}(k,\ell,-\ell)\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,\iota\ell)^{\iota}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,\iota\ell)^{-\iota}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{i}{L^{d}}\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,k)\sum_{\ell,\iota}q^{\eta}_{+}(\ell,k,-\ell)\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,\iota\ell)^{\iota}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,\iota\ell)^{-\iota}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{i}{L^{d}}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)\sum_{\ell,\iota}q^{\eta}_{-\iota}(\ell,-\ell,k)\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,\iota\ell)^{\iota}\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,\iota\ell)^{-\iota}.

The above right hand side can be written in a more compact form. We first expand the sum over
\iota
, make a change of variable
\ell^{\prime}=-\ell
in the sums where
\iota=-
, and then regroup terms using the symmetry of
q
in its first and third variables to get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2i}{L^{d}}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)\sum_{\ell}q^{\eta}_{+}(k,\ell,-\ell)\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,\ell)\overline{\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,\ell)}

\displaystyle-\frac{2i}{L^{d}}\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,k)\sum_{\ell}q^{\eta}_{+}(\ell,k,-\ell)\left|\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,\ell)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{2i}{L^{d}}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,k)\sum_{\ell}q^{\eta}_{-}(k,-\ell,\ell)\overline{\widehat{Z^{\overline{\eta}}}(t,\ell)}\widehat{Z^{\eta}}(t,\ell).

We now compute the equations satisfied by the correlations:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho^{0}(t,k)

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{Re}\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\overline{\widehat{Z^{0}}(t,k)}\partial_{t}\widehat{Z^{0}}(t,k)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{L^{d}}\rho^{0}(t,k)\sum_{\ell}\mathrm{Im}\left(q^{0}_{+}(k,\ell,-\ell)\overline{\rho^{\times}(\ell)}+q^{0}_{-}(k,-\ell,\ell)\rho^{\times}(\ell)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{4}{L^{d}}\mathrm{Im}\left(\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,k)}\sum_{\ell}q_{+}^{0}(\ell,k,-\ell)\rho^{0}(t,l)\right).

Recalling (
1.16
), we recognize the kernel
N^{0}(k,\ell)
in the following expression

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\Big(q^{0}_{+}(k,\ell,-\ell)\overline{\rho^{\times}(\ell)}+q^{0}_{-}(k,-\ell,\ell)\rho^{\times}(\ell)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{0}(k,\ell)\overline{\rho^{\times}(\ell)}\right),

which leads to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho^{0}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{L^{d}}\rho^{0}(t,k)\sum_{\ell}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{0}(k,\ell)\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,\ell)}\right)+\frac{4}{L^{d}}\mathrm{Im}\left(\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,k)}\sum_{\ell}q_{+}^{0}(\ell,k,-\ell)\rho^{0}(t,\ell)\right).

(1.26)

Exchanging the roles of
0
and
1
, we obtain similarly the equation satisfied by
\rho^{1}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho^{1}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{L^{d}}\rho^{1}(t,k)\sum_{\ell}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{1}(k,\ell)\rho^{\times}(t,\ell)\right)+\frac{4}{L^{d}}\mathrm{Im}\left(\rho^{\times}(t,k)\sum_{\ell}q_{+}^{1}(\ell,k,-\ell)\rho^{1}(t,\ell)\right).

(1.27)

As for the equation satisfied by
\rho^{\times}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho^{\times}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{Z^{0}}(t,k)\overline{\partial_{t}\widehat{Z^{1}}(t,k)}\right)+\mathbb{E}\left(\overline{\widehat{Z^{1}}(t,k)}\partial_{t}\widehat{Z^{0}}(t,k)\right)

(1.28)

\displaystyle=\frac{2i}{L^{d}}\rho^{\times}(t,k)\sum_{\ell}\left(P_{+}(k,\ell)\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,\ell)}+P_{-}(k,\ell)\rho^{\times}(t,\ell)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{2i}{L^{d}}\sum_{\ell}\left(\rho^{0}(t,k)\overline{q_{+}^{1}(\ell,k,-\ell)}\rho^{1}(t,\ell)-\rho^{1}(t,k)q_{+}^{0}(\ell,k,-\ell)\rho^{0}(t,\ell)\right)

where we recognized
P_{+}(k,\ell)
and
P_{-}(k,\ell)
as defined in (
1.17
). Collecting (
1.26
), (
1.27
) and (
1.28
) we have formally derived a system for the correlations
(\rho^{0},\rho^{1},\rho^{\times})
. Formally replacing the Riemann sums in these equations by integrals in the limit
L\to\infty
, we get

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\rho^{0}(t,\xi)&=4\rho^{0}(t,\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{0}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,\zeta)}\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta+4\mathrm{Im}\left(\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,\xi)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}q_{+}^{0}(\zeta,\xi,-\zeta)\rho^{0}(t,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\zeta\right),\\
\partial_{t}\rho^{1}(t,\xi)&=-4\rho^{1}(t,\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{Im}\left(\overline{N^{1}(\xi,\zeta)}\overline{\rho^{\times}(\zeta)}\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta+4\mathrm{Im}\left(\rho^{\times}(t,\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}q_{+}^{1}(\zeta,\xi,-\zeta)\rho^{1}(t,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\zeta\right),\\
\partial_{t}\rho^{\times}(t,\xi)&=2i\rho^{\times}(t,\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(P_{+}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{\rho^{\times}(t,\zeta)}+P_{-}(\xi,\zeta)\rho^{\times}(t,\zeta)\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta\\
&\quad+2i\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho^{0}(t,\xi)\overline{q_{+}^{1}(\zeta,\xi,-\zeta)}\rho^{1}(t,\zeta)-\rho^{1}(t,\xi)q_{+}^{0}(\zeta,\xi,-\zeta)\rho^{0}(t,\zeta)\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.29)

Compared to (
1.15
), we see that the system (
1.29
) contains extra coupling terms all involving the quantity
q^{\eta}_{+}(\zeta,\xi,-\zeta)
. Thanks to (
1.9
) and (
1.14
) we have

\displaystyle q^{\eta}_{+}(\zeta,\xi,-\zeta)=-\frac{Q^{\overline{\eta}}(\zeta,-\zeta)Q^{\eta}(0,\xi)}{4(2\pi)^{d}m\langle\xi\rangle\langle\zeta\rangle^{2}}.

Therefore, our assumption (
1.2
) on the nonlinearities
Q^{0}
and
Q^{1}
appearing in the starting system (
1.1
) reduces indeed (
1.29
) to (
1.15
). As suggested after the statement of Theorem
1.1
, the interactions between
\rho^{0}
,
\rho^{1}
and
\rho^{\times}
, though simplified by (
1.2
), remain non-trivial.

2
Diagrammatic

In this section we introduce our main tools to represent nonlinear interactions in the system (
1.12
) and to build an approximate solution:

•

In Section
2.1
we define a formal solution
X^{\eta}=\sum_{n\geq 0}X^{\eta}_{n}
. In Section
2.4
, each
X^{\eta}_{n}
is expressed as a sum over signed and coloured trees previously introduced in Sections
2.2
and
2.3
.

•

In Sections
2.5
and
2.6
we introduce and use the necessary materials to go into Fourier space.

•

In Section
2.7
we introduce couplings of signed and coloured trees, which will be used to estimate with high probability each
X^{\eta}_{n}
as well as the correlations.

•

Finally, in Section
2.8
we introduce bushes which will play a major combinatorics role in our proof.

2.1
Formal series expansion

In order to analyse the fixed point (
1.12
) and in particular the cubic interactions we will distinguish between the frequency regimes, high or low, of the interactions. In the case of the low frequency interactions, we also distinguish cases according to the position of the low interaction. To this purpose we introduce four smooth cutoff functions in frequency space.

Definition 2.1
.

Given a fixed
\gamma>0
and a fixed smooth function
\chi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\longrightarrow[0,1]
supported on
[0,2]
and equal to
1
on
[0,1]
, we define

\displaystyle\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}\ell}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{2}+k_{3}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{2}+k_{1}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{3}+k_{1}|),

\displaystyle\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}m}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{3}+k_{1}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{3}+k_{2}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{2}|),

\displaystyle\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}r}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{2}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{3}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{2}+k_{3}|),

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{high}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=1-\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}\ell}}-\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}m}}-\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}r}}.

Note that

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{high}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=

\displaystyle(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{2}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{3}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{2}+k_{3}|)

\displaystyle+\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{2}|)\times\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{3}|)\times\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{2}+k_{3}|)

\displaystyle+\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{2}|)\times\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{3}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{2}+k_{3}|)

\displaystyle+\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{2}+k_{3}|)\times\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{2}+k_{1}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{3}+k_{1}|)

\displaystyle+\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{3}+k_{1}|)\times\chi(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{3}+k_{2}|)\times(1-\chi)(\varepsilon^{-\gamma}|k_{1}+k_{2}|).

The cutoffs
\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}\ell}}
,
\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}m}}
and
\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}r}}
allow only one of the combinations to be low frequency. The case of
\chi_{\mathrm{high}}
is the negation of this, that is either all the combinations are in high frequencies or at least two of them are in low frequencies. The cutoff
\chi_{\mathrm{high}}
thus selects either high or very low frequencies.

We introduce the following time-dependent bilinear and trilinear operators acting on functions defined on the torus of size
L
:

\displaystyle\widehat{B^{\eta}_{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}(t,f,g)(k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\frac{q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})}{\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}e^{it\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}\hat{f}(k_{1})\hat{g}(k_{2}),

(2.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{C^{\eta}_{*,(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}}(t,f,g,h)(k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=k}\chi_{*}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})q_{\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})e^{it\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}}\hat{f}(k_{1})\hat{g}(k_{2})\hat{h}(k_{3}),

(2.2)

where
*
stands either for
{\mathrm{low-}\ell}
,
{\mathrm{low-}m}
,
{\mathrm{low-}r}
or
\mathrm{high}
.

Remark 2.1
.

Throughout the article, a sum over
*
always denote a sum over
{\mathrm{low-}\ell}
,
{\mathrm{low-}m}
,
{\mathrm{low-}r}
or
\mathrm{high}
, i.e

\displaystyle\sum_{*}=\sum_{*\in\{{\mathrm{low-}\ell},{\mathrm{low-}m},{\mathrm{low-}r},\mathrm{high}\}}.

This also applies to other mathematical symbols such as
\bigsqcup
.

We can now rewrite (
1.12
) in a more schematic form:

\displaystyle X^{\eta}(t)

\displaystyle=X^{\eta}_{\rm in}+\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}\left(B^{\eta}_{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}\left(t,X^{\bar{\eta},\iota_{1}}(t),X^{\bar{\eta},\iota_{2}}(t)\right)-B^{\eta}_{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}\left(0,X^{\bar{\eta},\iota_{1}}_{\rm in},X^{\bar{\eta},\iota_{2}}_{\rm in}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad-i\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{*}\sum_{\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3}\in\{\pm\}}C^{\eta}_{*,(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}\left(\tau,X^{\eta,\iota_{1}}(\tau),X^{\bar{\eta},\iota_{2}}(\tau),X^{\eta,\iota_{3}}(\tau)\right)\mathrm{d}\tau,

and define a formal solution as follows.

Definition 2.2
.

The sequence
\left(X^{\eta}_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N},\eta=0,1}
is defined recursively by

\displaystyle X_{0}^{\eta}(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=X_{\rm in}^{\eta},

(2.3)

\displaystyle X_{1}^{\eta}(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\varepsilon\sum_{\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}}\left(B^{\eta}_{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}\left(t,X_{0}^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{1}}(t),X_{0}^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{2}}(t)\right)-B^{\eta}_{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}\left(0,X_{0}^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{1}}(t),X_{0}^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{2}}(t)\right)\right),

(2.4)

\displaystyle X_{n+2}^{\eta}(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=-i\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{*}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n\\
\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}C^{\eta}_{*,(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}\left(\tau,X_{n_{1}}^{\eta,\iota_{1}}(\tau),X_{n_{2}}^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{2}}(\tau),X_{n_{3}}^{\eta,\iota_{3}}(\tau)\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

(2.5)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n+1\\
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}B_{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}^{\eta}\left(t,X_{n_{1}}^{\bar{\eta},\iota_{1}}(t),X_{n_{2}}^{\bar{\eta},\iota_{2}}(t)\right),

for
n\geq 0
.

Remark 2.2
.

•

One can indeed check easily that
X^{\eta}(t)=\sum_{n\geq 0}X^{\eta}_{n}(t)
formally solves (
1.12
).

•

By induction and thanks to our assumption (
1.2
) on the nonlinearities
Q^{\eta}
, for any
n\geq 1
,
\eta\in\{0,1\}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\widehat{X_{n}^{\eta}}(t,0)=0
.

2.2
Trees

We define recursively (rooted) trees adapted to our problem. They have four types
T_{{\mathrm{low-}\ell}}
,
T_{{\mathrm{low-}m}}
,
T_{{\mathrm{low-}r}}
, and
T_{\mathrm{high}}
of ternary nodes and two types
B_{\mathrm{st}}
(standard),
B_{\rm in}
(initial) of binary nodes. We write
\bot
the trivial tree (reduced to one leaf) and we set

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{\bot\},

(2.6)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{B_{\rm in}(\bot,\bot),B_{\mathrm{st}}(\bot,\bot)\},

(2.7)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n+2}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\bigsqcup_{*}\left\{T_{*}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})~\middle|~(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}}\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{3}},\;n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n\right\}

(2.8)

\displaystyle\sqcup\left\{B_{\mathrm{st}}(A_{1},A_{2})~\middle|~(A_{1},A_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}},\;n_{1}+n_{2}=n+1\right\},

for
n\geq 0
. The following standard definition introduces the different types of nodes and the leaves of a tree
A
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
be a tree. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{st}}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{\text{standard binary nodes of $A$}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\rm in}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{\text{initial binary nodes of $A$}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{B}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}_{\rm in}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{st}}(A).

For
*\in\{{\mathrm{low-}\ell},{\mathrm{low-}m},{\mathrm{low-}r},\mathrm{high}\}
we also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{*}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{\text{ternary nodes of $A$ of type $*$}\right\},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low-}\ell}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low-}m}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A),

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{high}}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(A).

Moreover, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{\text{leaves of $A$}\right\}.

We denote by
n_{\mathrm{st}}(A)
,
n_{\rm in}(A)
,
n_{B}(A)
,
n_{*}(A)
,
n_{\mathrm{low}}(A)
,
n_{T}(A)
and
n_{\ell}(A)
their respective cardinals. Finally, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}_{B}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{T}(A),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(A)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{r_{A}\right\},

where
r_{A}
denotes the root of
A.

Remark 2.3
.

By induction, if
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
then we have
n_{\ell}(A)=n+1
and
n=n_{B}(A)+2n_{T}(A)
. Therefore there exists a constant
\Lambda>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\#\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\leq\Lambda^{n}.

Indeed, a tree in
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
is uniquely determined by a sequence of symbols with
n_{B}
symbols of binary nodes and
n_{T}
symbols of ternary nodes and
n+1
symbols of leaves. Since there are two types of binary nodes and four types of ternary nodes, we deduce that the cardinal of
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{B}+2n_{T}=n}2^{n_{B}}4^{n_{T}}\begin{pmatrix}n_{B}+n_{T}+n+1\\
n_{B}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}n_{T}+n+1\\
n_{T}\end{pmatrix}\leq\sum_{n_{B}+2n_{T}=n}2^{n}2^{n_{B}+2n_{T}+2n+2}\leq 4(n+1)16^{n}.

We deduce that the cardinal is bounded by
4(32)^{n}
. For
n\geq 1
, this is less that
(128)^{n}
. For
n=0
, the cardinal of
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
is
1
such that we get the bound
\Lambda\geq 128
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
A\in\tilde{A}_{n}
be a tree.

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}(A)
, we denote by

\displaystyle\mathtt{children}(j)

the set of its children. More precisely, if
j=B_{*}(A_{1},A_{2})
for
*=\mathrm{st},{\rm in}
then
\mathtt{children}(j)=\{r_{A_{1}},r_{A_{2}}\}
; if
j=T_{*}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
for
*={\mathrm{low-}\ell},{\mathrm{low-}m},{\mathrm{low-}r},\mathrm{high}
then
\mathtt{children}(j)=\{r_{A_{1}},r_{A_{2}},r_{A_{3}}\}
.

•

If
j\in(\mathcal{N}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A))\setminus\mathcal{R}(A)
, we denote by

\displaystyle\mathtt{parent}(j)

the parent of
j
in
A
, and if
\mathcal{S}\subset(\mathcal{N}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A))\setminus\mathcal{R}(A)
we use the notation

\displaystyle\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{S})=\left\{\mathtt{parent}(j)~\middle|~j\in\mathcal{S}\right\}.

•

If
j\in(\mathcal{N}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A))\setminus\mathcal{R}(A)
, we define

\displaystyle\mathtt{siblings}(j)=\mathtt{children}\left(\mathtt{parent}(j)\right).

Note that
j\in\mathtt{siblings}(j)
.

Remark 2.4
.

We note that if
\mathcal{S}\subset(\mathcal{N}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A))\setminus\mathcal{R}(A)
then

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\#\mathtt{p}(\mathcal{S})_{1}+2\#\mathtt{p}(\mathcal{S})_{2}+3\#\mathtt{p}(\mathcal{S})_{3},

\displaystyle\#\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{S})

\displaystyle=\#\mathtt{p}(\mathcal{S})_{1}+\#\mathtt{p}(\mathcal{S})_{2}+\#\mathtt{p}(\mathcal{S})_{3},

where
\mathtt{p}(\mathcal{S})_{i}=\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}(A)~\middle|~\#\left(\mathtt{children}(j)\cap\mathcal{S}\right)=i\right\}
.

We introduce the standard parentality partial order relation
<
on the nodes and leaves of a tree.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
be a tree.

•

For
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(A)
, we say that
j<j^{\prime}
if there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
j_{1},\dots,j_{n+1}\in\mathcal{N}(A)
with
j_{1}=j
,
j_{n+1}=j^{\prime}
and such that

\displaystyle\forall i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,\;j_{i}\in\mathtt{children}{(j_{i+1})}.

•

Moreover, if
\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A)
and
j\in\mathcal{N}(A)
we say that
\ell<j
if there exists
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(A)
such that
\ell\in\mathtt{children}(j^{\prime})
and
j^{\prime}\leq j
.

Note that if
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A)
are such that
j<j^{\prime}
, then the sequence
j_{2},\dots,j_{n}\in\mathcal{N}(A)
linking
j
to
j^{\prime}
(i.e such that
j\in\mathtt{children}(j_{2})
,
j_{i}\in\mathtt{children}(j_{i+1})
and
j_{n}\in\mathtt{children}(j^{\prime})
) is unique. For this reason, for a given
j_{0}\in\mathcal{N}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A)
, the set
\{j\in\mathcal{N}(A):j_{0}\leq j\}
– which corresponds to the path from
j_{0}
to the root – is totally ordered.

2.3
Signed and coloured trees

To incorporate the fact that we work with a system of equations, with complex conjugates in the nonlinearities, we introduce the notion of a signed and coloured tree. Recall that if
\eta\in\{0,1\}
, then
\overline{\eta}=1-\eta
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
be a tree.

•

A
sign map
on
A
is a map

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathcal{L}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A)\longrightarrow\{\pm\}.

If
j
is a leaf or a branching node of
A
, then
\varphi(j)
is the sign of
j
.

•

A
colour map
on
A
is a map

\displaystyle c:\mathcal{L}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A)\longrightarrow\{0,1\},

such that

(i)

if
j
is a binary node, then
c(j^{\prime})=\overline{c(j)}
where
j^{\prime}
is any child of
j
,

(ii)

if
j
is a ternary node, then
c(\ell)=c(r)=c(j)
and
c(m)=\overline{c(j)}
where
\ell
,
m
and
r
denote respectively the left, middle and right child of
j
.

If
j\in\mathcal{L}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A)
, then
c(j)
is the colour of
j
.

•

If
\varphi
and
c
are a sign map and a colour map on
A
, then
(A,\varphi,c)
is called a
signed and coloured tree
.

•

For
\iota\in\{\pm\}
and
\eta\in\{0,1\}
we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\eta,\iota}_{n}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{(A,\varphi,c)\text{ signed and coloured tree}~\middle|~A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n},\;\varphi(r_{A})=\iota,\;c(r_{A})=\eta\right\}.

Remark 2.5
.

We note the following simple facts:

•

A colour map
c
on a tree
A
is uniquely determined by its value
c(r_{A})
at the root of
A
, opposite to a sign map
\varphi
on
A
which does not satisfy any recursive property (in particular there is no relation between the sign of a node and that of its children).

•

There exists some constant
\Lambda>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the cardinal of
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\eta,\iota}
is bounded by
\Lambda^{n}
. Indeed, the sign map is determined on the root, but there remains
n_{B}+n_{T}+n
signs to attribute. We deduce that
\#\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\eta,\iota}\leq 4^{n}\#\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
from which we deduce the bound.

Example 2.1
.

Below we give a graphical representation of the signed and coloured tree

\displaystyle(T_{\mathrm{high}}(B_{\rm in}(\bot,\bot),B_{\mathrm{st}}(\bot,\bot),B_{\mathrm{st}}(\bot,\bot)),\varphi,c_{0})

where
c_{0}
is the colour map obtained by attributing the colour
0
to the root. We have chosen the colour red to encode
\eta=0
, the colour yellow to encode
\eta=1
. The choice of the signs of the nodes and leaves is completely arbitrary.
high
+
in
+
🍂
-
🍂
-
st
+
🍂
+
🍂
+
st
+
🍂
-
🍂
+

2.4
Representation of
X_{n}
in terms of trees

The introduction of all this diagrammatic allows us to represent each element of the sequence
\left(X\eta_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
defined in
2.2
as the sum over trees representing nonlinear interactions. We first define recursively the contribution of a given coloured and signed tree.

Definition 2.7
.

If
(A,\varphi,c)
is a signed and coloured tree, we define
F_{(A,\varphi,c)}(t)
recursively:

•

We set

\displaystyle F_{(\bot,\iota,\eta)}(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=X^{\eta,\iota}_{\rm in},

(2.9)

where the sign and colour maps are identified with their single values
\iota\in\{\pm\}
and
\eta\in\{0,1\}
, and

\displaystyle F_{(B_{\rm in}(\bot,\bot),\varphi,c)}(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=-\varepsilon B^{\eta,\iota_{r}}_{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}\left(0,X^{\bar{\eta},\iota_{r}\iota_{1}}_{\rm in},X^{\bar{\eta},\iota_{r}\iota_{2}}_{\rm in}\right),

(2.10)

where
\iota_{r}
,
\iota_{1}
,
\iota_{2}
are the signs of the root, the left and right child, and
\eta
is the colour of the root.

•

If
A=B_{\mathrm{st}}(A_{1},A_{2})
, we set

\displaystyle F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\varepsilon B_{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}^{\eta,\iota_{r}}\left(t,F_{\left(A_{1},\iota_{r}\varphi_{|_{A_{1}}},c_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)}(t),F_{\left(A_{2},\iota_{r}\varphi_{|_{A_{2}}},c_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)}(t)\right),

(2.11)

where
\iota_{r}
and
\iota_{1}
,
\iota_{2}
are respectively the signs of the root of
A
and of the roots of
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
in
A
, and
\eta
is the colour of the root of
A
.

•

If
A=T_{*}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
, we set

\displaystyle F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=-i\iota_{r}\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{t}C_{*,\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}^{\eta,\iota_{r}}\left(\tau,F_{\left(A_{1},\iota_{r}\varphi_{|_{A_{1}}},c_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)}(\tau),F_{\left(A_{2},\iota_{r}\varphi_{|_{A_{2}}},c_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)}(\tau),F_{\left(A_{3},\iota_{r}\varphi_{|_{A_{3}}},c_{|_{A_{3}}}\right)}(\tau)\right)\mathrm{d}\tau,

(2.12)

where
\iota_{r}
and
\iota_{1}
,
\iota_{2}
,
\iota_{3}
are the signs of the root of
A
and the roots of
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
in
A
, and
\eta
is the colour of the root of
A
.

In (
2.11
) and (
2.12
), we used as a shortcut the notations
c_{|_{A_{i}}}
(resp.
\varphi_{|_{A_{i}}}
) for
i=1,2,3
to denote
c_{|_{\mathcal{N}(A_{i})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{i})}}
(resp.
\varphi_{|_{\mathcal{N}(A_{i})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{i})}}
).

The following lemma will be useful in the proof of Proposition
2.2
and it simply follows from the dependence of
F_{(A,\varphi,c)}
on
\varphi
.

Lemma 2.1
.

If
(A,\varphi,c)
is a signed and coloured tree and if
\iota\in\{\pm\}
, then

\displaystyle F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}^{\iota}=F_{\left(A,\iota\varphi,c\right)}.

(2.13)

Proof.

We prove (
2.13
) directly for each tree
A
using definitions (
2.9
)-(
2.11
). If
A=\bot
and if we denote by
\iota^{\prime}
the single value of its sign map (and
\eta
the single value of its colour map) we use (
2.9
) and get

\displaystyle F_{\left(\bot,\iota^{\prime},c\right)}^{\iota}

\displaystyle=\left(X^{\eta,\iota^{\prime}}_{\rm in}\right)^{\iota}=X^{\eta,\iota\iota^{\prime}}_{\rm in}=F_{\left(\bot,\iota\iota^{\prime},c\right)}.

If
A=B_{\mathrm{st}}(A_{1},A_{2})
or
A=B_{\rm in}(\bot,\bot)
, (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) imply that
F_{(A,\varphi,c)}
is of the form

\displaystyle F_{(A,\varphi,c)}=Q^{\varphi(r_{A})}(A,\varphi(r_{A})\varphi,c),

for some quantity
Q
. Therefore, using
\iota^{2}=1
we obtain

\displaystyle F^{\iota}_{(A,\varphi,c)}=Q^{\iota\varphi(r_{A})}(A,\varphi(r_{A})\varphi,c)=Q^{\iota\varphi(r_{A})}(A,\iota\varphi(r_{A})\iota\varphi,c)=F_{(A,\iota\varphi,c)}.

Similarly, if
A=T_{*}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
, (
2.12
) implies that
F_{(A,\varphi,\eta)}
is of the form

\displaystyle F_{(A,\varphi,c)}=\varphi(r_{A})iQ^{\varphi(r_{A})}(A,\varphi(r_{A})\varphi,c),

for some quantity
Q
. Therefore, using again
\iota^{2}=1
and
\left(iz\right)^{\iota}=\iota iz^{\iota}
we obtain

\displaystyle F^{\iota}_{(A,\varphi,c)}=\iota\varphi(r_{A})iQ^{\iota\varphi(r_{A})}(A,\varphi(r_{A})\varphi,c)=\iota\varphi(r_{A})iQ^{\iota\varphi(r_{A})}(A,\iota\varphi(r_{A})\iota\varphi,c)=F_{(A,\iota\varphi,c)},

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The following key proposition gives a representation of each element of the sequence
(X^{\eta}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
from Definition
2.2
as a sum over signed and coloured trees.

Proposition 2.1
.

For all
\eta\in\{0,1\}
,
\iota\in\{\pm\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle X^{\eta,\iota}_{n}=\sum_{(A,\varphi,c)\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\eta,\iota}}F_{(A,\varphi,c)}.

(2.14)

Proof.

We first introduce the following notation: if
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
,
\iota\in\{\pm\}
and
\eta\in\{0,1\}
we denote by
\mathrm{SM}(A,\iota)
the set of sign maps defined on
A
such that the sign of the root of
A
is
\iota
. If
Q
is now any quantity depending on a tree
A
, a sign map
\varphi
and a colour map
c
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{(A,\varphi,c)\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\eta,\iota}}Q(A,\varphi,c)=\sum_{A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathrm{SM}(A,\iota)}Q(A,\varphi,c_{\eta}),

(2.15)

where
c_{\eta}
is the unique colour map on
A
such that
c(r_{A})=\eta
.

We now prove (
2.14
) by induction over
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For
n=0
, (
2.14
) is proved by using (
2.3
), (
2.6
) and (
2.9
). For
n=1
, we start by expanding the LHS using (
2.4
) and (
2.3
):

\displaystyle X^{\eta,\iota}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\sum_{\iota^{\prime}_{1},\iota^{\prime}_{2}\in\{\pm\}}\left(B^{\eta,\iota}_{\left(\iota_{1}^{\prime},\iota_{2}^{\prime}\right)}\left(t,X_{\rm in}^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{1}^{\prime}},X_{\rm in}^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{2}^{\prime}}\right)-B^{\eta,\iota}_{\left(\iota_{1}^{\prime},\iota_{2}^{\prime}\right)}\left(0,X_{\rm in}^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{1}^{\prime}},X_{\rm in}^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{2}^{\prime}}\right)\right)

We perform the change of variable
\iota_{i}=\iota\iota^{\prime}_{i}
for
i=1,2
and use (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) to get

\displaystyle X^{\eta,\iota}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\sum_{\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}}\left(B^{\eta,\iota}_{\iota\left(\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\right)}\left(t,F_{(\bot,\iota\iota_{1},\bar{\eta})}(t),F_{(\bot,\iota\iota_{2},\bar{\eta})}(t)\right)-B^{\eta,\iota}_{\iota\left(\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\right)}\left(0,X_{\rm in}^{\overline{\eta},\iota\iota_{1}},X_{\rm in}^{\overline{\eta},\iota\iota_{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathrm{SM}(B_{\mathrm{st}}(\bot,\bot),\iota)}F_{\left(B_{\mathrm{st}}(\bot,\bot),\varphi,c_{\eta}\right)}(t)+\sum_{\varphi\in\mathrm{SM}(B_{\rm in}(\bot,\bot),\iota)}F_{\left(B_{\rm in}(\bot,\bot),\varphi,c_{\eta}\right)}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(A,\varphi,c)\in\mathcal{A}^{\eta,\iota}_{1}}F_{(A,\varphi,c)}(t),

where we used the definition (
2.7
) of
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and (
2.15
). This concludes the proof of (
2.14
) in the case
n=1
. Now assume that (
2.14
) holds for all
k\in\llbracket 0,n+1\rrbracket
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thanks to (
2.5
) we have

\displaystyle X_{n+2}^{\eta,\iota}(t)

\displaystyle=-\iota i\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n\\
\iota^{\prime}_{1},\iota^{\prime}_{2},\iota^{\prime}_{3}\in\{\pm\}\\
