*\end{subarray}}C^{\eta,\iota}_{*,(\iota^{\prime}_{1},\iota^{\prime}_{2},\iota^{\prime}_{3})}\left(\tau,X_{n_{1}}^{\eta,\iota^{\prime}_{1}}(\tau),X_{n_{2}}^{\overline{\eta},\iota^{\prime}_{2}}(\tau),X_{n_{3}}^{\eta,\iota^{\prime}_{3}}(\tau)\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n+1\\
\iota^{\prime}_{1},\iota^{\prime}_{2}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}B_{\left(\iota^{\prime}_{1},\iota^{\prime}_{2}\right)}^{\eta,\iota}\left(t,X_{n_{1}}^{\bar{\eta},\iota^{\prime}_{1}}(t),X_{n_{2}}^{\bar{\eta},\iota^{\prime}_{2}}(t)\right).

Since in both sums each
n_{i}
satisfies
n_{i}\leq n+1
we can use our induction assumption and use (
2.14
), (
2.15
) and the change of variable
\iota_{i}=\iota\iota^{\prime}_{i}
in both terms to obtain

\displaystyle X_{n+2}^{\eta,\iota}(t)

\displaystyle=-\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n\\
A_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\\
A_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}}\\
A_{3}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{3}}\\
*\end{subarray}}\iota i\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3}\in\{\pm\}\\
\varphi_{1}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{1},\iota\iota_{1})\\
\varphi_{2}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{2},\iota\iota_{2})\\
\varphi_{3}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{3},\iota\iota_{3})\end{subarray}}C^{\eta,\iota}_{*,\iota(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}\left(\tau,F_{\left(A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{\eta}\right)}(t),F_{\left(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{\bar{\eta}}\right)}(t),F_{\left(A_{3},\varphi_{3},c_{\eta}\right)}(t)\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n+1\\
A_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\\
A_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}\\
\varphi_{1}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{1},\iota\iota_{1})\\
\varphi_{2}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{2},\iota\iota_{2})\end{subarray}}B_{\iota\left(\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\right)}^{\eta,\iota}\left(t,F_{\left(A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{\bar{\eta}}\right)}(t),F_{\left(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{\bar{\eta}}\right)}(t)\right).

We now perform a change of variables in the sums over sign maps, by setting
\varphi^{\prime}_{i}=\iota\varphi_{i}
in both terms. We get

\displaystyle X_{n+2}^{\eta,\iota}(t)

\displaystyle=-\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n\\
A_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\\
A_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}}\\
A_{3}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{3}}\\
*\end{subarray}}\iota i\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\hskip-2.84544pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3}\in\{\pm\}\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{1},\iota_{1})\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{2},\iota_{2})\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{3}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{3},\iota_{3})\end{subarray}}C^{\eta,\iota}_{*,\iota(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}\left(\tau,F_{\left(A_{1},\iota\varphi_{1},c_{\eta}\right)}(t),F_{\left(A_{2},\iota\varphi_{2},c_{\bar{\eta}}\right)}(t),F_{\left(A_{3},\iota\varphi_{3},c_{\eta}\right)}(t)\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n+1\\
A_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\\
A_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{1},\iota_{1})\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{2},\iota_{2})\end{subarray}}B_{\iota\left(\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\right)}^{\eta,\iota}\left(t,F_{\left(A_{1},\iota\varphi_{1},c_{\bar{\eta}}\right)}(t),F_{\left(A_{2},\iota\varphi_{2},c_{\bar{\eta}}\right)}(t)\right).

We now use the following facts:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{1},\iota_{1})\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{2},\iota_{2})\end{subarray}}P\left(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\varphi^{\prime}_{1},\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in\mathrm{SM}\left(B_{\mathrm{st}}(A_{1},A_{2}),\iota\right)\end{subarray}}P\left(\varphi\left(\text{root of $A_{1}$}\right),\varphi\left(\text{root of $A_{2}$}\right),\varphi_{|_{A_{1}}},\varphi_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)

(2.16)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3}\in\{\pm\}\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{1},\iota_{1})\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{2},\iota_{2})\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{3}\in\mathrm{SM}(A_{3},\iota_{3})\end{subarray}}P\left(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3},\varphi^{\prime}_{1},\varphi^{\prime}_{2},\varphi^{\prime}_{3}\right)

(2.17)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in\mathrm{SM}\left(T_{*}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}),\iota\right)\end{subarray}}P\left(\varphi\left(\text{root of $A_{1}$}\right),\varphi\left(\text{root of $A_{2}$}\right),\varphi\left(\text{root of $A_{3}$}\right),\varphi_{|_{A_{1}}},\varphi_{|_{A_{2}}},\varphi_{|_{A_{3}}}\right),

where
P
denotes any quantity depending on the signs and the sign maps. We thus obtain, recalling also the definitions (
2.12
) and (
2.11
) and the recursive rules defining a colour map depending on the nature of a node:

\displaystyle X_{n+2}^{\eta,\iota}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n\\
A_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\\
A_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}}\\
A_{3}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{3}}\\
*\end{subarray}}\hskip 11.38092pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in\mathrm{SM}\left(T_{*}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}),\iota\right)\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.69046ptF_{\left(T_{*}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}),\varphi,c_{\eta}\right)}(t)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n+1\\
A_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\\
A_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\hskip 11.38092pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in\mathrm{SM}\left(B_{\mathrm{st}}(A_{1},A_{2}),\iota\right)\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.69046ptF_{\left(B_{\mathrm{st}}(A_{1},A_{2}),\varphi,c_{\eta}\right)}(t).

Using now the definition (
2.8
) of
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n+2}
and (
2.15
) we finally obtain

\displaystyle X^{\eta,\iota}_{n+2}(t)=\sum_{(A,\varphi,c)\in\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{\eta,\iota}}F_{(A,\varphi,c)}(t),

which proves that (
2.14
) holds for
n+2
. This concludes the induction and the proof of the proposition.
∎

2.5
Decoration of a tree

In order to express the Fourier transform
\widehat{X^{\eta,\iota}_{n}}
using (
2.14
), we need to associate wavenumbers to the leaves and nodes of a tree. We talk of decorations of a tree.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
be a tree.

•

A
decoration map
of
A
is a map

\displaystyle\kappa:\mathcal{L}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}

satisfying that for any node
j
of
A

\displaystyle\kappa(j)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)}\kappa(j^{\prime}).

(2.18)

•

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, we say that a decoration
\kappa
of
A
is a
k
-decoration of
A
if
\kappa(r_{A})=k
and
\kappa\left(\mathcal{L}(A)\right)\subset\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)\vcentcolon=\left\{\text{$k$-decoration of $A$}\right\}.

Thanks to (
2.18
), a decoration map
\kappa
of
A
is uniquely determined by its restriction
\kappa_{|_{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
to the leaves of
A
. More precisely, for a given tree, the map
\kappa\longmapsto\kappa_{|_{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
is a bijection.

Example 2.2
.

We consider the same tree as in Example
2.1
and decorate it to illustrate Definition
2.8
. We remove all signs from leaves and nodes for clarity.
high
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+k_{4}+k_{5}+k_{6}
in
k_{1}+k_{2}
🍂
k_{1}
🍂
k_{2}
st
k_{3}+k_{4}
🍂
k_{3}
🍂
k_{4}
st
k_{5}+k_{6}
🍂
k_{5}
🍂
k_{6}

Note that the blue wavenumbers are deduced from the black ones following the recursive rule (
2.18
).

In the following definition, we associate to each node a double or triple
\Delta
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
be a tree,
\varphi
a sign map on
A
and
\kappa
a decoration map on
A
.

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{st}}(A)
, we define

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}(A,\varphi,\kappa)\vcentcolon=\varphi(j)\langle\kappa(j)\rangle-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)}\varphi(j^{\prime})\langle\kappa(j^{\prime})\rangle.

(2.19)

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\rm in}(A)
, we define

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}(A,\varphi,\kappa)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=0,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}_{j}(A,\varphi,\kappa)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\varphi(j)\langle\kappa(j)\rangle-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)}\varphi(j^{\prime})\langle\kappa(j^{\prime})\rangle.

(2.21)

As it can be seen in the Definition
2.2
of the sequence
(X^{\eta}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
or in
2.7
of
F_{A}
, interactions associated to binary nodes are not directly integrated in time, while interactions associated to ternary nodes are. However, they are integrated in the sense that there might have a ternary node above them in the tree.
We associate to any binary node the closest ternary node above them to make this clear.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
and let
j\in\mathcal{N}(A)
.

•

If the set

\displaystyle\left\{j\leq j^{\prime}~\middle|~j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)\right\}

(2.22)

is empty we define
\mathtt{int}(j)=-1
.

•

Otherwise, the above set is totally ordered and we define

\displaystyle\mathtt{int}(j)=\min\left\{j\leq j^{\prime}~\middle|~j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)\right\}.

Remark 2.6
.

Definition
2.10
actually defines a map
\mathtt{int}:\mathcal{N}(A)\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)\sqcup\{-1\}
such that
\mathtt{int}_{|_{\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)}}
is the identity. For
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)\sqcup\{-1\}
, we will also use the notation (for instance in Section
3.2.2
)

\displaystyle\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j)\vcentcolon=\left\{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(A)~\middle|~\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})=j\right\}.

(2.23)

Note that this differs slightly from the usual preimage of a given
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)
, which is equal to
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j)\sqcup\{j\}
, where
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j)
is given by (
2.23
). This is due to the fact that we have chosen to define the map
\mathtt{int}
on
\mathcal{N}(A)
and not just on
\mathcal{N}_{B}(A)
.

With this notation we can define the moduli of resonance that will be integrated.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
be a tree,
\varphi
a sign map on
A
and
\kappa
a decoration map on
A
.

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)
, we set

\displaystyle\Omega_{j}(A,\varphi,\kappa)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\Delta_{j}(A,\varphi,\kappa)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(A)\\
\text{with }\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})=j\end{subarray}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}(A,\varphi,\kappa)

(2.24)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(A)\\
\text{with }\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})=j\end{subarray}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}(A,\varphi,\kappa).

•

We also define

\Omega_{-1}(A,\varphi,\kappa)\vcentcolon=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(A)\\
\text{with }\mathtt{int}(j)=-1\end{subarray}}\Delta_{j}(A,\varphi,\kappa).

(2.25)

Finally, we also need to associate nonlinear interactions between wavenumbers to every node of a tree, depending on their nature.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
be a tree,
\varphi
a sign map on
A
,
c
a colour map of
A
and
\kappa
a decoration map on
A
. We associate quadratic or cubic interations between wavenumbers to all nodes in the following way:

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{st}}(A)
we set

\displaystyle q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\vcentcolon=\varphi(j)\frac{q^{c(j)}(\kappa(\ell),\kappa(r))}{\Delta_{j}(A,\varphi,\kappa)},

(2.26)

where
\ell
and
r
are respectively the left and right child of
j
.

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\rm in}(A)
we set

\displaystyle q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\vcentcolon=-\varphi(j)\frac{q^{c(j)}(\kappa(\ell),\kappa(r))}{\Delta^{\prime}_{j}(A,\varphi,\kappa)},

(2.27)

where
\ell
and
r
are respectively the left and right child of
j
.

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{*}(A)
with
*\in\{{\mathrm{low-}\ell},{\mathrm{low-}m},{\mathrm{low-}r},\mathrm{high}\}
we set

\displaystyle q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\varphi(j)\chi_{j}(A,\kappa)q_{\varphi(j)\varphi(m)}^{c(j)}(\kappa(\ell),\kappa(m),\kappa(r)),

(2.28)

\displaystyle\chi_{j}(A,\kappa)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\chi_{*}(\kappa(\ell),\kappa(m),\kappa(r)),

(2.29)

where
\ell
,
m
and
r
are respectively the left, middle and right child of
j
.

Based on the assumptions we made on the nonlinearity of the original Klein-Gordon system (
KG
), we estimate each
q_{j}
in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
be a tree,
\varphi
a sign map on
A
,
c
a colour map of
A
and
\kappa
a decoration map on
A
. If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(A)
then

\displaystyle\left|q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\prod_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)}\left\langle\kappa(j^{\prime})\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)
then

\displaystyle\left|q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\chi_{j}(A,\kappa)\prod_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)}\left\langle\kappa(j^{\prime})\right\rangle^{-1}.

Proof.

Since
Q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})
is bounded by assumption, (
1.9
) implies that

\displaystyle|q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})|\lesssim\left\langle k_{1}\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle k_{2}\right\rangle^{-1}.

(2.30)

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(A)
and
\ell
and
r
denote its left and right children, then (
2.26
), (
2.27
), (
2.19
) and (
2.21
) imply that

\displaystyle\left|q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\frac{q^{\eta}(\kappa(\ell),\kappa(r))}{\Delta^{(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}_{\kappa(\ell),\kappa(r)}}\right|

for some
\eta
,
\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
depending on
j
. Using now (
2.30
) and (
1.10
) we obtain

\displaystyle\left|q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\langle\kappa(\ell)\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle\kappa(r)\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

If now
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)
and
\ell
,
m
and
r
denote its left, middle and right children, then (
1.14
), (
2.28
) and (
2.30
) imply that

\displaystyle\left|q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\chi_{j}(A,\kappa)\left\langle\kappa(\ell)\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle\kappa(m)\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle\kappa(r)\right\rangle^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big|m+2\left((\kappa(\ell)+\kappa(m)+\kappa(r))\cdot\kappa(m)-\varphi(j)\varphi(m)\left\langle\kappa(\ell)+\kappa(m)+\kappa(r)\right\rangle\left\langle\kappa(m)\right\rangle\right)\Big|^{-1}.

Note that applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
to
(\sqrt{m},a)
and
(\sqrt{m},b)
(for some
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
) gives
a\cdot b+m\leq\left\langle a\right\rangle\left\langle b\right\rangle
. In particular, this shows that

\displaystyle\Big|m+2\left((\kappa(\ell)+\kappa(m)+\kappa(r))\cdot\kappa(m)-\varphi(j)\varphi(m)\left\langle\kappa(\ell)+\kappa(m)+\kappa(r)\right\rangle\left\langle\kappa(m)\right\rangle\right)\Big|\geq m,

so that we obtain

\displaystyle\left|q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\chi_{j}(A,\kappa)\left\langle\kappa(\ell)\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle\kappa(m)\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle\kappa(r)\right\rangle^{-1}.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Example 2.3
.

q_{j}

2.1
. We arbitrarily label each node with blue integers, recall the signs of nodes and leaves but remove the wavenumbers for seek of readability.

k_{2},-
\mathrm{st}

\mathrm{st}

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}

\displaystyle=\langle k_{5}+k_{6}\rangle+\langle k_{5}\rangle-\langle k_{6}\rangle,

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}}

Example 2.4
.

To provide an example of how to compute
\Omega_{j}
, we consider the following tree. We remove from the graphical representation all the unnecessary information, in particular the nature of the binary or ternary nodes, and arbitrarily label each node by blue integers.
\bullet
6
\bullet
5
🍂
\bullet
4
\bullet
3
🍂
🍂
🍂
🍂
🍂
\bullet
2
🍂
\bullet
1
🍂
🍂
🍂

The nodes
5,3,1
are ternary nodes and the nodes
6,4,2
are standard binary nodes. We have

\displaystyle\mathtt{int}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6})=-1,\qquad\mathtt{int}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4})={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5},\qquad\mathtt{int}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2})=-1.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{-1}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6}}+\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}},\qquad\Omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5}}=\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5}}+\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4}},\qquad\Omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}}=\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}},\qquad\Omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}=\Delta_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}.

2.6
Fourier transform of
F_{(A,\varphi,c)}

We can now express the Fourier transform of each
F_{(A,\varphi,c)}
defined in Definition
2.7
.

Proposition 2.2
.

For all trees
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
,
\varphi
and
c
sign and colour maps on
A
, all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{(A,\varphi,c)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{n}}{L^{\frac{nd}{2}}}(-i)^{n_{T}(A)}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}e^{i\Omega_{-1}(A,\varphi,\kappa)t}

(2.31)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(A)}q_{j}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\int_{I_{A}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)}e^{i\Omega_{j}(A,\varphi,\kappa)t_{j}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A)}\hbox{\hskip 19.9307pt\hskip-19.9307pt\hbox{$X^{c(\ell),\varphi(\ell)}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-19.9307pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 19.9307pt}(\kappa(\ell))

where

\displaystyle I_{A}(t)\vcentcolon=\left\{(t_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)}\in[0,t]^{n_{T}(A)}~\middle|~j\leq j^{\prime}\Rightarrow t_{j}\leq t_{j^{\prime}}\right\}

and where we use the convention that the integral over
I_{A}(t)
equals 1 if
I_{A}(t)
is empty.

Proof.

We prove (
2.31
) by induction over the size
n\in\mathbb{N}
of
A
. If
n=0
, we only need to consider
A=\bot
and we can identify its sign and colour maps with their single values
\iota\in\{\pm\}
and
\eta\in\{0,1\}
. The definition (
2.9
) simply implies

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{(A,\iota,\eta)}}=\widehat{X^{\eta,\iota}_{\rm in}}

which matches the RHS of (
2.31
). Indeed, in this case there are no ternary nodes,
\Omega_{-1}(\bot)=0
and the only
k
-decoration is the map associating
k
to the single leaf.

If
n=1
there are two cases to consider according to (
2.7
). We assume first that
A=B_{\rm in}(\bot,\bot)
and denote by
\iota_{r}
,
\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
respectively the signs of the root
r
, left child
\ell_{1}
and right child
\ell_{2}
, and by
\eta
the colour of the root. On the one hand, (
2.10
) and (
2.1
) imply

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{(A,\varphi,c)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{\iota_{r}k_{1}+\iota_{r}k_{2}=k}\frac{q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})^{\iota_{r}}}{\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta}}_{\rm in}}(\iota_{r}\iota_{1}k_{1})^{\iota_{1}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta}}_{\rm in}}(\iota_{r}\iota_{2}k_{2})^{\iota_{2}}.

Performing the change of variable
k^{\prime}_{i}=\iota_{r}k_{i}
and using

\displaystyle q^{\eta}(\iota_{r}k^{\prime}_{1},\iota_{r}k^{\prime}_{2})^{\iota_{r}}

\displaystyle=q^{\eta}(k^{\prime}_{1},k^{\prime}_{2}),

(2.32)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota_{r}k^{\prime}_{1},\iota_{r}k^{\prime}_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{k^{\prime}_{1},k^{\prime}_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})},

(2.33)

the above identities being consequences of (
1.8
) and (
1.9
), we get

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{(A,\varphi,c)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k^{\prime}_{1}+k^{\prime}_{2}=k}\frac{q^{\eta}(k^{\prime}_{1},k^{\prime}_{2})}{\Delta_{k^{\prime}_{1},k^{\prime}_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{1}}_{\rm in}}(k^{\prime}_{1})\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{2}}_{\rm in}}(k^{\prime}_{2}).

On the other hand, for any quantity
P
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}_{1}+k^{\prime}_{2}=k}P(k^{\prime}_{1},k^{\prime}_{2})=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}P(\kappa(\ell_{1}),\kappa(\ell_{2}))

where
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
are the only two leaves of the tree. Together with
\Delta_{\kappa(\ell_{1}),\kappa(\ell_{2})}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}=\iota_{r}\Delta^{\prime}_{r}
,
c(r)=\eta
,
c(\ell_{1})=\overline{\eta}
,
c(\ell_{2})=\overline{\eta}
and (
2.27
), this implies that

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{(A,\varphi,c)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}q_{r}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)\widehat{X^{c(\ell_{1}),\varphi(\ell_{1})}_{\rm in}}(\kappa(\ell_{1}))\widehat{X^{c(\ell_{2}),\varphi(\ell_{2})}_{\rm in}}(\kappa(\ell_{2})),

which matches the RHS of (
2.31
) since
\Omega_{-1}(B_{\rm in}(\bot,\bot))=\Delta_{r}(B_{\rm in}(\bot,\bot))=0
(the root is an initial binary node, see (
2.20
)) and there are no ternary nodes.

If
A=B_{\mathrm{st}}(\bot,\bot)
, we use (
2.11
), (
2.9
) and (
2.1
) and obtain (with the same notations as for the first case above):

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}}(t,k)=\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\frac{q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})}{\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}e^{it\iota_{r}\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{1}}_{\rm in}}(k_{1})\widehat{X^{\overline{\eta},\iota_{2}}_{\rm in}}(k_{2}),

where we performed the same change of variable and used (
2.32
) and (
2.33
). The end of the proof is identical to the above case, using (
2.26
) instead of (
2.27
) and noting that
\Omega_{-1}(B_{\mathrm{st}}(\bot,\bot))=\Delta_{r}(B_{\mathrm{st}}(\bot,\bot))=\iota_{r}\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}
(see (
2.19
)).

Assume now that (
2.31
) holds for all
k\in\llbracket 0,n+1\rrbracket
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n+2}
. According to (
2.8
), there are two cases to consider. If
A=B_{\mathrm{st}}(A_{1},A_{2})
with
(A_{1},A_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}}
and
n_{1}+n_{2}=n+1
, we denote by
\iota_{r}
,
\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
the signs of the root of
A
, of the root of
A_{1}
and of the root of
A_{2}
in
A
, and also by
\eta
the colour of the root of
A
. Then (
2.11
), (
2.1
), the change of variable
k^{\prime}_{i}=\iota_{r}k_{i}
(with
k^{\prime}_{i}
being immediately relabeled
k_{i}
) and (
2.32
)-(
2.33
) imply

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\frac{q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})}{\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}e^{it\iota_{r}\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}\hbox{\hskip 34.79375pt\hskip-34.79375pt\hbox{$F^{\iota_{r}}_{\left(A_{1},\iota_{r}\varphi_{|_{A_{1}}},c_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)}$}\hskip-34.79375pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 34.79375pt}(t,k_{1})\hbox{\hskip 34.79375pt\hskip-34.79375pt\hbox{$F^{\iota_{r}}_{\left(A_{2},\iota_{r}\varphi_{|_{A_{2}}},c_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)}$}\hskip-34.79375pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 34.79375pt}(t,k_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\frac{q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})}{\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}e^{it\iota_{r}\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}\hbox{\hskip 31.29892pt\hskip-31.29892pt\hbox{$F_{\left(A_{1},\varphi_{|_{A_{1}}},c_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)}$}\hskip-31.29892pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 31.29892pt}(t,k_{1})\hbox{\hskip 31.29892pt\hskip-31.29892pt\hbox{$F_{\left(A_{2},\varphi_{|_{A_{2}}},c_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)}$}\hskip-31.29892pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 31.29892pt}(t,k_{2})

where we used the property (
2.13
). Since
n_{1},n_{2}\leq n+1
, we can use our induction assumption (
2.31
) on

F_{\left(A_{1},\varphi_{|_{A_{1}}},c_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)}

⋀

and

F_{\left(A_{2},\varphi_{|_{A_{2}}},c_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)}

⋀

. This gives

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right)^{1+n_{1}+n_{2}}(-i)^{n_{T}(A_{1})+n_{T}(A_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
\kappa_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{1}}(A_{1})\\
\kappa_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2})}{\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}e^{it\left(\iota_{r}\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}+\Omega_{-1}(A_{1},\kappa_{1})+\Omega_{-1}(A_{2},\kappa_{2})\right)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(A_{1})\\
j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}q_{j_{1}}(A_{1},\kappa_{1})q_{j_{2}}(A_{2},\kappa_{2})\int_{I_{A_{1}}(t)\times I_{A_{2}}(t)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{1})\\
j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{1}}(A_{1},\kappa_{1})t_{j_{1}}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{2}}(A_{2},\kappa_{2})t_{j_{2}}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\\
\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}\hbox{\hskip 23.83353pt\hskip-23.83353pt\hbox{$X^{c(\ell_{1}),\varphi(\ell_{1})}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-23.83353pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 23.83353pt}(\kappa_{1}(\ell_{1}))\hbox{\hskip 23.83353pt\hskip-23.83353pt\hbox{$X^{c(\ell_{2}),\varphi(\ell_{2})}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-23.83353pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 23.83353pt}(\kappa_{2}(\ell_{2})),

where in the various
\Omega_{-1}
,
\Omega_{j}
and
q_{j}
appearing in the formula, we only kept their dependence on the decoration maps, since the sign and colour maps are simply the restriction of
\varphi
and
c
to either
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
. Now, if we denote by
r
the root of
A
and by
r_{1},r_{2}
the roots of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
in
A
, a decoration
\kappa
on
A
satisfies
\kappa(r)=\kappa(r_{1})+\kappa(r_{2})
and
\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}}
and
\kappa_{|_{A_{2}}}
are decorations of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
. Conversely, if
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{1}}(A_{1})\times\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}}(A_{2})
then there exists a unique
\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}(A)
such that
\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}}=\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{|_{A_{2}}}=\kappa_{2}
. This shows that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
\kappa_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{1}}(A_{1})\\
\kappa_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}Q(k_{1},k_{2},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}Q\left(\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2}),\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}},\kappa_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)

which in turn implies, since
\mathcal{N}(A)=\mathcal{N}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{2})\sqcup\{r\}
and
\mathcal{L}(A)=\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})
, that

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}}(t,k)=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right)^{1+n_{1}+n_{2}}(-i)^{n_{T}(A_{1})+n_{T}(A_{2})}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}\frac{q^{\eta}(\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2}))}{\Delta_{\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2})}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}e^{it\left(\iota_{r}\Delta_{\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2})}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}+\Omega_{-1}\left(A_{1},\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)+\Omega_{-1}\left(A_{2},\kappa_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(A)\setminus\{r\}}q_{j}(A,\kappa)\int_{I_{A_{1}}(t)\times I_{A_{2}}(t)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{1})\\
j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{1}}(A_{1},\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}})t_{j_{1}}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{2}}(A_{2},\kappa_{|_{A_{2}}})t_{j_{2}}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A)}\hbox{\hskip 19.9307pt\hskip-19.9307pt\hbox{$X^{c(\ell),\varphi(\ell)}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-19.9307pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 19.9307pt}(\kappa(\ell)).

Thanks to (
2.26
) and (
2.19
) we have
\frac{q^{\eta}(\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2}))}{\Delta_{\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2})}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}}=q_{r}(A,\kappa)
and
\iota_{r}\Delta_{\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2})}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})}=\Delta_{r}(A,\kappa)
. Moreover, since the root of
A
is a binary node we have
\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)=\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{2})
and
I_{A_{1}}(t)\times I_{A_{2}}(t)=I_{A}(t)
. Thus we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right)^{1+n_{1}+n_{2}}(-i)^{n_{T}(A)}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}e^{it\left(\Delta_{r}(A,\kappa)+\Omega_{-1}\left(A_{1},\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)+\Omega_{-1}\left(A_{2},\kappa_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(A)}q_{j}(A,\kappa)\int_{I_{A}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)}e^{i\Omega_{j}(A,\kappa)t_{j}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A)}\hbox{\hskip 19.9307pt\hskip-19.9307pt\hbox{$X^{c(\ell),\varphi(\ell)}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-19.9307pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 19.9307pt}(\kappa(\ell)).

Using then
n_{1}+n_{2}=n+1
and
\Omega_{-1}(A,\kappa)=\Delta_{r}(A,\kappa)+\Omega_{-1}\left(A_{1},\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)+\Omega_{-1}\left(A_{2},\kappa_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)
concludes the proof of (
2.31
) for
A
.

Similarly, if
A=T_{*}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
with
(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{1}}\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{2}}\times\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{3}}
and
n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n
, we use (
2.12
), (
2.2
), (
2.13
) and our induction assumption applied to
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
to get

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right)^{n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}+2}(-i)^{n_{T}(A_{1})+n_{T}(A_{2})+n_{T}(A_{3})+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=k\\
\kappa_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{1}}(A_{1})\\
\kappa_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}}(A_{2})\\
\kappa_{3}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{3}}(A_{3})\end{subarray}}\iota_{r}\chi_{*}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})q_{\iota_{r}\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(A_{1})\\
j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}(A_{2})\\
j_{3}\in\mathcal{N}(A_{3})\end{subarray}}q_{j_{1}}(A_{1},\kappa_{1})q_{j_{2}}(A_{2},\kappa_{2})q_{j_{3}}(A_{3},\kappa_{3})

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{0}^{t}e^{i\tau\left(\iota_{r}\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}+\Omega_{-1}(A_{1},\kappa_{1})+\Omega_{-1}(A_{2},\kappa_{2})+\Omega_{-1}(A_{3},\kappa_{3})\right)}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\left(\int_{I_{A_{1}}(\tau)\times I_{A_{2}}(\tau)\times I_{A_{3}}(\tau)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{1})\\
j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{2})\\
j_{3}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{3})\end{subarray}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{1}}(A_{1},\kappa_{1})t_{j_{1}}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{2}}(A_{2},\kappa_{2})t_{j_{2}}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{3}}(A_{3},\kappa_{3})t_{j_{3}}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{2}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{3}}\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\\
\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\\
\ell_{3}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{3})\end{subarray}}\widehat{X^{c(\ell_{1}),\varphi(\ell_{1})}_{\rm in}}(\kappa_{1}(\ell_{1}))\widehat{X^{c(\ell_{2}),\varphi(\ell_{2})}_{\rm in}}(\kappa_{2}(\ell_{2}))\widehat{X^{c(\ell_{3}),\varphi(\ell_{3})}_{\rm in}}(\kappa_{3}(\ell_{3})),

where we also used

\displaystyle\chi_{*}(\iota_{r}k_{1},\iota_{r}k_{2},\iota_{r}k_{3})

\displaystyle=\chi_{*}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),

\displaystyle q_{\iota_{r}\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(\iota_{r}k_{1},\iota_{r}k_{2},\iota_{r}k_{3})

\displaystyle=q_{\iota_{r}\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota_{r}k_{1},\iota_{r}k_{2},\iota_{r}k_{3}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})},

which follow from Definition
2.1
, (
1.13
) and (
1.14
). Now, as in the case of
B_{\mathrm{st}}(A_{1},A_{2})
above, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=k\\
\kappa_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{1}}(A_{1})\\
\kappa_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}}(A_{2})\\
\kappa_{3}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{3}}(A_{3})\end{subarray}}Q(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3})=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}Q\left(\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2}),\kappa(r_{3}),\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}},\kappa_{|_{A_{2}}},\kappa_{|_{A_{3}}}\right)

where
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
and
r_{3}
are the roots of
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
in
A
. This implies, also using

\displaystyle n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}+2

\displaystyle=n+2,

\displaystyle n_{T}(A_{1})+n_{T}(A_{2})+n_{T}(A_{3})+1

\displaystyle=n_{T}(A),

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(A)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{2})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{3})\sqcup\{r\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(A)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{3}),

that

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right)^{n+2}(-i)^{n_{T}(A)}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(A)}q_{j}(A,\kappa)

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{0}^{t}e^{i\Omega_{r}(A,\kappa)\tau}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\hskip-5.69046pt\int_{I_{A_{1}}(\tau)\times I_{A_{2}}(\tau)\times I_{A_{3}}(\tau)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{1})\\
j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{2})\\
j_{3}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{3})\end{subarray}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{1}}\left(A_{1},\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)t_{j_{1}}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{2}}\left(A_{2},\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)t_{j_{2}}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{3}}\left(A_{3},\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)t_{j_{3}}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{2}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{3}}\hskip-5.69046pt\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A)}\widehat{X^{c(\ell),\varphi(\ell)}_{\rm in}}(\kappa(\ell)),

Note that we used the following facts

\displaystyle\iota_{r}\Delta_{\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2}),\kappa(r_{3})}^{\iota_{r}(\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\iota_{3})}+\Omega_{-1}\left(A_{1},\kappa_{|_{A_{1}}}\right)+\Omega_{-1}\left(A_{2},\kappa_{|_{A_{2}}}\right)+\Omega_{-1}\left(A_{3},\kappa_{|_{A_{3}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Omega_{r}(A,\kappa),

\displaystyle\iota_{r}\chi_{*}(\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2}),\kappa(r_{3}))q_{\iota_{r}\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(\kappa(r_{1}),\kappa(r_{2}),\kappa(r_{3}))

\displaystyle=q_{r}(A,\kappa),

which follows from (
2.19
), (
2.24
) and (
2.28
). Now, since
\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)=\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{2})\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{3})\sqcup\{r\}
and
j<r
for all
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{2})\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{3})
we have

\displaystyle I_{A}(t)=\left\{(t_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)}\in[0,t]^{n_{T}(A)}~\middle|~t_{r}\in[0,t],\;\forall i=1,2,3,(t_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{i})}\in I_{A_{i}}(t_{r})\right\}.

This implies, after having identified the variable
\tau
with
t_{r}
, that

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{\left(A,\varphi,c\right)}}(t,k)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right)^{n+2}(-i)^{n_{T}(A)}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(A)}q_{j}(A,\kappa)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\int_{I_{A}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)}e^{i\Omega_{j}(A,\kappa)t_{j}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A)}\widehat{X^{c(\ell),\varphi(\ell)}_{\rm in}}(\kappa(\ell)).

Since
\Omega_{-1}(A,\kappa)=0
(because the root is a ternary node), this shows that (
2.31
) holds for any
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n+2}
, thus concluding our induction and the proof of the proposition.
∎

Example 2.5
.

A_{1}
of Example

, we have

\displaystyle I_{A_{1}}(t)=\{(t_{j})_{j\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1,3,5}\}}\;|\;t_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}\in[0,t]\,\wedge\,0\leq t_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}}\leq t_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5}}\leq t\}.

Remark 2.7
.

We make two important comments on Proposition
2.2
:

(i)

Thanks to our assumption (
1.2
), if
n\geq 1
then for any
\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{0}(A)
,
q_{r}(A,\varphi,c,\kappa)=0
and

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{(A,\varphi,c)}}(t,0)=0.

(2.34)

(ii)

After a change of variables, we get

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{(A,\varphi,c)}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)

(2.35)

\displaystyle\;=\frac{\varepsilon^{n_{B}(A)}}{L^{\frac{nd}{2}}}(-i)^{n_{T}(A)}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A)}e^{i\varepsilon^{-2}\Omega_{-1}t}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(A)}q_{j}\int_{I_{A}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A)}e^{i\Omega_{j}\varepsilon^{-2}t_{j}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A)}\widehat{X^{c(\ell),\varphi(\ell)}_{\rm in}}(\kappa(\ell)),

where we completely dropped the dependence on
A
,
\varphi
,
c
and
\kappa
of the
\Omega_{-1}
,
q_{j}
and
\Omega_{j}
for brevity, and where we used
n=n_{B}(A)+2n_{T}(A)
.

2.7
Coupling between trees

In order to study the evolution of the correlations, we need to estimate quantities of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{F_{(A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1})}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k_{1})\widehat{F_{(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2})}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k_{2})\right).

According to (
2.31
), this is will involve the following expectation

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\\
\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}\widehat{X^{c_{1}(\ell),\varphi_{1}(\ell)}_{\rm in}}(\kappa_{1}(\ell_{1}))\widehat{X^{c_{2}(\ell),\varphi_{2}(\ell)}_{\rm in}}(\kappa_{2}(\ell_{2}))\right).

Since
\widehat{X^{\eta}_{\rm in}}(k)=\mu^{\eta}_{k}
,
Wick’s formula implies that the above expectation vanishes unless the leaves in
\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\cup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})
are paired in a particular fashion.
To better explain this phenomenon and its consequences, we introduce below the notion of coupling.

Definition 2.13

(Coupling)
.

Let
(A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1})
and
(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2})
be two signed and coloured trees and consider a map
\sigma:\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})
.

•

We say that the triplet
C=\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)
is a coupling if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

The cardinal of

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}\vcentcolon=\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A_{1})~\middle|~\varphi_{1}(\ell)=-\right\}\sqcup\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A_{2})~\middle|~\varphi_{2}(\ell)=-\right\},

(2.36)

is equal to the cardinal of

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}\vcentcolon=\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A_{1})~\middle|~\varphi_{1}(\ell)=+\right\}\sqcup\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A_{2})~\middle|~\varphi_{2}(\ell)=+\right\}.

(2.37)

(ii)

The map
\sigma
is an involution and a bijection from
\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}
to
\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
.

•

For
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\{0,1\}
and
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)\text{ coupling}~\middle|~(A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1})\in\mathcal{A}^{\eta_{1},\iota_{1}}_{n_{1}},\;(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2})\in\mathcal{A}^{\eta_{2},\iota_{2}}_{n_{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\{0,1\}\\
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}^{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}.

•

If
C=\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)
is a coupling, then we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{X}(C)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}_{X}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{X}(A_{2}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(C)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(C)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{2}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(C)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{R}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{R}(A_{2})

for
X\in\{{\rm in},\mathrm{st},B,\mathrm{high},{\mathrm{low-}\ell},{\mathrm{low-}m},{\mathrm{low-}r},\mathrm{low},T\}
and
n_{X}(C)
,
n_{\ell}(C)
the respective cardinals of
\mathcal{N}_{X}(C)
,
\mathcal{L}(C)
. Moreover, if
C\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
then we define

\displaystyle n(C)\vcentcolon=n_{1}+n_{2}.

Example 2.6
.

Set
A=B_{\mathrm{st}}(T_{\mathrm{high}}(\bot,\bot,\bot),\bot)
with the colour of the root
0
and the following sign map

\displaystyle\varphi(r)

\displaystyle=-,\quad\varphi(rg)=-,\quad\varphi(rd)=+,\quad\varphi(rgg)=-,\quad\varphi(rgm)=+,\quad\varphi(rgd)=-,

where
r
is the root,
rg
is the left child of the root,
rd
is the right child of the root,
rgg
is the left child of
rg
,
rgm
is the middle child of
rg
and
rgd
is the right child of
rg
. Set
A^{\prime}=B_{\mathrm{st}}(\bot,\bot)
with the colour of the root
1
and the following sign map

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(r^{\prime})=+,\quad\varphi^{\prime}(r^{\prime}g^{\prime})=-,\quad\varphi^{\prime}(r^{\prime}d^{\prime})=+,

where
r^{\prime}
is the root of
A^{\prime}
,
r^{\prime}g^{\prime}
is its left child and
r^{\prime}d^{\prime}
its right child. Define
\sigma
by

\displaystyle\sigma(rgg)=rd,\quad\sigma(rgd)=r^{\prime}d^{\prime},\quad\sigma(rg^{\prime})=rgm.

The triplet
C=\big((A,\varphi,c_{0}),(A^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},c_{1}),\sigma\big)
forms a coupling, which we represent graphically below. The minus leaves are associated to the plus leaves by means of the same symbol. The signs of the branching nodes are omitted for readibility.

\mathrm{st}
high
🍀
-
✿
+
❃
-
🍀
+
\mathrm{st}
✿
-
❃
+

We introduce the following definition of decoration of a coupling, i.e a decoration of two trees compatible with the coupling map
\sigma
and Wick’s formula.

Definition 2.14

(Decoration of a coupling)
.

Let
C=\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)
be a coupling.

•

A decoration map of
C
is a map

\displaystyle\kappa:\mathcal{L}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{N}(C)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}

such that
\kappa_{|_{\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{1})}}
and
\kappa_{|_{\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{2})}}
are decoration maps on
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
in the sense of Definition
2.8
and such that

\displaystyle\forall\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C),\;\kappa(\ell)+\kappa(\sigma(\ell))=0.

(2.38)

•

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, we say that a decoration
\kappa
of
C
is a
k
-decoration of
C
if
\kappa(r_{A_{1}})=k
(where
r_{A_{1}}
is the root of
A_{1}
) and
\kappa\left(\mathcal{L}(C)\right)\subset\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\vcentcolon=\left\{\text{$k$-decoration of $C$}\right\}.

•

If
\kappa
is a decoration map of
C
and if we denote by
\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{|_{\mathcal{L}(A_{i})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{i})}}
for
i=1,2
, then we define

\displaystyle q_{j}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=q_{j}(A_{i},\varphi,c_{i},\kappa_{i}),\qquad\text{for $j\in\mathcal{N}(A_{i})$},

\displaystyle\Omega_{j}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\Omega_{j}(A_{i},\varphi,\kappa_{i}),\qquad\text{for $j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{i})$},

\displaystyle\Omega_{-1}(C)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\Omega_{-1}(A_{1},\varphi_{1},\kappa_{1})+\Omega_{-1}(A_{2},\varphi_{2},\kappa_{2}).

Remark 2.8
.

We make two comments to illustrate the previous definition.

(i)

A decoration map
\kappa
of a coupling
C=\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)
is uniquely determined by its restriction
\kappa_{|_{\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}}}
to the negative (or the positive) leaves of
C
. Indeed, if the image of the negative leaves is given, (
2.38
) and the fact that
\sigma:\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
is a bijection allow us to define uniquely
\kappa
on
\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})
. Since
\kappa_{|_{\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{1})}}
and
\kappa_{|_{\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{2})}}
are asked to be decoration maps of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, (
2.18
) then completely defines
\kappa
.

(ii)

If
\kappa
is a decoration map of a coupling
C=\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)
, then
\kappa(r_{A_{1}})+\kappa(r_{A_{2}})=0
, where
r_{A_{1}}
and
r_{A_{2}}
are the roots of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
. In particular, if
\kappa
is a
k
-decoration of
C
then
\kappa(r_{A_{2}})=-k
.

Example 2.7
.

Here is an example of decoration map for the coupling
C
of Example
2.6
:
\mathrm{st}
k_{1}-k_{3}
*
k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}
🍀
-,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-k_{2}}
✿
+,k_{1}
❃
-,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-k_{3}}
🍀
+,k_{2}
\mathrm{st}
k_{3}-k_{1}
✿
-,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-k_{1}}
❃
+,k_{3}

The following proposition makes fully explicit the role played by couplings in the computation of correlations, as introduced at the beginning of this section.

Proposition 2.3
.

For all
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\{0,1\}
,
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\}
,
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{X^{\eta_{1},\iota_{1}}_{n_{1}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\widehat{X^{\eta_{2},\iota_{2}}_{n_{2}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,-k)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}}\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k),

(2.39)

where
F_{C}
is defined in Fourier space by

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}}{L^{\frac{n(C)d}{2}}}(-i)^{n_{T}(C)}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)}e^{i\varepsilon^{-2}\Omega_{-1}(C)t}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)}q_{j}(C)

(2.40)

\displaystyle\times\int_{I_{C}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}e^{i\Omega_{j}\varepsilon^{-2}t_{j}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}}M^{c(\ell),c(\sigma(\ell))}(\varphi(\ell)\kappa(\ell))^{\varphi(\ell)}.

where if
C=\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)
is a coupling then

\displaystyle I_{C}(t)\vcentcolon=I_{A_{1}}(t)\times I_{A_{2}}(t)

and where we use the convention that the integral over
I_{C}(t)
equals 1 if
I_{C}(t)
is empty.

Proof.

We first use (
2.14
) to go from the sequence
X^{\eta,\iota}_{n}
to trees:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{X^{\eta_{1},\iota_{1}}_{n_{1}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\widehat{X^{\eta_{2},\iota_{2}}_{n_{2}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,-k)\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{n_{1}}^{\eta_{1},\iota_{1}}\\
(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{n_{2}}^{\eta_{2},\iota_{2}}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{F_{\left(A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}\right)}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\widehat{F_{\left(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}\right)}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,-k)\right).

Then we use (
2.35
) to express the Fourier transforms on the RHS:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{X^{\eta_{1},\iota_{1}}_{n_{1}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\widehat{X^{\eta_{2},\iota_{2}}_{n_{2}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,-k)\right)

(2.41)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{n_{1}}^{\eta_{1},\iota_{1}}\\
(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{n_{2}}^{\eta_{2},\iota_{2}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\varepsilon^{n_{B}(A_{1})+n_{B}(A_{2})}}{L^{\frac{(n_{1}+n_{2})d}{2}}}(-i)^{n_{T}(A_{1})+n_{T}(A_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A_{1})\\
\kappa_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{-k}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}e^{i\varepsilon^{-2}\left(\Omega_{-1}(A_{1})+\Omega_{-1}(A_{2})\right)t}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(A_{1})\\
j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}q_{j_{1}}(A_{1})q_{j_{2}}(A_{2})\int_{I_{A_{1}}(t)\times I_{A_{2}}(t)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{1})\\
j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{1}}(A_{1})\varepsilon^{-2}t_{j_{1}}}e^{i\Omega_{j_{2}}(A_{2})\varepsilon^{-2}t_{j_{2}}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{j_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\\
\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}\hbox{\hskip 27.73636pt\hskip-27.73636pt\hbox{$X^{c_{1}(\ell_{1}),\varphi_{1}(\ell_{1})}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-27.73636pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 27.73636pt}(\kappa_{1}(\ell_{1}))\hbox{\hskip 27.73636pt\hskip-27.73636pt\hbox{$X^{c_{2}(\ell_{2}),\varphi_{2}(\ell_{2})}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-27.73636pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 27.73636pt}(\kappa_{2}(\ell_{2}))\right),

where the various
\Omega_{-1}(A_{i})
,
q_{j}(A_{i})
and
\Omega_{j}(A_{i})
stand for
\Omega_{-1}(A_{i},\varphi_{i},\kappa_{i})
,
q_{j}(A_{i},\varphi_{i},c_{i},\kappa_{i})
and
\Omega_{j}(A_{i},\varphi_{i},\kappa_{i})
for
i=1,2
. In order to unify the notations, we define maps
\varphi
,
c
,
\kappa
from
\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{2})
to
\{\pm\}
,
\{0,1\}
,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
respectively so that their restriction to
\mathcal{L}(A_{i})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(A_{i})
are given by
\varphi_{i}
,
c_{i}
,
\kappa_{i}
respectively, for
i=1,2
. This allows us to write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\\
\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}\hbox{\hskip 27.73636pt\hskip-27.73636pt\hbox{$X^{c_{1}(\ell_{1}),\varphi_{1}(\ell_{1})}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-27.73636pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 27.73636pt}(\kappa_{1}(\ell_{1}))\hbox{\hskip 27.73636pt\hskip-27.73636pt\hbox{$X^{c_{2}(\ell_{2}),\varphi_{2}(\ell_{2})}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-27.73636pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 27.73636pt}(\kappa_{2}(\ell_{2}))\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})}\widehat{X^{c(\ell),\varphi(\ell)}_{\rm in}}(\kappa(\ell)) \right).

Now Wick’s formula implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})}\widehat{X^{c(\ell),\varphi(\ell)}_{\rm in}}(\kappa(\ell)) \right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\text{ pairing of}\\
\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell\in S_{\sigma}^{-}}\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{X^{c(\ell),\varphi(\ell)}_{\rm in}}(\kappa(\ell))\hbox{\hskip 30.81467pt\hskip-30.81467pt\hbox{$X^{c(\sigma(\ell)),\varphi(\sigma(\ell))}_{\rm in}$}\hskip-30.81467pt\hskip-4.16667pt\raisebox{19.87904pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt}}}}$}}\hskip-4.16667pt\hskip 30.81467pt}(\kappa(\sigma(\ell)))\right)

(2.42)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\text{ pairing of}\\
\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell\in S_{\sigma}^{-}}\delta_{\varphi(\ell)+\varphi(\sigma(\ell))}\delta_{\kappa(\ell)+\kappa(\sigma(\ell))}M^{c(\ell),c(\sigma(\ell))}(\varphi(\ell)\kappa(\ell))^{\varphi(\ell)},

where a pairing of a finite set is an involution without fixed point, where
S_{\sigma}^{-}
is a subset of
\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})
such that
S_{\sigma}^{-}\sqcup\sigma\left(S_{\sigma}^{-}\right)=\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})
and where we also used our assumptions on the initial data. This shows that in order to contribute, a pairing
\sigma
of
\mathcal{L}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{2})
has to be such that

\displaystyle\varphi(\ell)+\varphi(\sigma(\ell))=0\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa(\ell)+\kappa(\sigma(\ell))=0

(2.43)

for all
\ell\in S_{\sigma}^{-}
. Together with the fact that each pairing is an involution, the first condition in (
2.43
) implies that
\sigma\left(\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}\right)=\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
, where
\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}
and
\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
are defined in (
2.36
)-(
2.37
). This shows that we are allowed to consider
S_{\sigma}^{-}=\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}
and that
\sigma:\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
is a bijection. In particular,
C=\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)
is a coupling as defined in Definition
2.13
. Therefore, after having switched the sum over the coupling maps
\sigma
and the decoration map
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{X^{\eta_{1},\iota_{1}}_{n_{1}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\widehat{X^{\eta_{2},\iota_{2}}_{n_{2}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,-k)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{C=\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}}\frac{\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}}{L^{\frac{(n_{1}+n_{2})d}{2}}}(-i)^{n_{T}(C)}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\quad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A_{1})\\
\kappa_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{-k}(A_{2})\\
\text{$\kappa(\ell)+\kappa(\sigma(\ell))=0$, $\forall\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}$}\end{subarray}}e^{i\varepsilon^{-2}\Omega_{-1}(C)t}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)}q_{j}(C)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\quad\times\int_{I_{C}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}e^{i\Omega_{j}\varepsilon^{-2}t_{j}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}}M^{c(\ell),c(\sigma(\ell))}(\varphi(\ell)\kappa(\ell))^{\varphi(\ell)},

where
\varphi
,
c
and
\kappa
are defined as above based on
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
. Finally, note that Definition
2.14
implies that there is a bijection between
\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)
and the set

\displaystyle\left\{(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(A_{1})\times\mathcal{D}_{-k}(A_{2})~\middle|~\forall\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{-},\;\kappa(\ell)+\kappa(\sigma(\ell))=0\right\}.

Therefore we finally obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{X^{\eta_{1},\iota_{1}}_{n_{1}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\widehat{X^{\eta_{2},\iota_{2}}_{n_{2}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,-k)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sum_{C=\big((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1}),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma\big)\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}}\frac{\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}}{L^{\frac{(n_{1}+n_{2})d}{2}}}(-i)^{n_{T}(C)}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad\times\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)}e^{i\varepsilon^{-2}\Omega_{-1}(C)t}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)}q_{j}(C)\int_{I_{C}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}e^{i\Omega_{j}\varepsilon^{-2}t_{j}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}}M^{c(\ell),c(\sigma(\ell))}(\varphi(\ell)\kappa(\ell))^{\varphi(\ell)},

which concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

We make two comments on Proposition
2.3
:

(i)

As for the first comment in Remark
2.7
, the assumption (
1.2
) implies that

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,0)

\displaystyle=0.

(2.44)

(ii)

The result of Proposition
2.3
will be particularly useful to prove the convergence of the series
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X^{\eta}_{n}
over a large probability set. Indeed, (
2.39
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\left|\widehat{X^{\eta}_{n}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta,\eta,+,-}_{n,n}}\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k),

(2.45)

so that estimating
\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
will allow us to estimate
\widehat{X^{\eta}_{n}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
.

2.8
Bushes

In this section we introduce the notion of prebush and bush associated to particular nodes of trees and couplings. The definitions below only depend on the nature of nodes themselves, not on their signs/colours, nor on the involution
\sigma
in the case of a coupling. They will be mainly useful in the case of couplings.

Definition
2.15
below allows us to distinguish between the children of low ternary nodes that participate to the single low frequency interaction.

Definition 2.15

(Marked and unmarked)
.

Let
C
be a coupling or a tree.

•

We say that
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
is
marked
if one of the following conditions hold:

(i)

j
is the middle or right child of a
{\mathrm{low-}\ell}
node,

(ii)

j
is the left or right child of a
{\mathrm{low-}m}
node,

(iii)

j
is the left or middle child of a
{\mathrm{low-}r}
node,

We say that
j
is
unmarked
if it is not marked.

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
, we define

\displaystyle\mathtt{offspring}(j)\vcentcolon=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)\\
\text{$j^{\prime}$ marked }\end{subarray}}\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)~\middle|~\ell\leq j^{\prime}\right\}.

(2.46)

It follows from Definition
2.15
that the children of a binary node or a high ternary node are unmarked and that all branching nodes have at least one unmarked child. Moreover, the offspring of a low ternary node are precisely the leaves ultimately contributing to the low-frequency interaction of this node.

Definition 2.16

(Prebush)
.

Let
C
be a coupling or a tree. We define the
prebush
of any
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
recursively:

•

If
\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)
, then

\displaystyle\mathtt{prebush}(\ell)\vcentcolon=\{\ell\}.

(2.47)

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)
, then

\displaystyle\mathtt{prebush}(j)\vcentcolon=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)\\
\text{$j^{\prime}$ unmarked }\end{subarray}}\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime}).

(2.48)

The following lemma contains basic properties of prebushes.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
C
be a coupling or a tree.

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
, then

\displaystyle\mathtt{prebush}(j)=\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)~\middle|~\text{$\ell\leq j$ and there exists no marked node or leaf $j^{\prime}$ such that $\ell\leq j^{\prime}<j$}\right\}.

(2.49)

In particular,
\mathtt{prebush}(j)\neq\emptyset
.

•

If
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
are both marked, then

\displaystyle j\neq j^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\mathtt{prebush}(j)\cap\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime})=\emptyset.

(2.50)

Proof.

We start by the first part of the lemma.
Let
\ell
be an element of the set on the RHS of (
2.49
). By definition,
\ell
is unmarked and there exists a family
(j_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}
of unmarked nodes such that
\ell\in\mathtt{children}(j_{1})
,
j_{i}\in\mathtt{children}(j_{i+1})
and
j_{n}\in\mathtt{children}(j)
. Thanks to (
2.47
) and (
2.48
), this implies that

\displaystyle\ell\in\mathtt{prebush}(j_{1})\subset\cdots\subset\mathtt{prebush}(j_{n})\subset\mathtt{prebush}(j),

which shows the inclusion from right to left in (
2.49
).

The inclusion from left to right is proved by induction. If
j\in\mathcal{L}(C)
, (
2.49
) is a rewriting of (
2.47
). Now, let
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)
and assume that the inclusion from left to right in (
2.49
) holds for all
j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)
, and let us prove that it also holds for
j
. Let
\ell\in\mathtt{prebush}(j)
, thanks to (
2.48
) there exists
j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)
such that
j^{\prime}
is unmarked and
\ell\in\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime})
. Therefore, our induction assumption implies that
(a)

\ell\leq j^{\prime}
and that
(b
) there is no marked node or leaf
j^{\prime\prime}
such that
\ell\leq j^{\prime\prime}<j^{\prime}
. Since
j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)
, the first point
(a)
already implies
\ell\leq j
. Moreover, if there exists
j^{\prime\prime\prime}
marked such that
\ell\leq j^{\prime\prime\prime}<j
then by uniqueness of the path linking a leaf to a node we either have
j^{\prime\prime\prime}=j^{\prime}
, which is impossible since
j^{\prime}
is unmarked, or
\ell\leq j^{\prime\prime\prime}<j^{\prime}
, which is impossible by the second point
(b)
. This proves the inclusion from left to right for
j
, and concludes the proof of (
2.49
). Since each branching nodes have at least one unmarked child, (
2.49
) proves that
\mathtt{prebush}(j)
is never empty.

We now prove the second part. By contradiction, if
\ell\in\mathtt{prebush}(j)\cap\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime})
, then (
2.49
) implies
\ell\leq j
and
\ell\leq j^{\prime}
. This shows that
j
and
j^{\prime}
are comparable and that
\ell\leq j<j^{\prime}
or
\ell\leq j^{\prime}<j
, which contradicts the fact that both
j
and
j^{\prime}
are marked. This concludes the proof of (
2.50
) and of the lemma.
∎

Definition 2.17

(Bush)
.

Let
C
be a coupling or a tree.

•

We define the
bush
of any
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
by

\displaystyle\mathtt{bush}(j)\vcentcolon=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)\\
\text{$j^{\prime}$ marked }\end{subarray}}\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime}).

(2.51)

•

We define

\displaystyle\mathtt{Bushes}(C)\vcentcolon=\left\{\mathtt{bush}(j)~\middle|~j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\right\}.

Example 2.8
.

We illustrate here Definitions
2.15
,
2.16
and
2.17
. Consider the following tree.
{\mathrm{low-}\ell}
1
\mathrm{st}
2
2
{\mathrm{low-}m}
4
5
6
{\rm in}
6
8
9
1
\mathrm{high}
3
3
4
{\mathrm{low-}r}
5
{\rm in}
7
10
11
7
\mathrm{high}
8
12
13
14

We have labelled arbitrarily the nodes by
blue
numbers and the leaves by
magenta
numbers. The marked children of low nodes are put into
boxes
. We have

\displaystyle\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2})=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}2},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}6}\},

\displaystyle\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3})

\displaystyle=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}4},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}12},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}13},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}14}\},

\displaystyle\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4})

\displaystyle=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}6}\},

\displaystyle\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5})

\displaystyle=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}12},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}13},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}14}\},

\displaystyle\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6})

\displaystyle=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}8},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}9}\},

\displaystyle\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7})

\displaystyle=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}10},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}11}\}.

Note that
\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3})
does not intersect
\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6})
or
\mathtt{prebush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7})
. Moreover we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{bush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1})=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}3},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}4},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}12},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}13},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}14}\},\quad\mathtt{bush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4})=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}5},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}8},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}9}\},\quad\mathtt{bush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5})=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}7},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}10},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}11}\}.

The following proposition gathers important properties of bushes.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
C
be a coupling or a tree.

(i)

The following properties hold:

\displaystyle\forall j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C),

\displaystyle\;\mathtt{bush}(j)\neq\emptyset,

(2.52)

\displaystyle\forall j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C),

\displaystyle\;j\neq j^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\mathtt{bush}(j)\cap\mathtt{bush}(j^{\prime})=\emptyset.

(2.53)

(ii)

Moreover, for
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
we have

\displaystyle\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)~\middle|~\ell\leq j\right\}=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\\
j^{\prime}\leq j\end{subarray}}\mathtt{bush}(j^{\prime})\sqcup\mathtt{prebush}(j),

(2.54)

and for
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{offspring}(j)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\\
\text{$j^{\prime}\leq j$ and $j^{\prime}\nleq j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}$}\end{subarray}}\mathtt{bush}(j^{\prime}),

(2.55)

where
j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}
is the unmarked child of
j
.

Proof.

We start with the first part of the proposition:

•

The first part of Lemma
2.3
says that all prebushes are non-empty, which thanks to (
2.51
) implies in turn that all bushes are non-empty, thus proving (
2.52
).

•

Consider now
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
with
j\neq j^{\prime}
and assume that there is
\ell\in\mathtt{bush}(j)\cap\mathtt{bush}(j^{\prime})
. According to (
2.51
), there exists
j_{1}\in\mathtt{children}(j)
and
j_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j^{\prime})
both marked such that
\ell\in\mathtt{prebush}(j_{1})\cap\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime}_{1})
which contradicts the second part of Lemma
2.3
. This proves (
2.53
).

We now look at the second part of the proposition:

•

If
\ell\leq j
, then either there exists no marked node or leaf in between
\ell
and
j
or there exists at least one. In the first situation, (
2.49
) implies that
\ell\in\mathtt{prebush}(j)
. In the second situation, let
j^{\prime}
the minimal such marked node or leaf such that
\ell\leq j^{\prime}<j
. By minimality, (
2.49
) again implies that
\ell\in\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime})
. We have thus proved

\displaystyle\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)~\middle|~\ell\leq j\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{prebush}(j)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}<j\\
\text{$j^{\prime}$ marked}\end{subarray}}\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{prebush}(j)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\\
j^{\prime\prime}\leq j\end{subarray}}\mathtt{bush}(j^{\prime\prime}),

where we have used the definition of bushes (
2.51
). This proves (
2.54
).

•

Finally, if
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
then (
2.46
) implies

\displaystyle\mathtt{offspring}(j)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)\setminus\{j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}\}}\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)~\middle|~\ell\leq j^{\prime}\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)\setminus\{j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}\}}\left(\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime})\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\\
j^{\prime\prime}\leq j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathtt{bush}(j^{\prime\prime})\right)

where we applied (
2.54
) to each
j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)\setminus\{j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}\}
. We obtain

\displaystyle\mathtt{offspring}(j)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)\setminus\{j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}\}}\mathtt{prebush}(j^{\prime})\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\\
\text{$j^{\prime}<j$ and $j^{\prime}\nleq j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}$}\end{subarray}}\mathtt{bush}(j^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\\
\text{$j^{\prime}\leq j$ and $j^{\prime}\nleq j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}$}\end{subarray}}\mathtt{bush}(j^{\prime\prime}),

where we have used (
2.51
).

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Example 2.9
.

We remark that in Example
2.8
, the bushes do not intersect each other and match

\displaystyle\mathtt{offspring}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1})=\mathtt{bush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1})\sqcup\mathtt{bush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5}),\quad\mathtt{offspring}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4})=\mathtt{bush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4}),\quad\mathtt{offspring}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5})=\mathtt{bush}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5}).

As opposed to the above definitions, the next one does depend on the involution
\sigma
and therefore cannot be applied to a tree but only to a coupling.

Definition 2.18

(Self-coupled bushes)
.

Let
C
be a coupling and
\sigma
its coupling map.

•

We say that
B\in\mathtt{Bushes}(C)
is
self-coupled
if the set

\displaystyle B_{+}\vcentcolon=B\cap\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}

has the same cardinal as the set

\displaystyle B_{-}\vcentcolon=B\cap\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}

and if
\sigma(B_{+})=B_{-}
.

•

We define

\displaystyle n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\#\left\{B\in\mathtt{Bushes}(C)~\middle|~\text{$B$ is self-coupled}\right\}.

The following lemma gives a natural bound on the number of couplings with a given number of self-coupled bushes. In its proof, we will need a simple fact about factorials: if
(a_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket}
are some non-zero integers, then

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{p}a_{i}!\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{p}a_{i}-p+1\right)!

(2.56)

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
n,q\in\mathbb{N}
and let

\displaystyle c_{n,q}:=\#\left\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}~\middle|~n_{1}+n_{2}=n,\;n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)=q\right\}.

There exists
\Lambda>0
such that

\displaystyle c_{n,q}\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\left(\frac{n}{2}+2-q\right)!.

Proof.

Let
C=((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1})),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}),\sigma)\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
be a coupling with
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
and
q
self-coupled bushes. Define

\displaystyle M\vcentcolon=\#\left\{B\in\mathtt{Bushes}(A_{1})\sqcup\mathtt{Bushes}(A_{2})~\middle|~\#B_{+}=\#B_{-}\right\},

where
\#B_{+}
and
\#B_{-}
are introduced in Definition
2.18
, and assume
M>0
. Recall that for a self-coupled bush
B
, we have that
\#B_{+}=\#B_{-}
and the pairing
\sigma
is a bijection from
B_{+}
to
B_{-}
. Therefore
q\leq M
.

Let
\left(\mathtt{bush}(j_{i})\right)_{i\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket}
be an enumeration of the self-coupled bushes of
C
and
m_{i}=\#\mathtt{bush}(j_{i})_{+}
their number of positive leaves. The number of different possible pairings
\sigma
for which
\mathtt{bush}(j_{i})
is self-coupled for all
i\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket
is equal to

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{q}m_{i}!\times\left(\frac{n+2}{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{q}m_{i}\right)!

(2.57)

where
\frac{n+2}{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{q}m_{i}
corresponds to the number of positive leaves in
A_{1}\sqcup A_{2}
that do not belong to any
\mathtt{bush}(j_{i})
. We now apply (
2.56
) to (
2.57
), in particular noting that
\frac{n+2}{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{q}m_{i}
might vanish, and obtain

\displaystyle\#\left\{\text{$\sigma$ pairing such that $\left(\mathtt{bush}(j_{i})\right)_{i\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket}$ are self-coupled}\right\}\leq\left(\frac{n+2}{2}-q+1\right)!.

Since the choice of the
q
self-coupled bushes among the
M
bushes satisfying
\#B_{+}=\#B_{-}
is arbitrary, we obtain

\displaystyle\#\left\{\text{$\sigma$ pairing such that $n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)=q$}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\binom{M}{q}\left(\frac{n+2}{2}-q+1\right)!\leq\Lambda_{1}^{n}\left(\frac{n+2}{2}-q+1\right)!

with
\Lambda_{1}\geq\sqrt{2}
. In the above inequality we have used that
M\leq n_{T}(C)\leq\frac{n}{2}
. Finally, we have

\displaystyle c_{n,q}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{1}^{n}\left(\frac{n+2}{2}-q+1\right)!

\displaystyle\quad\times\#\left\{((A_{1},\varphi_{1},c_{1})),(A_{2},\varphi_{2},c_{2}))\in\mathcal{A}_{n_{1}}^{\eta_{1},\iota_{1}}\times\mathcal{A}_{n_{2}}^{\eta_{2},\iota_{2}}~\middle|~\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\{0,1\},\;\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\in\{\pm\},\;n_{1}+n_{2}=n\right\}.

According to Remark
2.5
, there exists
\Lambda_{2}
such that we have
\#\mathcal{A}_{n_{i}}^{\eta_{i},\iota_{i}}\leq\Lambda_{2}^{n_{i}}
. Thus we get

\displaystyle c_{n,q}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{1}^{n}\left(\frac{n+2}{2}-q+1\right)!\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n}\Lambda_{2}^{n_{1}}\Lambda_{2}^{n_{2}}\leq(2\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2})^{n}\left(\frac{n+2}{2}-q+1\right)!,

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

3
Convergence of the series

The aim of this section is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 3.1
.

There exists
\Lambda>0
such that for all
C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|\leq\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{\frac{n(C)}{2}}.

(3.1)

Moreover, there exists
\alpha>0
such that if
C
satisfies

\displaystyle n(C)-2n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)

\displaystyle\geq 80,\qquad n(C)\leq|\ln\varepsilon|^{3},

then for all
\varepsilon\leq\delta
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|\leq\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{\frac{n(C)}{2}}\varepsilon^{\alpha\left(\frac{n(C)}{2}-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)\right)}.

(3.2)

We will deduce the following key corollary, whose proof is detailed in Section
3.4
.

Corollary 3.1
.

There exists
\Lambda>0
such that, for any fixed
A>0
, with probability
\geq 1-L^{-A}
we have

\left\|X_{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,\varepsilon^{-2}\delta],H^{s}(L\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leq(\Lambda\delta)^{\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{2}}L^{\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{\beta}+A}

(3.3)

for any
0\leq n\leq|\ln\varepsilon|^{3}
and any
s\geq 0
.

Remark 3.1
.

The proof of corollary
3.1
will also require us to study the time derivative of
X_{n}
. Its tree decomposition will be performed in Section
3.3
, where the equivalent of Proposition
3.1
is also stated, see Proposition
3.6
.

Proving Proposition
3.1
will be done by distinguishing two regimes: one regime where low ternary nodes are dominating and one where high ternary nodes are dominating. The first one corresponds to the case where

\displaystyle n_{B}(C)+2(n_{\mathrm{low}}(C)-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C))\geq\frac{n(C)-2n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)}{10},

and will be treated in Section
3.1
, while the second one corresponds to the case where

\displaystyle n_{B}(C)+2(n_{\mathrm{low}}(C)-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C))<\frac{n(C)-2n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)}{10},

and will be treated in Section
3.2
.

The study of these two regimes will involve two combinatorial objects associated to couplings: bushes for the low nodes regime and spanning trees for the high nodes regime. However, in both cases we will need a sort of dual point of view on the decoration maps
\kappa
introduced for trees in Definition
2.8
and for couplings in Definition
2.14
. Recall the first part of Remark
2.8
, which says that a decoration map of a given coupling
C
can be uniquely defined from its restriction to
\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
. In other words, there exists a natural bijection from the set of decoration maps of
C
and
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}
, which is the set of maps from
\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
C
be a coupling.

•

If
\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
, we consider the linear map
K(\ell):(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by

\displaystyle K(\ell)\left((\kappa(\ell^{\prime}))_{\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}\right)=\kappa(\ell).

(3.4)

•

If
\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}
, we define

\displaystyle K(\ell)\vcentcolon=-K(\sigma(\ell)),

(3.5)

where
\sigma
is the coupling map of
C
, and if
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)
, we define

\displaystyle K(j)\vcentcolon=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)\\
\ell<j\end{subarray}}K(\ell).

(3.6)

•

We define a linear subspace of
L\left((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}},\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
by

\displaystyle V(C)\vcentcolon=\mathrm{Vect}\left\{K(\ell)~\middle|~\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}\right\}.

(3.7)

Remark 3.2
.

We note two simple facts:

•

By definition we have
K(j)\in V(C)
for all
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
.

•

If
\kappa
is a decoration of
C
as in Definition
2.14
, then
\kappa
can be seen as an element of
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}
and (
3.4
)-(
3.6
) imply that for all
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
we have

\displaystyle K(j)(\kappa)=\kappa(j).

3.1
Low nodes regime

The goal of this section is to estimate
\widehat{F_{C}}
in the low nodes regime.

Proposition 3.2
.

There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{low}},\Lambda>0
such that if
C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
is a coupling satisfying

\displaystyle n_{B}(C)+2(n_{\mathrm{low}}(C)-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C))\geq\frac{n(C)-2n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)}{10},

(3.8)

and

\displaystyle n(C)-2n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)\geq 80,

(3.9)

then for any
\varepsilon\leq\delta
and any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|\lesssim(\Lambda\delta)^{\frac{n(C)}{2}}\varepsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{low}}\left(\frac{n(C)}{2}-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)\right)}.

(3.10)

Remark 3.3
.

Thanks to (
2.44
), the estimate (
3.10
) is obviously true if
k=0
. Therefore, in the rest of Section
3.1
we assume that
k\neq 0
.

The following lemma gives a first rough estimate for
\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
.

Lemma 3.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

(3.11)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\\
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)}\chi\left(\left|\sum_{\ell\in\mathtt{offspring}(j)}\kappa(\ell)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right).

Proof.

We recall (
2.40
):

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}(-i)^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)}e^{-i\Omega_{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}t}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)}q_{j}\int_{I_{C}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}e^{-i\Omega_{j}\varepsilon^{-2}t}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}}M^{c(\ell),c(\sigma(\ell))}(\varphi(\ell)\kappa(\ell))^{\varphi(\ell)}.

We use that the oscillating integral is less than
\delta^{n_{T}(C)}
if
t\in[0,\delta]
, that the maps
M^{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}
are continuous maps with compact support included in
B(0,R)
and that the
q_{j}
are uniformly bounded (see Lemma
2.2
) to get that there exists a constant
\Lambda
independant of the coupling such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\\
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)}\chi_{j},

where we also used the simple fact that
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)
implies
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C))\subset B(0,R)
and where
\chi_{j}
satisfies
\chi_{j}\leq\chi\left(\left|\kappa(j_{1})+\kappa(j_{2})\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)
with
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
the marked children of
j
. Therefore, (
2.46
) implies that

\displaystyle\chi_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\chi\left(\left|\sum_{\ell\in\mathtt{offspring}(j)}\kappa(\ell)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right),

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Following this lemma, Proposition
3.2
is proved in the following sections. In Section
3.1.1
, we study the properties of non-self-coupled bushes and in particular obtain a triangular structure for the product in (
3.11
). In Section
3.1.2
, we define a basis of
V(C)
and use it to perform a change of variables in the sum over decorations. Benefiting from both the triangular structure of the product and the change of variables, we finally estimate
\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
in Section
3.1.3
.

3.1.1
Non-self-coupled bushes

For
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\vcentcolon=\sum_{\ell\in\mathtt{bush}(j)}K(\ell).

(3.12)

By definition of a self-coupled bush,
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)
vanishes as a linear map if and only if
\mathtt{bush}(j)
is self-coupled. To make things more precise, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\vcentcolon=\left\{\text{low nodes whose bush is not self-coupled}\right\},\qquad n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\vcentcolon=\#\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C),

so that in particular we have
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\neq 0
for all
j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
. We enumerate the elements in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
in a way compatible with the order in
C
, that is we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)=\left\{j_{i}~\middle|~i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket\right\},

with
j_{p_{1}}<j_{p_{2}}
implies
p_{1}<p_{2}
. By using the decomposition with the
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)
’s over positive leaves, the following lemma constructs a linearly independent subfamily of large cardinal, making crucial use of the non-self-coupledness.

Lemma 3.2
.

The rank of the family
(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j))_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)}
is grater or equal to
\frac{n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}{2}
.

Proof.

If
