\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)=\emptyset
the lemma is trivial. If not, for each
j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
we define the following subset of
\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
:

\displaystyle B_{j}\vcentcolon=\left\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}~\middle|~\left(\ell\in\mathtt{bush}(j)\,\mathrm{and}\,\sigma(\ell)\notin\mathtt{bush}(j)\right)\,\mathrm{or}\,\left(\sigma(\ell)\in\mathtt{bush}(j)\,\mathrm{and}\,\ell\notin\mathtt{bush}(j)\right)\right\}.

The set
B_{j}
is non-empty because
\mathtt{bush}(j)
is non-empty (recall (
2.52
)) and non-self-coupled by definition. Since bushes are disjoint (recall (
2.53
)), we also observe that for any
\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
there exist at most two nodes
j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
such that
\ell\in B_{j_{1}}\cap B_{j_{2}}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)=\sum_{\ell\in B_{j}}\iota_{j}(\ell)K(\ell),

(3.13)

where
\iota_{j}(\ell)=1
if
\ell\in\mathtt{bush}(j)
and
\iota_{j}(\ell)=-1
if
\sigma(\ell)\in\mathtt{bush}(j)
.

We set
b_{j}:=\#B_{j}
and choose an enumeration of the elements of
B_{j}
,
B_{j}=\left\{\ell^{j}_{i}~\middle|~i\in\llbracket 1,b_{j}\rrbracket\right\}
. We build the sets
\mathcal{N}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
and
\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
by induction as follows. For
i=0
, we set
\mathcal{N}_{0}\vcentcolon=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{R}_{0}\vcentcolon=\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
. If
(\mathcal{N}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{i})
and
(\mathcal{R}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{R}_{i})
are constructed, then define
\mathcal{N}_{i+1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
as follows:

•

if
\mathcal{R}_{i}=\emptyset
, then
\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\vcentcolon=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{N}_{i+1}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}_{i}
,

•

otherwise, define
R_{i}=\min\mathcal{R}_{i}
and set

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{i+1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}_{i}\cup\{j_{R_{i}}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{i+1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{R}_{i}\setminus\left\{r\in\mathcal{R}_{i}~\middle|~\ell^{j_{R_{i}}}_{1}\in B_{j_{r}}\right\}.

As observed above, given
\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
we have
\#\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)~\middle|~\ell\in B_{j}\right\}\leq 2
. Therefore, as long as
\mathcal{R}_{i}\neq\emptyset
we have

\displaystyle 1\leq\left\{r\in\mathcal{R}_{i}~\middle|~\ell^{j_{R_{i}}}_{1}\in B_{j_{r}}\right\}\leq 2,

which implies

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{R}_{i}-2\leq\#\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\leq\#\mathcal{R}_{i}-1.

The inequality on the right implies that
\mathcal{R}_{n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}=\emptyset
and the one on the left that
\#\mathcal{R}_{i}\geq n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-2i
. We define
i_{0}\vcentcolon=\min\left\{i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket~\middle|~\mathcal{R}_{i}=\emptyset\right\}
and thus have
i_{0}\geq\frac{n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}{2}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{N}_{i}\cap\left\{j_{r}~\middle|~r\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\right\}=\emptyset
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
which implies that
\#\mathcal{N}_{i+1}=\#\mathcal{N}_{i}+1
as long as
\mathcal{R}_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Hence

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}=i_{0}\geq\frac{n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}{2}.

Now we prove that the family
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}}
is linearly independent. We fix an enumeration
\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}=\left\{J_{i}~\middle|~i\in\llbracket 1,i_{0}\rrbracket\right\}
and let
(r_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,i_{0}\rrbracket}\in\mathbb{R}^{i_{0}}
be such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}}r_{i}\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i})=0.

(3.14)

We assume that the
r_{i}
s are not all zeros and set
i_{1}:=\min\left\{i\in\llbracket 1,i_{0}\rrbracket~\middle|~r_{i}\neq 0\right\}
. If
i_{1}=i_{0}
, then (
3.14
) simply becomes
r_{i_{1}}\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i_{1}})=0
, implying
r_{i_{1}}=0
since
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i_{1}})\neq 0
. Then necessarily
i_{1}<i_{0}
, and we note that by construction we have
\ell^{J_{i_{1}}}_{1}\notin B_{J_{i}}
for all
i\in\llbracket i_{1}+1,i_{0}\rrbracket
. Since (
3.14
) can be rewritten

\displaystyle\sum_{i=i_{1}}^{i_{0}}r_{i}\sum_{\ell\in B_{J_{i}}}\iota_{J_{i}}(\ell)K(\ell)=0

with the help of (
3.13
), we see that
K\left(\ell^{J_{i_{1}}}_{1}\right)
only appears once in the sum with
r_{i_{1}}\iota_{J_{i_{1}}}\left(\ell^{J_{i_{1}}}_{1}\right)
as coefficient. Since the family
(K(\ell))_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}
is linearly independent, we again deduce that
r_{i_{1}}=0
and reach a contradiction. Therefore
r_{i}=0
for all
i
. This concludes the proof of the lemma since we have constructed a linearly independent subfamily of
(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j))_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)}
of cardinal grater or equal to
\frac{n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}{2}
.
∎

The following lemma constructs a basis of
\mathrm{Vect}\left\{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\,|\,j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\right\}
compatible, in some way, with the parentality order.

Lemma 3.3
.

Assume that
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\neq\emptyset
. There exists a subset
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
such that

(i)

its cardinal
n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\vcentcolon=\#\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
satisfies
n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\geq\frac{n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}{2}
,

(ii)

the family
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)}
is linearly independent in
V(C)
,

(iii)

there exists an enumeration of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)=\left\{J_{i}~\middle|~i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket\right\}
such that

a.

there exists no
j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
satisfying
j<J_{1}
;

b.

if
n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\geq 2
, for
i\in\llbracket 2,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
and
j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
we have

\displaystyle j<J_{i}\Longrightarrow\,\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\in\mathrm{Vect}\left\{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i^{\prime}})~\middle|~i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket\right\}.

(3.15)

Proof.

We first build a subset
P\subseteq\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
by induction in the following way:
P_{1}\vcentcolon=\{1\}
and if
P_{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq P_{m}
for
m\geq 1
are defined then

•

if
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j_{m+1})
is a linear combination of
(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j_{p}))_{p\in P_{m}}
, we set
P_{m+1}\vcentcolon=P_{m}
,

•

otherwise, we set
P_{m+1}\vcentcolon=P_{m}\sqcup\{m+1\}
.

We define
P\vcentcolon=P_{n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\vcentcolon=\left\{j_{m}~\middle|~m\in P\right\}.

Statement
(ii)
is satisfied since the family of linear maps
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)}
is linearly independent by construction. We also have that for any
i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j_{i})\in\mathrm{Vect}\left\{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j_{i^{\prime}})~\middle|~i^{\prime}\in P\cap\llbracket 1,i\rrbracket\right\}.

(3.16)

This shows that
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)}
is a basis of
\mathrm{Vect}\left\{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\,|\,j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\right\}
and proves statement
(i)
thanks to Lemma
3.2
.

It only remains to prove
(iii)
. Note that it is always possible to enumerate
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)=\left\{J_{i}~\middle|~i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket\right\},

in such a way that for all
I,I^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
, writing
J_{I}=j_{i}
and
J_{I^{\prime}}=j_{i^{\prime}}
with
i,i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
, we have that
i<i^{\prime}
is equivalent to
I<I^{\prime}
.
Since
j_{1}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
by construction, this enumeration is such that
J_{1}=j_{1}
and there exists no
j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
satisfying
j<J_{1}
, which is point
a.
of the third statement. Furthermore, if
n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\geq 2
we observe the following. Let
i\in\llbracket 2,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
and
j_{m}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
for some
m\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
. Thanks to (
3.16
), there exists
M\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
such that
J_{M}=j_{\max P\cap\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket}
and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j_{m})\in\mathrm{Vect}\left\{\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i^{\prime}})~\middle|~i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket\right\}.

(3.17)

Assume that
j_{m}<J_{i}
and write
J_{i}=j_{m^{\prime}}
for
m^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
. Since
j_{m}<j_{m^{\prime}}
we have that
m<m^{\prime}
, hence
\max P\cap\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket<m^{\prime}
, which in turn implies
M<i
. This implies
M\leq i-1
and together with (
3.17
) this concludes the proof of (
3.15
).
∎

The third point of the previous lemma allows us to rewrite (
3.11
) in a more suitable form.

Lemma 3.4
.

There exist functions
\Xi_{J_{i}}
for
i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}

(3.18)

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\\
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\chi\left(\left|\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i})(\kappa)+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i^{\prime}})(\kappa)\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right).

Proof.

We start from (
3.11
) and first bound by
1
the
\chi_{j}
for
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
. Using in addition (
2.55
) we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\\
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\chi\left(\left|\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i})(\kappa)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\\
\text{$j<J_{i}$ and $j\nleq J_{i,\mathrm{unmarked}}$}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)(\kappa)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right),

where
J_{i,\mathrm{unmarked}}
is the unmarked children of
J_{i}
and where we also used the fact that
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)=0
if
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
. The third point of the previous lemma shows that there exists a function
\Xi_{J_{i}}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\\
\text{$j<J_{i}$ and $j\nleq J_{i,\mathrm{unmarked}}$}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)(\kappa)=\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i^{\prime}})(\kappa)\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right),

with the convention that
\Xi_{J_{1}}=0
since the sum on the left is a sum over the emptyset in the case
i=1
. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

3.1.2
A basis of
V(C)
and a change of variable

Since the family of linear maps
\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)}
is linearly independent in
V(C)
, we can complete it into a basis. Given that
V(C)
has dimension
\#\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}=\frac{n}{2}+1
, we set
p:=\frac{n}{2}+1-n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}
and let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}\vcentcolon=\left\{\ell_{i}~\middle|~i\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}

be such that

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(j),K(\ell)\,|\,j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}(C),\ell\in\mathfrak{L}\right)

(3.19)

forms a basis of
V(C)
. We will use this basis to make a change of variables in (
3.18
).

We first benefit from the natural bijection between the set of decoration maps of
C
and
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}
, given by the restriction to
\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}
of all decorations, and rewrite the sum on
\kappa
in (
3.18
) as a sum over
B(0,R)^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}
(we still denote the elements of this set by
\kappa
). This gives

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\kappa\in B(0,R)^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\chi\left(\left|\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i})(\kappa)+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i^{\prime}})(\kappa)\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{K(r)(\kappa)=k},

where
r
is the root of the first tree in the coupling
C
. The fact that (
3.19
) is a basis of
V(C)
implies that the map

\displaystyle\Phi:\kappa\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}\longmapsto\left(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i})(\kappa),K(\ell_{i^{\prime}})(\kappa)\,|\,i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket,i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket\right)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\frac{n}{2}+1}

is a bijection and moreover satisfies
\Phi\left(\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}\right)\subset\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}+1}
after the definition (
3.12
) of
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}
. This allows us to make the change of variable
\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}},y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)=\Phi(\kappa)
and obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

(3.20)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\\
\left(y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\cap B(0,R)\right)^{p}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\chi\left(\left|x_{i}+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{K}(r)\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}},y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)=k}

where
\tilde{K}(r)
is a map defined on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\frac{n}{2}+1}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{K}(r)\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}},y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)=K(r)\left(\Phi^{-1}\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}},y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)\right).

Since
K(r)\in V(C)
, there exist some real numbers
(a_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket}
et
(b_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket}
such that

\displaystyle K(r)=\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}a_{i}\mathfrak{K}_{\mathrm{bush}}(J_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{p}b_{i}K(\ell_{i}).

This implies the following expression for
\tilde{K}(r)
:

\displaystyle\tilde{K}(r)\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}},y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}a_{i}x_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}b_{i}y_{i},

where we also used the definition of the change of variable
\Phi
. Therefore (
3.20
) becomes

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

(3.21)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\\
\left(y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\cap B(0,R)\right)^{p}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\chi\left(\left|x_{i}+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}a_{i}x_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}b_{i}y_{i}=k}.

3.1.3
Proof of Proposition
3.2

We are now ready to conclude the proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Starting from (
3.21
), we distinguish two cases. The first case is when
a_{i}=0
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
. If this holds, then the situation where all the
b_{i}
’s vanish, i.e when
\tilde{K}(r)=0
, gives a zero contribution because of the characteristic function in (
3.21
) and
k\neq 0
. Therefore we can assume that there exists
i_{0}\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
such that
b_{i_{0}}\neq 0
. This implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{\left(y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\cap B(0,R)\right)^{p}}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{K}(r)\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}},y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)=k}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{(y_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket\setminus\{i_{0}\}}\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\cap B(0,R)\right)^{p-1}}\mathbbm{1}_{b_{i_{0}}y_{i_{0}}=k-\sum_{i\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket\setminus\{i_{0}\}}b_{i}y_{i}}.

We thus obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\left(y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\cap B(0,R)\right)^{p}}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{K}(r)\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}},y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)=k}\lesssim\Lambda^{(p-1)d}L^{(p-1)d}R^{(p-1)d}.

(3.22)

Plugging (
3.22
) into (
3.20
) gives

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{\left(p-1-\frac{n}{2}\right)d}

(3.23)

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\chi\left(\left|x_{i}+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right),

where we also absorbed
R
into the universal constant
\Lambda
. In order to estimate the sum over
\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}
, we benefit from the triangular structure of the product and write

\displaystyle\sum_{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\chi\left(\left|x_{i}+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sum_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\chi\left(\left|x_{1}\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)\left(\cdots\left(\sum_{x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\chi\left(\left|x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}+\Xi_{J_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)\right)\cdots\right).

Since
L\varepsilon^{\gamma}=\varepsilon^{\gamma-\beta}
tends to infinity if
\varepsilon
tends to 0 (since
\beta>2
and
\gamma
is small), for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\#\left\{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}~\middle|~|x+\xi|\lesssim 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}\right\}\leq\Lambda^{d}L^{d}\varepsilon^{\gamma d},

(3.24)

we gain a factor
\Lambda^{d}L^{d}\varepsilon^{\gamma d}
for each sum over
x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
for
i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
. This implies

\displaystyle\sum_{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\chi\left(\left|x_{i}+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)\leq\Lambda^{n}L^{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}d}\varepsilon^{\gamma n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}d}.

By combining this estimate with (
3.23
) we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)+\gamma n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}d}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}.

(3.25)

where we crucially used that
p=\frac{n}{2}+1-n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}
so that
L
disappears from the estimate.

The second case is when there exists at least one non-zero
a_{i}
. We set
i_{0}=\max\left\{i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket~\middle|~a_{i}\neq 0\right\}
. Again, we start from (
3.20
) and benefit from the triangular structure of the product to rewrite (
3.20
) as

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\cap B(0,R)\right)^{p}\\
\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{i_{0}}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{i_{0}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{i_{0}}\chi\left(\left|x_{i}+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{a_{i_{0}}x_{i_{0}}=k-f(y)-\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}-1}a_{i}x_{i}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{x_{i_{0}+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\chi\left(\left|x_{i_{0}+1}+\Xi_{J_{i_{0}+1}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i_{0}\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left(\cdots\left(\sum_{x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\chi\left(\left|x_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}+\Xi_{J_{n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)\right)\cdots\right)

where for clarity we used the notation
f(y)=\sum_{i=1}^{p}b_{i}y_{i}
. As above, each sum over the
x_{i}
’s for
i\in\llbracket i_{0}+1,n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\rrbracket
gives a factor
\Lambda^{d}L^{d}\varepsilon^{\gamma d}
so that we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{\left(n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-i_{0}-\frac{n}{2}\right)d}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)+\gamma(n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-i_{0})d}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\cap B(0,R)\right)^{p}\\
\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{i_{0}}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{i_{0}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{i_{0}}\chi\left(\left|x_{i}+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{a_{i_{0}}x_{i_{0}}=k-f(y)-\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}-1}a_{i}x_{i}}.

Since
a_{i_{0}}\neq 0
, the sum over
x_{i_{0}}
does not contribute and if we simply bound the corresponding cutoff function and the characteristic function by
1
we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{\left(n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-i_{0}-\frac{n}{2}\right)d}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)+\gamma(n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-i_{0})d}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\cap B(0,R)\right)^{p}\\
\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{i_{0}-1}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{i_{0}-1}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{i_{0}-1}\chi\left(\left|x_{i}+\Xi_{J_{i}}\left(\left(x_{i^{\prime}}\right)_{i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\right|\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\right).

Using again the triangular structure for the product as in the first case, we finally obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}L^{\left(n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-i_{0}-\frac{n}{2}+p+i_{0}-1\right)d}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)+\gamma(n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-i_{0})d+\gamma(i_{0}-1)d}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)+\gamma(n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-1)d},

(3.26)

where we again used
p=\frac{n}{2}+1-n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}
.

We conclude the estimate of
\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
, by distinguishing between
n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}
vanishing or not. If
n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}=0
, then (
3.25
) holds with
n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}=0
. Then, using first
\varepsilon\leq\delta
and
n(C)=n_{B}(C)+2n_{T}(C)
and then (
3.8
) together with
n_{\mathrm{low}}-n_{\mathrm{scb}}=n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}=0
we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{\frac{n_{B}(C)}{2}}\delta^{\frac{n(C)}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{10}\left(\frac{n(C)}{2}-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)\right)}\delta^{\frac{n(C)}{2}}.

If
n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\geq 1
, then the estimates (
3.25
) and (
3.26
) show that we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\delta^{\frac{n(C)}{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{n_{B}(C)}{2}+\gamma(n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-1)d},

where we already used
\varepsilon\leq\delta
and
n(C)=n_{B}(C)+2n_{T}(C)
. Using now the first point of Lemma
3.3
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{n_{B}(C)}{2}+\gamma(n^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-1)d

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\left(1,\frac{\gamma d}{2}\right)\left(n_{B}(C)+2n^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}-4\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\left(1,\frac{\gamma d}{2}\right)\left(\frac{n(C)-2n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)}{10}-4\right).

Using (
3.9
) we obtain
\frac{n(C)-2n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)}{10}-4\geq\frac{1}{10}\left(\frac{n(C)}{2}-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)\right)
and therefore

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|\leq\Lambda^{n}\delta^{\frac{n(C)}{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{20}\min\left(1,\frac{\gamma d}{2}\right)\left(\frac{n(C)}{2}-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)\right)}.

Setting
\alpha_{\mathrm{low}}\vcentcolon=\min\left(\frac{1}{10},\frac{1}{20}\min\left(1,\frac{\gamma d}{2}\right)\right)
concludes the proof of Proposition
3.2
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

As a byproduct of this proof, we can easily obtain the weak bound (
3.1
). Indeed, the estimates (
3.25
)-(
3.26
) (which don’t yet make any use of the assumptions (
3.8
)-(
3.9
)) imply in particular that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}.

Since
\varepsilon\leq\delta
and
n(C)=n_{B}(C)+2n_{T}(C)
we clearly have
\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\delta^{n_{T}(C)}\leq\delta^{\frac{n(C)}{2}}
.

3.2
High nodes regime

The goal of this section is to estimate
\widehat{F_{C}}
in the high nodes regime.

Proposition 3.3
.

There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{high}},\Lambda>0
such that if
C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
is a coupling satisfying

\displaystyle n_{B}(C)+2(n_{\mathrm{low}}(C)-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C))

\displaystyle<\frac{n(C)-2n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)}{10},

(3.27)

\displaystyle n(C)-2n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)

\displaystyle\geq 10,

(3.28)

\displaystyle n(C)

\displaystyle\leq|\ln\varepsilon|^{3},

(3.29)

then for any
\varepsilon\leq\delta
and any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|\leq\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{\frac{n(C)}{2}}\varepsilon^{\alpha\left(\frac{n(C)}{2}-n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)\right)}.

(3.30)

As in the low nodes regime of Proposition
3.2
, (
2.44
) implies that (
3.30
) holds if
k=0
, so that we can already assume
k\neq 0
. Moreover, note that the high nodes regimes implies
n_{T}(C)\geq 1
, since otherwise (
3.27
) would imply
n_{B}(C)<\frac{n_{B}(C)}{10}
.

Proposition
3.3
is proved in the following sections. In Section
3.2.1
, we express the oscillating integrals in terms of total orders of ternary nodes. In Section
3.2.2
, we define total orders on the whole coupling based on total orders on ternary nodes. In Sections
3.2.3
and
3.2.4
, we give properties of a special family
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
and use it to perform a change of variable. In Section
3.2.5
we estimate the sums originating in the oscillating integrals and finally in Section
3.2.6
we conclude the proof of Proposition
3.3
.

3.2.1
Representation of oscillating integrals

In this subsubsection we explain how to pass from an oscillating integral to a manageable function of the dispersion relation. We first introduce notions related to order relations on finite sets. Recall that if
<_{1}
and
<_{2}
are two order relations (partial or not) on a given set, we say that
<_{2}
is compatible with
<_{1}
if
x<_{1}y
implies
x<_{2}y
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
A
be a nonempty finite set with a partial order relation
<_{A}
.

•

If
\rho:\llbracket 1,\#A\rrbracket\longrightarrow A
is a bijection, we define the total order relation
<_{\rho}
on
A
by

\displaystyle a_{1}<_{\rho}a_{2}\iff\rho^{-1}(a_{1})<\rho^{-1}(a_{2}).

•

We denote by
\mathfrak{M}(A,<_{A})
the set of bijections
\rho:\llbracket 1,\#A\rrbracket\longrightarrow A
such that
<_{\rho}
is compatible with
<_{A}
.

Remark 3.5
.

If
<
is a total order relation on
A
, then
A
can be written under the form

\displaystyle A=\left\{a_{1}<\cdots<a_{\#A}\right\}

If we define
\rho(i)=a_{i}
for
i\in\llbracket 1,\#A\rrbracket
then we have
<_{\rho}=<
. This shows that

\displaystyle\left\{\text{bijection from $\llbracket 1,\#A\rrbracket$ to $A$}\right\}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\left\{\text{total order relation on $A$}\right\}

\displaystyle\rho\qquad

\displaystyle\longmapsto\qquad<_{\rho}

is a bijection.

In the sequel, the notation
\mathfrak{M}(A,<_{A})
will always be used with
A
a subset of leaves and branching nodes of a coupling and with
<_{A}
the natural parentality partial order. Therefore, we will use the shorter notation
\mathfrak{M}(A)
instead of
\mathfrak{M}(A,<_{A})
.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any coupling
C
, any decoration
\kappa
of
C
and any real positive number
\delta>0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{I_{C}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}e^{i\Omega_{j}\varepsilon^{-2}t_{j}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{\rho\in\mathfrak{M}\left(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\right)}\frac{e^{n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}t}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{-i\xi t}}{n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}-i\xi}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}\frac{1}{n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{j}\right)}\mathrm{d}\xi,

where

\displaystyle\omega^{\rho}_{j}\vcentcolon=\varepsilon^{-2}\sum_{k=\rho^{-1}(j)}^{n_{T}(C)}\Omega_{\rho(k)}.

(3.31)

Proof.

To make notations lighter, we will simply denote
n_{T}(C)
by
n_{T}
in what follows. First, note that there exists a negligible set
\mathcal{Z}_{C}\subset[0,t]^{n_{T}}
such that

\displaystyle I_{C}(t)=\mathcal{Z}_{C}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\rho\in\mathfrak{M}\left(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\right)}\left\{(t_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}\in[0,t]^{n_{T}}~\middle|~0\leq t_{\rho(1)}<\cdots<t_{\rho(n_{T})}\leq t\right\}.

(3.32)

We thus get that

\displaystyle\int_{I_{C}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}e^{-i\Omega_{j}\varepsilon^{-2}t_{j}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in\mathfrak{M}\left(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\right)}\int_{0\leq t_{\rho(1)}<\cdots<t_{\rho(n_{T})}\leq t}\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}e^{i\Omega_{\rho(k)}\varepsilon^{-2}t_{\rho(k)}}\mathrm{d}t_{\rho(k)}.

We make the change of variables
s_{1}=t_{\rho(1)}
and
s_{k}=t_{\rho(k)}-t_{\rho(k-1)}
for
k\in\llbracket 2,n_{T}\rrbracket
. Using
\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}s_{\ell}=t_{\rho(k)}
and the fact that the Jacobian determinant of this change of variables is 1 we obtain, for a given
\rho\in\mathfrak{M}\left(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\right)
,:

\displaystyle\int_{0\leq t_{\rho(1)}<\cdots<t_{\rho(n_{T})}\leq t}\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}e^{i\Omega_{\rho(k)}\varepsilon^{-2}t_{\rho(k)}}\mathrm{d}t_{\rho(k)}

\displaystyle=\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{n_{T}}}\mathbbm{1}_{[0,t]}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n_{T}}s_{k}\right)\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}e^{i\omega^{\rho}_{\rho(k)}s_{k}}\mathrm{d}s_{k}.

where
\omega^{\rho}_{\rho(k)}
is defined in (
3.31
).
For any
x>0
and some fixed
\eta>0
, we now use the identity

\mathbbm{1}_{[0,t]}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\delta(t^{\prime}-x)\mathbbm{1}_{[-\infty,t]}(t^{\prime})e^{\eta(t^{\prime}-x)}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}

(here
\delta
is the Dirac distribution) and Fubini’s theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0\leq t_{\rho(1)}<\cdots<t_{\rho(n_{T})}\leq t}

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}e^{i\Omega_{\rho(k)}\varepsilon^{-2}t_{\rho(k)}}\mathrm{d}t_{\rho(k)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{n_{T}}}\mathbbm{1}_{[-\infty,t]}(t^{\prime})e^{\eta(t^{\prime}-\sum_{k=1}^{n_{T}}s_{k})}\delta\left(t^{\prime}-\sum_{k=1}^{n_{T}}s_{k}\right)\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}e^{i\omega^{\rho}_{\rho(k)}s_{k}}\mathrm{d}s_{k}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.

The change of variables
(t^{\prime},s_{1},\dots,s_{n_{T}})\mapsto(t^{\prime}-\sum_{k=1}^{n_{T}}s_{k},s_{1},\dots,s_{n_{T}})
, followed by another application of Fubini’s theorem, yields that what above

=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\delta(t^{\prime})\underbrace{e^{\eta t^{\prime}}\left(\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{n_{T}}}\mathbbm{1}_{[-\infty,t]}\left(t^{\prime}+\sum_{k=1}^{n_{T}}s_{k}\right)\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}e^{i\omega^{\rho}_{\rho(k)}s_{k}}\mathrm{d}s_{k}\right)}_{F(t^{\prime})}dt^{\prime}=\langle\delta,F\rangle_{L^{2}}

Observe that
F,\hat{F}\in L^{1}
since

\displaystyle\hat{F}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-(i\xi-\eta)t^{\prime}}\left(\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{n_{T}}}\mathbbm{1}_{[-\infty,t]}\left(t^{\prime}+\sum_{k=1}^{n_{T}}s_{k}\right)\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}e^{i\omega^{\rho}_{\rho(k)}s_{k}}\mathrm{d}s_{k}\right)dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{n_{T}}}e^{-(i\xi-\eta)(t^{\prime}-\sum_{k=1}^{n_{T}}s_{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{[-\infty,t]}(t^{\prime})\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}e^{i\omega^{\rho}_{\rho(k)}s_{k}}\mathrm{d}s_{k}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-(i\xi-\eta)t^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{[-\infty,t]}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\,\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{(i\xi-\eta+i\omega^{\rho}_{\rho(k)})s_{k}}ds_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{e^{(\eta-i\xi)t}}{\eta-i\xi}\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}\frac{1}{\eta-i(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{\rho(k)})}

so recalling that
\sqrt{2\pi}\hat{\delta}=1
we finally get

\langle\delta,F\rangle_{L^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{(\eta-i\xi)t}}{\eta-i\xi}\prod_{k=1}^{n_{T}}\frac{1}{\eta-i(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{\rho(k)})}\,d\xi.

The choice
\eta=n_{T}\delta^{-1}
concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Note that
\omega^{\rho}_{j}
defined in (
3.31
) can alternatively be expressed as

\displaystyle\omega^{\rho}_{j}=\varepsilon^{-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\\
j\leq_{\rho}j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Omega_{j^{\prime}}.

(3.33)

This representation formula for oscillating integrals already allows us to start the estimate of
\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
.

Lemma 3.6
.

The following decomposition holds

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)=\sum_{\rho\in\mathfrak{M}\left(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\right)}\widehat{F^{\rho}_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)

(3.34)

where
\widehat{F^{\rho}_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)
satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F^{\rho}_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)^{\frac{1}{p}}}{\left|n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}-i\xi\right|}\mathrm{d}\xi

(3.35)

for some
p>2d
and where we defined

\displaystyle A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\\
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\in(\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C))\setminus\mathcal{R}(C)}\left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}\frac{\chi_{j}^{p}}{\left|n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}-i(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{j})\right|^{p}}.

(3.36)

Proof.

Applying Lemma
3.5
to (
2.40
) we obtain the decomposition (
3.34
) with

\displaystyle\widehat{F^{\rho}_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}(-i)^{n_{T}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)}e^{-i\Omega_{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}t}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)}q_{j}

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{e^{n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}t}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{-i\xi t}}{n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}-i\xi}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}\frac{1}{n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}-i(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{j})}\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{-}}M^{c(\ell),c(\sigma(\ell))}(\varphi(\ell)\kappa(\ell))^{\varphi(\ell)}.

We use the fact that the maps
M^{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}
are continuous with compact support included in
B(0,R)
and the results of Lemma
2.2
to obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|\widehat{F^{\rho}_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\varepsilon^{n_{B}(C)}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi)}{\left|n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}-i\xi\right|}\mathrm{d}\xi,

where we used
t\leq\delta
to absorb the term
e^{n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}t}
in the universal constant
\Lambda
and where we used the notations

\displaystyle f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\\
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)\end{subarray}}f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi,\kappa),

\displaystyle f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi,\kappa)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\prod_{j\in(\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C))\setminus\mathcal{R}(C)}\left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}\frac{\chi_{j}}{\left|n_{T}(C)\delta^{-1}-i(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{j})\right|}.

We now wish to apply Hölder’s inequality on the sum over
\kappa
defining
f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi)
. For this, we proceed similarly to what done in Section
3.1.2
and first rewrite
f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi)
as

\displaystyle f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in B(0,R)^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}\\
K(r)(\kappa)=k\end{subarray}}f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi,\kappa),

where
r
denotes the root of the first tree in the coupling
C
. Since
k\neq 0
, we can assume that there exist numbers
b_{\ell}
non all vanishing such that
K(r)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}b_{\ell}K(\ell)
. By performing a change of variable similar to the one of Section
3.1.2
and a counting argument similar to the one at the beginning of Section
3.1.3
we can prove that

\displaystyle\#\left\{\kappa\in B(0,R)^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}~\middle|~K(r)(\kappa)=k\right\}\leq\Lambda^{n+1}L^{\frac{nd}{2}},

where we also used
\#\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}=\frac{n}{2}+1
. We can thus apply Hölder’s inequality with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
:

\displaystyle f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi)

\displaystyle\lesssim\Lambda^{n+1}L^{\frac{nd}{2}}\left(L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\\
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)\end{subarray}}f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi,\kappa)^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Plugging the expression of
f_{C}^{\rho}(\xi,\kappa)
into this inequality concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proving Proposition
3.3
thus reduces to estimating
A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)
defined by (
3.36
).

3.2.2
Induced orders from
\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)

The previous section shows that we need to estimate
A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)
for each
\rho\in\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C))
. In this section, we define a map from
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C))
to
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}(C))
and
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C))
with particular properties. Following Remark
3.5
, it is equivalent to define a total order relation on a given set or a bijection enumerating this set. Moreover, in (
3.37
) and (
3.41
) below we use the following abuse of notation: if
A
is a set with a total order relation
<
,
A^{\prime}\subset A
is a subset of
A
and
a\in A\setminus A^{\prime}
, we write
A^{\prime}<a
if
a^{\prime}<a
for all
a^{\prime}\in A
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
C
be a coupling and
<_{\rho}
a total order relation on
\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)
, which we enumerate as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)=\{j_{1}<_{\rho}\cdots<_{\rho}j_{n_{T}(C)}\}.

We define a total order relation
<_{\hat{\rho}}
on
\mathcal{N}(C)
in the following way:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(C)=\left\{\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j_{1})<_{\hat{\rho}}j_{1}<_{\hat{\rho}}\cdots<_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j_{n_{T}(C)})<_{\hat{\rho}}j_{n_{T}(C)}<_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{int}^{-1}(-1)\right\},

(3.37)

where
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j)
for
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\sqcup\{-1\}
is defined in (
2.23
) and where
<_{\hat{\rho}}
restricted to each
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j)
is any total order relation compatible with the parentality order.

The following lemma shows in particular that the previous definition defines a map from
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C))
to
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}(C))
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
C
be a coupling,
\rho\in\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C))
and
\hat{\rho}
defined from
\rho
as in Definition
3.3
.

(i)

For all
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(C)
we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})<_{\rho}\mathtt{int}(j)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j,

(3.38)

\displaystyle\mathtt{int}(j)=-1\;\text{ and }\;\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})\neq-1

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j,

(3.39)

\displaystyle\mathtt{int}(j)=\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})\;\text{ and }\;j^{\prime}<j

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j.

(3.40)

(ii)

The relation
<_{\hat{\rho}}
is compatible with the parentality order.

Proof.

Let
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(C)
such that
\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})<_{\rho}\mathtt{int}(j)
. We distinguish three cases:

•

If
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)
, the assumption rewrites
j^{\prime}<_{\rho}j
and by (
3.37
) we get
j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j
.

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)
and
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)
then the assumption rewrites
j^{\prime}<_{\rho}\mathtt{int}(j)
. By definition we have
j\in\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{int}(j))
so that the assumption and (
3.37
) also implies
j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j
.

•

If
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)
and
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)
then the assumption rewrites
\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})<_{\rho}j
and since
j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime}))
, (
3.37
) again implies
j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j
.

In conclusion, this proves (
3.38
). Moreover, if
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(C)
are such that
\mathtt{int}(j)=-1
and
\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})\neq-1
, then
j\in\mathtt{int}^{-1}(-1)
and
j^{\prime}\notin\mathtt{int}^{-1}(-1)
so that (
3.37
) implies
j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j
, thus proving (
3.39
). Finally, (
3.40
) follows from the compatibility of
<_{\hat{\rho}}
restricted to each
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j)
for
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\sqcup\{-1\}
with the parentality order.

We now turn to the compatibility of
<_{\hat{\rho}}
with the parentality order. Let
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(C)
such that
j^{\prime}<j
. This already implies
\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})\leq\mathtt{int}(j)
and we distinguish three cases:

•

If
\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})<\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})
, then the compatibility of
<_{\rho}
with the parentality order implies that
\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})<_{\rho}\mathtt{int}(j)
, which in turn implies
j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j
according to (
3.37
).

•

If
\mathtt{int}(j)=\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})
and
j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)
and
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)
, then
j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j)
and (
3.37
) implies
j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j
.

•

If
\mathtt{int}(j)=\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})=\vcentcolon j^{\prime\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)
, then both
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j^{\prime\prime})
. The fact that
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(j^{\prime\prime})
has been ordered for
<_{\hat{\rho}}
in a way compatible with the parentality order gives
j^{\prime}<_{\hat{\rho}}j
.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We now define a total order on
\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
C
be a coupling and
<_{\hat{\rho}}
a total order relation on
\mathcal{N}(C)
, which we enumerate as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(C)=\{j_{1}<_{\hat{\rho}}\cdots<_{\hat{\rho}}j_{n(C)}\}.

We define a total order relation
<_{\tilde{\rho}}
on
\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
in the following way:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)=\left\{\mathtt{children}(j_{1})<_{\tilde{\rho}}\cdots<_{\tilde{\rho}}\mathtt{children}(j_{n(C)})<_{\tilde{\rho}}r_{2}<_{\tilde{\rho}}r_{1}\right\},

(3.41)

where
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
denote the roots of the first and second tree in
C
respectively, and where
<_{\tilde{\rho}}
restricted to each
\mathtt{children}(j)
is arbitrary if
j\notin\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
and defined by

\displaystyle\mathtt{children}(j)=\{j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}<_{\tilde{\rho}}\{\text{marked children of $j$}\}\},

if
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
, where
j_{\mathrm{unmarked}}
is the unmarked children of
j
and
<_{\tilde{\rho}}
restricted to
\{\text{marked children of $j$}\}
is arbitrary.

The following lemma shows in particular that the combination of the two previous definitions defines a map from
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C))
to
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C))
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
C
be a coupling,
\rho\in\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C))
,
\hat{\rho}
defined from
\rho
as in Definition
3.3
and
\tilde{\rho}
defined from
\hat{\rho}
as in Definition
3.4
.

(i)

For all
j,j^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C))\setminus\mathcal{R}(C)
we have

\displaystyle j<_{\tilde{\rho}}r_{2}<_{\tilde{\rho}}r_{1},

(3.42)

where
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
denote the roots of the first and second tree in
C
respectively, and

\displaystyle\mathtt{parent}(j^{\prime})<_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{parent}(j)\Longrightarrow j^{\prime}<_{\tilde{\rho}}j,

(3.43)

and

\left.\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{parent}(j)=\mathtt{parent}(j^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\\
&\text{$j^{\prime}$ is unmarked}\\
&\text{$j$ is marked}\end{aligned}\right\}\Longrightarrow j^{\prime}<_{\tilde{\rho}}j.

(3.44)

(ii)

The relation
<_{\tilde{\rho}}
is compatible with the parentality order.

Proof.

The properties (
3.42
)-(
3.44
) directly follow from (
3.41
) and the way that each
\mathtt{children}(j)
for
j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)
has been ordered for
<_{\tilde{\rho}}
. It remains to prove the compatibility of
<_{\tilde{\rho}}
with the parentality order. For this, consider
j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
and assume that
j_{1}>j_{2}
. If
j_{1}\in\mathcal{R}(C)
, then
j_{2}\notin\mathcal{R}(C)
and (
3.41
) implies
j_{2}<_{\tilde{\rho}}j_{1}
. Now, if
j_{1}
is not a root,
j_{2}
is not a root either and
j_{2}<j_{1}
implies
\mathtt{parent}(j_{2})<\mathtt{parent}(j_{1})
. Thanks to the second point of Lemma
3.7
, we get
\mathtt{parent}(j_{2})<_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{parent}(j_{1})
and thus
j_{2}<_{\tilde{\rho}}j_{1}
thanks to (
3.41
). This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Note that the properties (
3.38
)-(
3.40
) are equivalent to the definition of
\hat{\rho}
from
\rho
in Definition
3.3
. Similarly the properties (
3.42
)-(
3.44
) are equivalent to the definition of
\tilde{\rho}
from
\hat{\rho}
in Definition
3.4
. However, the compatibility of
\hat{\rho}
and
\tilde{\rho}
with the parentality order is a consequence of how they are defined but also of the compatibility of
\rho
itself with the parentality order.

Now that we can induce a total order
<_{\tilde{\rho}}
on
\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
from any total order
<_{\rho}
on
\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)
, we need to be able to extract a basis of
V(C)
compatible with any such
<_{\tilde{\rho}}
. This is the content of Proposition
3.4
below, which can be found in Section 5 of
[
LS11
]
. Since the proof of Proposition
3.4
requires some minor adaptations from
[
LS11
]
, we postpone its proof to Appendix
A
. First a definition.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
C
be a coupling. We say that
K\in V(C)
is a
signed combination
of the finite family
(K(j))_{j\in J}
of
V(C)
if

\displaystyle K=\sum_{j\in J}s_{j}K(j)

where
s_{j}\in\{-1,0,1\}
.

Remark 3.8
.

Thanks to Definition
3.1
, for any
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
, the linear map
K(j)
is a signed combination of
(K(\ell))_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
C\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
be a coupling and let

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}:\llbracket 1,n(C)+n_{\ell}(C)\rrbracket\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)

be a bijection with
<_{\tilde{\rho}}
its order relation. There exists a subset
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\subset\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
such that

•

\#\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)=\frac{n}{2}+1
,

•

the linear maps
(K(j))_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)}
are linearly independent,

•

for all
j\in\left(\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)\right)\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
,
K(j)
is a signed combination of

\displaystyle(K(j^{\prime}))_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C),j<_{\tilde{\rho}}j^{\prime}}.

3.2.3
The family
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)

The goal of this section is to make an appropriate change of variables in
A^{\rho}_{C}(k,\xi)
, whose expression we recall from (
3.36
):

\displaystyle A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)

\displaystyle=L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\\
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\in(\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C))\setminus\mathcal{R}(C)}\left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}\frac{\chi_{j}^{p}}{\left|n_{T}\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{j}\right)\right|^{p}},

where
\rho
is a fixed element of
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C))
, and where from now on we write
n_{T}
instead of
n_{T}(C)
for brevity (the same applies for similar notations).

We define
\hat{\rho}
from
\rho
as in Definition
3.3
and
\tilde{\rho}
from
\hat{\rho}
as in Definition
3.4
. We apply Proposition
3.4
to
\tilde{\rho}
and get a family
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\subset\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
of cardinal
\frac{n}{2}+1
such that the family
(K(j))_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)}
is linearly independent and any
K(j)
is a signed combination of
(K(j^{\prime}))_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C),j\leq_{\tilde{\rho}}j^{\prime}}
. From
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
, we can define the important family
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\tilde{\rho}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
as above. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\vcentcolon=\left\{\min_{<_{\tilde{\rho}}}\left(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\cap\mathtt{siblings}(j)\right)~\middle|~j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus\mathcal{R}(C)\right\}.

(3.45)

For
i=1,2,3
we also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{i}\vcentcolon=\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)~\middle|~\#\left(\mathtt{children}(j)\cap\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\right)=i\right\}.

Roughly speaking, we go from
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
to its subset
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
by selecting the smallest (for the order defined by
\tilde{\rho}
) element of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
in each
\mathtt{children}
sets. We enumerate
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)=\{j_{1}<_{\tilde{\rho}}\cdots<_{\tilde{\rho}}j_{n^{\prime\prime}}\},\qquad n^{\prime\prime}\vcentcolon=\#\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C).

The following lemma contains important properties concerning
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, which will be used throughout the next sections.

Lemma 3.9
.

The following holds:

(i)

If
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
denote the roots of the first and second tree respectively in the coupling
C
, then
r_{1}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
and
r_{2}\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
;

(ii)

The
\mathcal{P}_{i}
s satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{3}

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

(3.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{2}

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{N}_{T}(C),

(3.47)

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{P}_{1}+2\#\mathcal{P}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{2}

(3.48)

(iii)

The set
\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C))
can be ordered as follows

\displaystyle\mathtt{parent}(j_{1})<_{\hat{\rho}}\cdots<_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{parent}(j_{n^{\prime\prime}}),

(3.49)

and we have

\displaystyle n^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n}{4}

(3.50)

(iv)

There exists at least one
j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
such that
\mathtt{parent}(j)\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)
and
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j))=\emptyset
.

Proof.

We prove each point of the lemma successively:

•

Since
k\neq 0
, we can assume
K(r_{1})\neq 0
. Therefore, if
r_{1}\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
then
K(r_{1})
is a signed combination of
(K(j^{\prime}))_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C),r_{1}<_{\tilde{\rho}}j^{\prime}}
. However, Lemma
3.8
implies that the set
\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)~\middle|~r_{1}<_{\tilde{\rho}}j\right\}
is empty. Therefore, we must have
r_{1}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
. Moreover, since
K(r_{2})=-K(r_{1})
, we must also have
r_{2}\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
. The fact that
r_{1}\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
follows from the definition of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
itself.

•

Assume that there exists
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)
such that
\mathtt{children}(j)\subset\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
. On the one hand, by definition of the decoration of a coupling we have

\displaystyle K(j)=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)}K(j^{\prime}),

(3.51)

which already implies that
j\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
since otherwise (
3.51
) would contradict the linear independence of
(K(j^{\prime}))_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)}
. On the other hand, from the definition of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
and
j\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
, we also have

\displaystyle K(j)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\\
j<_{\tilde{\rho}}j^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}s_{j^{\prime\prime}}K(j^{\prime\prime}),

(3.52)

for some
s_{j^{\prime\prime}}\in\{-1,0,1\}
. Equating the RHS of (
3.51
) and (
3.52
) we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j)}K(j^{\prime})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\\
j<_{\tilde{\rho}}j^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}s_{j^{\prime\prime}}K(j^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=0.

(3.53)

Since
\tilde{\rho}
is compatible with the parentality order (recall Lemma
3.8
), we have
\mathtt{children}(j)\cap\left\{j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)~\middle|~j<_{\tilde{\rho}}j^{\prime\prime}\right\}=\emptyset
. Therefore (
3.53
) contradicts again the linear independence of the family
(K(j^{\prime}))_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)}
. Therefore there exists no
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)
such that
\mathtt{children}(j)\subset\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
, which proves simultaneously (
3.46
) and (
3.47
) since
j\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
cannot belong to
\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)
. For (
3.48
), we apply Remark
2.4
to
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus\{r_{1}\}
: thanks to (
3.46
) and
\#\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)=\frac{n}{2}+1
we directly obtain (
3.48
).

•

According to the construction of
\tilde{\rho}
from
\hat{\rho}
and in particular the property (
3.43
), we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{parent}(j_{1})\leq_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{parent}(j_{2})\leq_{\hat{\rho}}\cdots\leq_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{parent}(j_{n^{\prime\prime}}).

Moreover, since each
j\in\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C))
has exactly one children in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
, the equality case in the above string is impossible so that (
3.49
) holds. For (
3.50
), note first that (
3.49
) already implies that
n^{\prime\prime}=\#\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C))
. Now, since
\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C))=\mathcal{P}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{P}_{2}
, (
3.48
) implies

\displaystyle n^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\#\mathcal{P}_{1}+\#\mathcal{P}_{2}=\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\#\mathcal{P}_{1}\geq\frac{n}{4}.

•

For the last point of the lemma, assume by contradiction that for all
j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
we have
j\in I\sqcup II
where

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathtt{parent}^{-1}(\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)),

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)~\middle|~\mathtt{parent}(j)\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\text{ and }\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j))\neq\emptyset\right\}.

We define a map
f
from
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
to
\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)
in the following way: if
j\in I
, set
f(j)=\mathtt{parent}(j)
, and if
j\in II
, set
f(j)
any element of
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j))
. Since
\mathtt{parent}_{|_{\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)}}
is an injection (which follows for instance from (
3.49
)), we have
\#f^{-1}(j)\leq 2
for every
j\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)
. This implies
n^{\prime\prime}\leq 2n_{B}
, and also
2n_{B}\geq\frac{n}{4}
thanks to (
3.50
). However, the high nodes regime assumption (
3.27
) implies
n_{B}<\frac{n}{10}
, thus leading to a contradiction.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In the next section, the sets
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
will allow us to define a well-chosen change of variables in
A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)
. First, we rearrange the two products appearing in
A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)
. For the first product, we use
\left\langle k\right\rangle\geq\sqrt{m}
and
r_{2}\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
to obtain

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in(\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C))\setminus\mathcal{R}(C)}\left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})}\left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\times\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)}\left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}.

For the second product, we use
\left|n_{T}\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{j}\right)\right|\geq n_{T}\delta^{-1}
and
\chi_{j}\leq 1
to obtain

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)}\frac{\chi_{j}^{p}}{\left|n_{T}\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{j}\right)\right|^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p\left(n_{T}-n^{\prime\prime}_{T}\right)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\cap\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C))}\frac{\chi_{j}^{p}}{\left|n_{T}\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{j}\right)\right|^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p\left(n_{T}-n^{\prime\prime}_{T}\right)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\\
\mathtt{parent}(j)\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\end{subarray}}\frac{\chi_{\mathtt{parent}(j)}^{p}}{\left|n_{T}\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{\mathtt{parent}(j)}\right)\right|^{p}},

where
n^{\prime\prime}_{T}\vcentcolon=\#\left(\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\cap\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C))\right)
and where we changed variables in the product over
\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\cap\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C))
(using the fact that each
j\in\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C))
has exactly one child in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
). Putting everything together, we get

\displaystyle A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)\leq\Lambda^{n+1}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p\left(n_{T}-n^{\prime\prime}_{T}\right)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)\\
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})}\left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)}A_{j}(\kappa,\xi),

(3.54)

where
A_{j}(\kappa,\xi)
is defined by

A_{j}(\kappa,\xi)\vcentcolon=\left\{\begin{aligned} \left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}&,\quad\text{if $\mathtt{parent}(j)\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)$,}\\
\left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\chi_{\mathtt{parent}(j)}^{p}\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}&,\quad\text{if $\mathtt{parent}(j)\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)$ and $\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j))\neq\emptyset$,}\\
\frac{\chi_{\mathtt{parent}(j)}^{p}\left\langle\kappa(j)\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}}{\left|n_{T}\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{\mathtt{parent}(j)}\right)\right|^{p}}&,\quad\text{if $\mathtt{parent}(j)\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)$ and $\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j))=\emptyset$.}\end{aligned}\right.

(3.55)

3.2.4
A change of variable in
A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)

The following lemma exhibits an important structure in the quantities
A_{j_{i}}(\kappa,\xi)
for
j_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
, which is mainly a consequence of the definition of
\hat{\rho}
and
\tilde{\rho}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For
i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
,
A_{j_{i}}(\kappa,\xi)
is a function of
(K(j_{i^{\prime}})(\kappa))_{i\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}}
and
(K(j)(\kappa))_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)}
.

Proof.

We fix
i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
and first prove that each
K(j)
for
j\in\mathtt{siblings}(j_{i})
is a function of

\displaystyle\left(\left(K(j_{i^{\prime}})\right)_{i\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}},\left(K(j)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)}\right).

This is obviously true for
j_{i}
, so consider
j^{\prime}\in\mathtt{siblings}(j_{i})\setminus\{j_{i}\}
. If
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
, then
j^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
since
j^{\prime}\neq j_{i}
and
j_{i}
cannot have another sibling in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
. The claim thus holds in the case
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
. If
j^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
, then by definition of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
,
K(j^{\prime})
is a signed combination of
\left(K(j^{\prime\prime})\right)_{j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C),j^{\prime\prime}>_{\tilde{\rho}}j^{\prime}}
. However, if
j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
is such that
j^{\prime\prime}>_{\tilde{\rho}}j^{\prime}
, then either
j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathtt{siblings}(j^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)=\{j_{i}\}
or
j^{\prime\prime}>_{\tilde{\rho}}j_{i}
(recall (
3.41
)) so that
j=j_{i^{\prime}}
with
i^{\prime}>i
. The claim thus also holds in the case
j^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
.

In order to prove the lemma, it remains to prove that
A_{j_{i}}(\kappa)
only depends on
K(j)
for
j\in\mathtt{siblings}(j_{i})
. This is obvious if
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)
. In the case where
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)
and
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))\neq\emptyset
, then
A_{j_{i}}(\kappa)
involves also
\chi_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}
, which thanks to (
2.29
) depends on
K(j)
for
j\in\mathtt{siblings}(j_{i})
. Finally, if
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)
and
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))=\emptyset
, then
A_{j_{i}}(\kappa)
involves also
\omega^{\rho}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}
. From (
3.33
) we obtain

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\omega^{\rho}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}=\Delta_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{T}(C)\\
j^{\prime}>_{\rho}\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\end{subarray}}\Omega_{j^{\prime}},

where we also used (
2.24
) and
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))=\emptyset
to write
\Omega_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}=\Delta_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}
. Using (
2.24
) again we get

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\omega^{\rho}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}=\Delta_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(C)\\
\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})>_{\rho}\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\end{subarray}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}.

By definition of
\hat{\rho}
(recall (
3.37
)),
\mathtt{int}(j^{\prime})>_{\rho}\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})
is equivalent to
j^{\prime}>_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})
so that

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\omega^{\rho}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}=\Delta_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(C)\\
j^{\prime}>_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\end{subarray}}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}.

First, note that (
2.19
) and
K(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))=\sum_{j\in\mathtt{siblings}(j_{i})}K(j)
imply that
\Delta_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}
depends on
K(j)
for
j\in\mathtt{siblings}(j_{i})
. For the same reason, if
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(C)
then
\Delta_{j^{\prime}}
is a function of
K(j^{\prime\prime})
for
j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j^{\prime})
. Moreover, if
j^{\prime}>_{\hat{\rho}}\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})
, then (
3.43
) implies that
j^{\prime\prime}>_{\tilde{\rho}}j_{i}
for all
j^{\prime\prime}\in\mathtt{children}(j^{\prime})
. Therefore, also using the definition of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
,
\Delta_{j^{\prime}}
is a function of
\left(\left(K(j^{\prime\prime\prime})\right)_{j^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C),j^{\prime\prime\prime}>_{\tilde{\rho}}j_{i}}\right)
, which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

As in Section
3.1.2
, we will now perform a change of variables in the sum over
\kappa
in (
3.54
), chosen so that we benefit from the structure exhibited in the previous lemma. Recall that a decoration map of a coupling can be seen as an element of
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}
. Therefore the sum over
\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)
with the constraint
\kappa(\mathcal{L}(C)_{+})\subset B(0,R)
can be written as a sum over
\kappa\in B(0,R)^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}
with the constraint that
K(r_{1})(\kappa)=k
. Now consider the map

\displaystyle\Psi:\kappa\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}\longmapsto\left(\left(K(j_{i})(\kappa)\right)_{i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket},\left(K(j)(\kappa)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})},K(r_{1})(\kappa)\right)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\frac{n}{2}+1}.

Since
r_{1}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
and
\left(K(j)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)}
is a basis of
V(C)
,
\Psi
is a bijection. Moreover it satisfies
\Psi\left(\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{\mathcal{L}(C)_{+}}\right)\subset\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}+1}
. This allows us to make the change of variables

\displaystyle\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}},(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})},k_{r_{1}}\right)=\Psi(\kappa)

and obtain

\displaystyle A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p\left(n_{T}-n^{\prime\prime}_{T}\right)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})}\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-n^{\prime\prime}}\\
\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{n^{\prime\prime}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})}\left\langle k_{j}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{A}_{j_{i}},

where we defined the quantities
\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}\left((x_{i^{\prime}})_{i\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}},(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})},k,\xi\right)

(3.56)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=A_{j_{i}}\left(\Psi^{-1}\left((x_{i^{\prime}})_{1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}},(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})},k\right),\xi\right).

Note that in (
3.56
) we already took into account the constraint
k_{r_{1}}=k
and also already used the result of Lemma
3.10
, since
\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}
does not depend on
x_{i^{\prime}}
if
i^{\prime}<i
. This last fact allows us to rewrite the above estimate of
A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)
in the following way

\displaystyle A_{C}^{\rho}(k,\xi)

(3.57)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}L^{-\frac{nd}{2}}\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p\left(n_{T}-n^{\prime\prime}_{T}\right)}

\displaystyle\sum_{(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})}\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-n^{\prime\prime}}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})}\left\langle k_{j}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\left(\sum_{x_{n^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\tilde{A}_{j_{n^{\prime\prime}}}\left(\cdots\left(\sum_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\tilde{A}_{j_{1}}\right)\cdots\right)\right).

3.2.5
Estimating individual sums

In this section, we estimate individually each sum
\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}
appearing in (
3.57
). This will be done by highlighting the dependence of
\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}
on the variable
x_{i}
. The estimates will crucially depend on the nature of
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})
and we distinguish five cases:

\mathbf{C1}
:
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\in\mathcal{N}_{B}(C)
,

\mathbf{C2}
:
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\cap\mathcal{P}_{2}
,

\mathbf{C3}
:
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{low}}(C)\cap\mathcal{P}_{1}
,

\mathbf{C4}
:
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{high}}(C)
and
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))\neq\emptyset
,

\mathbf{C5}
:
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{high}}(C)
and
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))=\emptyset
.

Since
\mathtt{parent}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C))=\mathcal{P}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{P}_{2}
, the five above cases cover all possibilities.

In Lemma
3.12
below, we will need to bound and approximate sums over
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, this will be done with the following technical lemma.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a smooth function and let
q>d
.

(i)

If
f(x)\lesssim\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-q}
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}f(x)\lesssim L^{d},

(3.58)

where the implicit constant only depends on
q
;

(ii)

If
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f
is finite and
C_{x,L}=B(x,\frac{1}{2L})
is the
d
-dimensional ball for the
\infty
norm, then

\left|\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}f(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{L^{d+1}}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\sup_{C_{x,L}}\left|\nabla f\right|.

(3.59)

If in addition
|\nabla f(x)|\lesssim\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-q}
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}f(x)\lesssim L^{d-1}+L^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f,

(3.60)

where the implicit constant only depends on
q
.

Proof.

The bound (
3.58
) is a consequence of the Poisson summation formula which implies

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}f(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim L^{d}\sum_{x\in L\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|h(x)|,

where
h
is the Fourier transform of
x\longmapsto\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-q}
. Since
q>d
, the function
x\longmapsto\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-q}
and all its derivatives are in
L^{1}
and thus
h
decays faster than any polynomial, so that the sum
\sum_{x\in L\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|h(x)|
can be bounded independently of
L
. Moreover, if
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f
finite and denoting
C_{x,L}\vcentcolon=B\left(x,\frac{1}{2L}\right)
we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}f(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\int_{C_{x,L}}\left|f(y)-f(x)\right|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{L^{d+1}}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\sup_{C_{x,L}}\left|\nabla f\right|

If
|\nabla f(x)|\lesssim\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-q}
this gives

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}f(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{L^{d+1}}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{L},

where we applied (
3.58
) to
\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-q}
. This estimate imples (
3.60
) and concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
and fix
(x_{i^{\prime}})_{i+1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}}\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{n^{\prime\prime}-i}
,
(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})}\in\left(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-n^{\prime\prime}}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. The following estimate holds:

\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}\left(x_{i},(x_{i^{\prime}})_{i+1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}},(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})},k,\xi\right)\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} L^{d}&,\quad\text{in case $\mathbf{C1}$,}\\
L^{d}\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}&,\quad\text{in cases $\mathbf{C3}$ and $\mathbf{C4}$,}\\
L^{d}\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}\varepsilon^{\alpha_{0}}&,\quad\text{in cases $\mathbf{C2}$ and $\mathbf{C5}$,}\end{aligned}\right.

(3.61)

for some
\alpha_{0}>0
depending only on
\beta
,
p
,
\gamma
,
d
and for
\varepsilon
small enough compared to
\delta
.

Proof.

In this proof, we write
\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}
instead of
\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}\left(x_{i},(x_{i^{\prime}})_{i+1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}},(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})},k,\xi\right)
. We start with the case
\mathbf{C1}
. According to (
3.56
) and (
3.55
) we have
\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}=\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}
. Since
p>2d
and thanks to (
3.58
) we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}\lesssim L^{d},

which proves (
3.61
) in the case
\mathbf{C1}
. We now consider the cases
\mathbf{C3}
and
\mathbf{C4}
. From (
3.56
) and (
3.55
), and also using
\chi^{p}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}\leq 1
we simply obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}\lesssim\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}.

We proceed similarly as for the case
\mathbf{C1}
and obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}\lesssim L^{d}\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p},

which proves (
3.61
) in the case
\mathbf{C3}
and
\mathbf{C4}
.

We now consider the case
\mathbf{C2}
. First, (
3.56
) and (
3.55
) implies that

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}\lesssim\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\chi^{p}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p},

where we used
\left|n_{T}\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi-\omega^{\rho}_{\mathtt{parent}(j)}\right)\right|\leq n_{T}\delta^{-1}
if
\mathtt{int}^{-1}(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))=\emptyset
. By definition of the case
\mathbf{C2}
, we have
\mathtt{siblings}(j_{i})=\{j_{i},j_{i,1},j_{i,2}\}
and we can assume that
j_{i,1}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
and
j_{i,2}\notin\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)
. By definition of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)
we have
j_{i}<_{\tilde{\rho}}j_{i,1}
. We now distinguish two cases:

•

If
j_{i}
is marked, then according to (
3.44
),
j_{i,1}
is marked. Thanks to Definitions
2.1
and
2.15
we have
\chi^{p}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{i}+k_{j_{i,1}}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}}
and therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}\#\left\{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}~\middle|~|x_{i}+k_{j_{i,1}}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}L^{d}\varepsilon^{\gamma d},

where we used (
3.24
).

•

If
j_{i}
is unmarked, then
j_{i,2}
and
j_{i,2}
must be the two marked children of
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})
and thus Definitions
2.1
and
2.15
again implies
\chi^{p}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{|K(j_{i,2})(\kappa)+k_{j_{i,1}}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}}
. Since
K(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))=K(j_{i})+K(j_{i,1})+K(j_{i,2})
this becomes
\chi^{p}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{|K(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))(\kappa)-x_{i}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}}
. Since
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})>j_{i}
, the second point of Lemma
3.8
implies that
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})>_{\tilde{\rho}}j_{i}
and thus
K(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))(\kappa)
is a signed combination of
(x_{i^{\prime}})_{i+1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}}
and
(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C))}
and thus we again obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}\#\left\{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}~\middle|~|K(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))(\kappa)-x_{i}|\lesssim 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}L^{d}\varepsilon^{\gamma d}.

By taking
0<\alpha_{0}<\gamma d
, we have proved (
3.61
) in the case
\mathbf{C2}
.

We now consider the case
\mathbf{C5}
. First, (
3.56
) and (
3.55
) implies that

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}\lesssim\frac{\chi_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}^{p}\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}}{\left|n_{T}\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}\right)\right|^{p}}.

According to Definition
2.1
and the fact that
\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{high}}(C)
,
\chi_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}^{p}
is a sum of four terms: three terms corresponding to the situation with at least two low-frequency interactions among the three possible ones, and one term corresponding to three high-frequency interactions. More precisely, writing as above
\mathtt{siblings}(j_{i})=\{j_{i},j_{i,1},j_{i,2}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}\in\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\tilde{A}_{j_{i}}\lesssim B_{1}+B_{2}+B_{3}+B_{4},

with

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{i}+K(j_{i,1})(\kappa)|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}}\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{i}+K(j_{i,2})(\kappa)|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}},

\displaystyle B_{2}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|K(j_{i,1})(\kappa)+x_{i}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}}\mathbbm{1}_{|K(j_{i,1})(\kappa)+K(j_{i,2})(\kappa)|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}},

\displaystyle B_{3}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|K(j_{i,2})(\kappa)+x_{i}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}}\mathbbm{1}_{|K(j_{i,2})(\kappa)+K(j_{i,1})(\kappa)|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}},

\displaystyle B_{4}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{i}+K(j_{i,1})(\kappa)|\geq\varepsilon^{\gamma}}\mathbbm{1}_{|x_{i}+K(j_{i,2})(\kappa)|\geq\varepsilon^{\gamma}}\mathbbm{1}_{|K(j_{i,1})(\kappa)+K(j_{i,2})(\kappa)|\geq\varepsilon^{\gamma}}}{\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle^{\frac{p}{2}}\left|n_{T}\delta^{-1}-i\left(\xi+\omega^{\rho}_{\mathtt{parent}(j_{i})}\right)\right|^{p}}.

We start by estimating
B_{2}
and
B_{3}
. Since
K(j_{i,1})(\kappa)+K(j_{i,2})(\kappa)=K(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))(\kappa)-x_{i}
we have

\displaystyle B_{2}+B_{3}\lesssim\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle^{-\frac{p}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|K(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))(\kappa)-x_{i}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}}.

As above, one can show that
K(\mathtt{parent}(j_{i}))(\kappa)
is a function of
(x_{i^{\prime}})_{i+1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}}
and
(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)\sqcup\{r_{1}\})}
. Therefore we obtain

\displaystyle B_{2}+B_{3}\lesssim\left(\frac{\delta}{n_{T}}\right)^{p}L^{d}\varepsilon^{\alpha_{0}},

(3.62)

if
0<\alpha_{0}<\gamma d
. For
B_{1}
, we claim that at least one of the
K(j_{i,q})(\kappa)
for
q=1,2
only depends on
(x_{i^{\prime}})_{i+1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime\prime}}
and
(k_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(C)}
. First, note that
j_{i,1}
and
j_{i,2}
