, we get that

\tilde{F}_{\tilde{C}}(k)=-iL^{-d}\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}\iota\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{1},-k_{1},k)q_{\iota\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A_{1}))}^{\eta}(k_{1},-k_{1},k)\tilde{F}_{\tilde{C}_{1}}(-k_{1})\tilde{F}_{\tilde{C}_{2}}(k)

We use that ternary
q
is symmetric in its left and right variables and that

\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{1},-k_{1},k)=(1-\chi)(|k+k_{1}|\varepsilon^{-\gamma})(1-\chi)(|k-k_{1}|\varepsilon^{-\gamma})=\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}\ell}}(k,-k_{1},k_{1})

to obtain

\tilde{F}_{\tilde{C}}(k)=-iL^{-d}\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}\iota\chi_{{\mathrm{low-}\ell}}(k,-k_{1},k_{1})q_{\iota\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A_{1}))}^{\eta}(k,-k_{1},k_{1})\tilde{F}_{\tilde{C}_{1}}(-k_{1})\tilde{F}_{\tilde{C}_{2}}(k)

We perform the change of variables
k_{1}\leftarrow-k_{1}
to get the result. Checking that
\tilde{C}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
is done as in the first situation.

We now prove the third part of the proposition. We pass from
\mathtt{Res}_{n}
to
\mathcal{R}_{n}
by simply transforming right-resonant nodes into left-resonant nodes. Therefore a given
C\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
corresponds to
2^{n}
couplings in
\mathtt{Res}_{n}
with no middle-resonant node. We obtain these
2^{n}
couplings by deciding at each node if we do the reverse operation than the one we have described, that is transforming a left-resonant node into a right-resonant node.
∎

Example 5.1
.

We illustrate the transformation we perform in the above proof in the situations

\displaystyle C=

\displaystyle((T_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A_{1},A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}),\iota,\eta),A^{\prime}_{2},\sigma),

\displaystyle C=

\displaystyle((T_{{\mathrm{low-}\ell}}(A_{2},A_{1},A_{1}^{\prime}),\iota,\eta),A^{\prime}_{2},\sigma),

\displaystyle C=

\displaystyle(A_{2},(T_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A_{1},A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})\iota,\eta),\sigma),

\displaystyle C=

\displaystyle(A_{2},(T_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A_{2}^{\prime},A_{1},A_{1}^{\prime})\iota,\eta),\sigma).

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathrm{low-}r}}

5.2.2
The maps
\mathtt{BtoR}
and
\mathtt{RtoB}

In the rest of this section, we will relate resonant couplings with only right-resonant nodes to binary trees.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

•

We set
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
the set of couples
(R,\rho)
such that
R\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
and
\rho
is a striclty increasing injective map from
\mathcal{N}_{T}(R)
to
\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

•

We also set
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{ord}}
the set of couples
(R,\rho)
such that
R\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
and
\rho
is a striclty increasing injective map from
\mathcal{N}_{T}(R)
to
\llbracket 1,n_{T}(R)\rrbracket
.

Our goal is to show that
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{ord}}
is in bijection with some set of binary trees with ordered nodes. For that we will use
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
as an intermediary. First, we introduce our new binary trees.

Definition 5.5
.

We introduce the following objects.

(i)

We define the set
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
of binary trees by induction as follows: we set
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{0}=\{\perp\}
and

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n+1}=\left\{N_{\mathtt{left}}(B_{1},B_{2}),N_{\mathtt{right}}(B_{1},B_{2})~\middle|~(B_{1},B_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{1}}\times\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{2}},\;n_{1}+n_{2}=n\right\},

where
N_{\mathtt{left}}
and
N_{\mathtt{right}}
are new binary nodes. We denote by
\mathcal{N}_{l}(B)
(resp.
\mathcal{N}_{r}(B)
) the set of nodes of type
N_{\mathtt{left}}
(resp.
N_{\mathtt{right}}
).

(ii)

If
B\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
, a sign map on
B
is a map
\mathcal{N}(B)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(B)\longrightarrow\{\pm\}
such that for any node, the sign of its left child matches its own.

(iii)

If
B\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
, a colour map on
B
is a map
c:\mathcal{N}(B)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(B)\longrightarrow\{0,1\}^{2}
satisfying the following recursive rule: if
j\in\mathcal{N}(B)
with left and right children denoted by
\ell
and
r
, the colours of
\ell
and
r
can be read off in the following table (depending on the nature of
j
and its colour):

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&c(j)={\bf 0}&c(j)={\bf 1}&c(j)=\times&c(j)=\overline{\times}\\
\hline\cr j\in\mathcal{N}_{l}(B)&({\bf 0},\times)&({\bf 1},\overline{\times})&(\times,\times)&(\overline{\times},\overline{\times})\\
\hline\cr j\in\mathcal{N}_{r}(B)&({\bf 0},\times)&({\bf 1},\overline{\times})&(\times,\overline{\times})&(\overline{\times},\times)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(5.12)

where we use the notations
{\bf 0}=(0,0)
,
{\bf 1}=(1,1)
,
\times=(0,1)
and
\overline{\times}=(1,0)
.

We denote by
\mathcal{B}_{n}
the set of triplets
(B,\varphi,c)
where
B\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
,
\varphi
is a sign map on
B
and
c
a colour map on
B
.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

•

We set
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
the set of couples
((B,\varphi,c),\rho)
such that
(B,\varphi,c)\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\rho
is a striclty increasing injective map from
\mathcal{N}(B)
to
\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

•

We also set
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{ord}}
the set of couples
((B,\varphi,c),\rho)
such that
(B,\varphi,c)\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\rho
is a striclty increasing injective map from
\mathcal{N}(B)
to
\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
.

We apply Remark
5.1
to
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
in the following sense. If
((B,\varphi,c),\rho)\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
with
n>0
, then the tree
B
writes
B=\left(N_{*}\left(\tilde{B}_{1},\tilde{B}_{2}\right),\varphi,c,\rho\right)
. We allow ourselves to also write
B=(N_{*}(B_{1},B_{2}),\iota,E,M)
with for
i=1,2
,
B_{i}=\left(\tilde{B}_{i},\varphi_{|\mathcal{N}(B_{i})\cup\mathcal{L}(B_{i})},c_{|\mathcal{N}(B_{i})\cup\mathcal{L}(B_{i})},\rho_{|\mathcal{N}(B_{i})}\right)
and
(\iota,E,M)=(\varphi,c,\rho)(\mathtt{root}(B))
.

Moreover, we set
\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{\mathtt{map}}
(res.
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
) the set of couples
(C,\rho)
(resp.
(A,\rho)
such that
C\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
(resp.
A\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
) and
\rho
is a strictly increasing injective map from
\mathcal{N}(C)
(resp.
\mathcal{N}(A)
) to
\mathbb{N}
. We allow ourselves to write
(A,\rho)\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
with
n>0
as

(A,\rho)=T_{*}((A_{1},\rho_{1}),(A_{2},\rho_{2}),(A_{3},\rho_{3})),\iota,\eta,M)

(resp.
B_{*}((A_{1},\rho_{1}),(A_{2},\rho_{2})),\iota,\eta,M)
) where
A=(T_{*}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}),\iota,\eta)
(resp.
(B_{*}(A_{1},A_{2}),\iota,\eta)
, where
\rho_{i}=\rho_{|\mathcal{N}(A_{i})}
and
M=\rho(\mathtt{root}(A))
. We remark that the images of
\rho_{i}
do not intersect. If
(C,\rho)\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{\mathtt{map}}
, we allow ourselves to write
(C,\rho)
as
(C,\rho)=((A,\rho_{|\mathcal{N}(A)}),(A^{\prime},\rho_{|\mathcal{N}(A^{\prime})}),\sigma)
where
C=(A,A^{\prime},\sigma)
.

Definition 5.7
.

We define the function

\displaystyle\mathtt{BtoR}:\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}\longrightarrow\bigcup_{n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{C}_{n_{1},n_{2}}^{\mathtt{map}}

recursively in the following way:

•

If
n=0
, then an element
B
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is a triplet
(\perp,\iota,E)
where
\iota\in\{\pm\}
and
E\in\{0,1\}^{2}
. In that case
\mathtt{BtoR}(B)
is the coupling

((\bot,\iota,\eta),(\bot,-\iota,\eta^{\prime}),\sigma)

where
(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=E
and
\sigma
is the only function possible.

•

If
n>0
then
B\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
writes
B=(N_{*}(B_{1},B_{2}),\iota,E,M)
with
*\in\{\mathtt{left},\mathtt{right}\}
and
B_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}}^{\mathtt{map}}
with
n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1
. Define
C_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathtt{BtoR}(B_{i})
and write
C_{i}=(A_{i},A^{\prime}_{i},\sigma_{i})
and
E=(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
:

–

If
*=\mathtt{left}
, then we introduce
A=(N_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A_{2},A_{2}^{\prime},A_{1}),\iota,\eta,M)
. We set
C=(A,A^{\prime}_{1},\sigma)
where
\sigma
is defined as

\sigma(l)=\left\{{\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma_{1}(l)&\mathrm{if}\,l\in\mathcal{L}(C_{1})\\
\sigma_{2}(l)&\mathrm{otherwise.}\end{array}}\right.

–

If
*=\mathtt{right}
, then we introduce
A^{\prime}=(N_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A_{2},A^{\prime}_{2},A^{\prime}_{1}),-\iota,\eta^{\prime},M)
. We set
C=(A_{1},A^{\prime},\sigma)
where
\sigma
is defined as

\sigma(l)=\left\{{\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma_{1}(l)&\mathrm{if}\,l\in\mathcal{L}(C_{1})\\
\sigma_{2}(l)&\mathrm{otherwise.}\end{array}}\right.

Example 5.2
.

We draw the transformation that
\mathtt{BtoR}
performs on a few examples.
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet\,\bullet}

\bullet

\bullet

\mathtt{BtoR}(B_{1})

\bullet

\bullet

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}

\rightarrowtail
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{low-}r}}

\mathtt{BtoR}(B_{1})

Definition 5.8
.

We define the following sets:

•

We define
\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{map}}_{n}(\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime},M)
to be the set of couples
(R,\rho)
such that
(R,\rho)\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
with the sign of the root of the left tree of
R
being
\iota
and the colour of the root of the left tree of
R
is
\eta
and the colour of the root of its right tree is
\eta^{\prime}
and
\max\mathrm{Im}\rho=M
.

•

We define
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}(\iota,E,M)
to be the set of couples
(B,\rho)\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
such that the colour of the root of
B
is
E
, its sign is
\iota
and
\max\mathrm{Im}\rho=M
.

By convention, we set
\mathcal{B}^{\mathtt{map}}_{0}(\iota,E,M)=\{(\bot,\iota,E)\}
and

\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{map}}_{0}(\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime},M)=\{((\bot,\iota,\eta),(\bot,-\iota,\eta^{\prime}),\sigma)\}

where
\sigma
is trivial.

At this stage, it is not clear that
\mathtt{BtoR}
maps indeed
\cup_{n}\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
to couplings because of the colour condition and the properties that the maps
\rho
need to satisfy (namely that it is increasing and injective). This the purpose of the next proposition.

Proposition 5.3
.

The image of
\mathcal{B}^{\mathtt{map}}_{n}(\iota,E,M)
by
\mathtt{BtoR}
is included in
\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{map}}_{n}(\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime},M)
where
(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=E
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
. What we prove by induction is not only the proposition but also the fact that for all
(B,\rho_{B})\in\mathcal{B}^{\mathtt{map}}_{n}(\iota,E,M)
, writing
(R,\rho_{R})=\mathtt{BtoR}((B,\rho_{B}))
, we have that
\mathrm{Im}\rho_{R}=\mathrm{Im}\rho_{B}
. If
n=0
, this is due to the definition of
\mathtt{BtoR}
and the fact that
\mathrm{Im}\rho_{B}=\mathrm{Im}\rho_{R}=\emptyset
.

If
n>0
, we keep the same notations as in Definition
5.7
. We deal with the case
*=\mathtt{left}
. We assume that
B\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}(\iota,E,M)
and
B_{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\mathtt{map}}_{n_{i}}(\iota_{i},E_{i},M_{i})
. By induction, we have that

C_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{map}}_{n_{i}}(\iota_{i},\eta_{i},\eta^{\prime}_{i},M_{i})
where
(\eta_{i},\eta^{\prime}_{i})=E_{i}
.

First, we get automatically that the number of nodes of
C
is
n
. Then we have to check that the condition of colour is satisfied by the root
r
of
A
. We do this in the case
\eta=0
. By definition, the colour of
r
is
\eta
. In this case, the colour
E
is either
(0,0)
or
\times
. This implies that
E_{2}=\times=(0,1)
and
E_{1}
is either
(0,0)
or
\times
. In any case, we have that the colour of the root of
A_{2}
is
0
, the one of
A_{2}^{\prime}
is
1
and the one of
A_{1}
is
0
, which matches the colour condition on trees. The colour of the root of
A_{2}^{\prime}
is either
0
or
1
. This yields that
C\in\mathcal{C}_{m_{1},m_{2}}^{\mathtt{map}}(\iota,\iota^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime})
for some
\iota^{\prime},m_{1},m_{2}
.

Because
C_{2}
is a coupling, the set
\mathtt{offsprings}(r)
is self-coupled. Using that all the nodes below
r
are resonant, and thus their bushes are self-coupled, this implies that the bush of
r
is self-coupled. By definition of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, we have that
\iota_{1}=\iota
, which implies that the sign of the root of
A_{1}
is
\iota
. Therefore,
r
is resonant, and
\iota^{\prime}=-\iota
.

Finally,
\rho_{i}
respects the order of parentality and the images of
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are disjoint (because their images are the sames as the images of the maps in
B_{1},B_{2}
). For all
j<r
, we have that
\rho(j)\leq\max(M_{1},M_{2})<M
. This ensures that
\rho
respects the order of parentality, is injective and, setting
B=(B^{\prime},\rho_{B})
, we have that
\mathrm{Im}\rho=\mathrm{Im}\rho_{B}
. Therefore, we have
C\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}(\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime},M)
.

The cases
\eta=1
and
*=\mathtt{right}
follows the same lines. The condition colour on the binary trees
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is made to ensure that the image of
\mathtt{BtoR}
is indeed included in couplings, and the sign condition is made to match the sign condition of resonant nodes.
∎

We now define a map which will be ultimately proved to be the inverse of
\mathtt{BtoR}
.

Definition 5.9
.

We define the function

\displaystyle\mathtt{RtoB}:\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}\longrightarrow\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}

recursively in the following way:

•

If
n=0
, then an element
R
of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is of the form
((\bot,\iota,\eta),(\bot,-\iota,\eta^{\prime}),\sigma)
where
\sigma
is the trivial coupling, where
\iota\in\{\pm\}
and
(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
. In that case
\mathtt{RtoB}(R)
is the tree
(\bot,\iota,(\eta,\eta^{\prime}))
.

•

If
n>0
then
R\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
writes
R=((A,A^{\prime},\sigma),\rho)
.

–

If
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A))=\max\mathrm{Im}\rho
, then
C=(A,A^{\prime},\sigma)
writes
C=((T_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A_{1},A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}),\iota,\eta),A^{\prime}_{2},\sigma)
such that
C_{i}\vcentcolon=(A_{i},A_{i}^{\prime},\sigma_{|\mathcal{L}(A_{i})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{i}^{\prime})})\in\mathcal{R}_{n_{i}}
with
n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1
. We have that
(C_{i},\rho_{|\mathcal{N}(C_{i})})\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{map}}_{n_{i}}
. We set
B_{i}=\mathtt{RtoB}((C_{i},\rho_{|\mathcal{N}(C_{i})}))
and

\mathtt{RtoB}((C,\rho))=(N_{\mathtt{left}}(B_{2},B_{1}),\iota,(\eta,\eta^{\prime}),\max\mathrm{Im}\rho)

where
\eta^{\prime}=c(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))
.

–

If
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))=\max\mathrm{Im}\rho
, then
C=(A,A^{\prime},\sigma)
writes
C=(A_{2},(T_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A_{1},A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime}),-\iota,\eta^{\prime}),\sigma)
such that
C_{i}\vcentcolon=(A_{i},A_{i}^{\prime},\sigma_{|\mathcal{L}(A_{i})\sqcup\mathcal{L}(A_{i}^{\prime})})\in\mathcal{R}_{n_{i}}
with
n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1
. We have that
(C_{i},\rho_{|\mathcal{N}(C_{i})})\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{map}}_{n_{i}}
. We set
B_{i}=\mathtt{RtoB}((C_{i},\rho_{|\mathcal{N}(C_{i})}))
and

\mathtt{RtoB}((C,\rho))=(N_{\mathtt{right}}(B_{2},B_{1}),\iota,(\eta,\eta^{\prime}),\max\mathrm{Im}\rho)

where
\eta=c(\mathtt{root}(A))
.

Note that in the previous definition, we used implicitly the fact that if
n>0
and
R=((A,A^{\prime},\sigma),\rho)\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
then either
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A))=\max\mathrm{Im}\rho
or
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))=\max\mathrm{Im}\rho
. This is true since
\mathrm{Im}\rho\subset\mathbb{N}
is finite and not empty (because
n>0
) and thus
\max\mathrm{Im}\rho
is well-defined, and also since, although
\mathtt{root}(A)
or
\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime})
might not be nodes, both cannot be leaves. Hence, either
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A))
or
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))
is well-defined and maximal.

Example 5.3
.

We draw the transformation that
\mathtt{RtoB}
performs on a few examples. Below, we always have
M^{\prime}<M
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet\,\bullet}
\mathtt{left},\iota,M
\bullet

\bullet
\mathtt{RtoB}(R_{2}),\iota
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}
\mathtt{RtoB}(R_{1})
\rightarrowtail
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{low-}r}}
\iota,M
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}
\iota
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}

R_{1}
R_{2}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}

\bullet

\bullet
\mathtt{RtoB}(R_{2}),\iota

\rightarrowtail

\bullet

\bullet
\mathtt{RtoB}(R_{2}),\iota

\rightarrowtail
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathrm{low-}r}}
-\iota

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}
-\iota
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}
\iota,M^{\prime}
R_{1}
R_{2}

It is not so clear that
\mathtt{RtoB}
maps indeed
\cup_{n}\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
to
\cup_{n}\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
because it is not clear that the colour and sign conditions are respected. It is also unclear why the properties of the maps
\rho
(namely that it is increasing and injective) should be respected. This the purpose of next proposition.

Proposition 5.4
.

The image of
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}(\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime},M)
by
\mathtt{RtoB}
is included in
\mathcal{B}^{\mathtt{map}}_{n}(\iota,(\eta,\eta^{\prime}),M)
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same lines as the one of Proposition
5.3
.
∎

Proposition 5.5
.

The map
\mathtt{BtoR}
is the inverse of the map
\mathtt{RtoB}
.

Proof.

Once Propositions
5.3
and
5.4
have been stated, it is explicit that the maps have been constructed to be the inverse of each other but for the fact if
((A,A^{\prime},\sigma),\rho)
is the image under
\mathtt{BtoR}
of

B=(N_{\mathtt{left}}(B_{1},B_{2}),\iota,E,M)

(resp.

B=(N_{\mathtt{right}}(B_{1},B_{2}),\iota,E,M)),

then
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A))>\rho(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))
(resp.
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A))<\rho(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))
) or
A^{\prime}
(resp.
A
) is a leaf. However, this is due to the fact that
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A))=M
(resp.
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))=M
) and that if it exists
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))\in\mathrm{Im}(\rho_{B_{1}})
(resp.
\rho(\mathtt{root}(A))\in\mathrm{Im}(\rho_{B_{1}})
) where
\rho_{B_{1}}
is the restriction of
\rho_{B}
to
\mathcal{N}(B_{1})
and
B=(B^{\prime},\rho_{B}))
. By definition,
\mathrm{Im}(\rho_{B_{1}})\subseteq([|0,M-1|])
.
∎

Corollary 5.1
.

The set
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{ord}}(\iota,(\eta,\eta^{\prime}))
is in bijection with
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime})
.

5.2.3
Contributions of resonant couplings

Definition 5.10
.

We define:

•

For any
(R,\rho_{R})\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
, we set

F_{R}^{\rho_{R}}(t,k)\vcentcolon=(-i)^{n}L^{-nd}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(C)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(R)}q_{j}\prod_{l\in\mathcal{L}_{-}(R)}M^{c(l),c(\sigma(l))}(-\kappa(l))\frac{t^{n}}{n!}.

•

For any
(B,\rho_{B})\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{map}}
, we set

G_{B}^{\rho_{B}}\vcentcolon=F_{R}^{\rho_{R}}

where
(R,\rho_{R})=\mathtt{BtoR}(B,\rho_{B})
.

•

For
B\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, we set

G_{B}\vcentcolon=\sum_{\rho_{B}\in\mathfrak{M}(B)}G_{B}^{\rho_{B}}.

Remark 5.4
.

We note that first, for any
R\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
, we have that

\widehat{F_{R}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)=\sum_{\rho_{R}\in\mathfrak{M}(R)}F_{R}^{\rho_{R}}(t,k).

What is more, the map
\mathtt{BtoR}
does not need the order on nodes to be defined. As a consequence if
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are two orders on the nodes of
B
, setting
(R_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{i})=\mathtt{BtoR}(B,\rho_{i})
, we have that
R_{1}=R_{2}
. The reverse statement is not true.

In the rest of the subsection, we prove some recursive structure over
G_{B}
using the recursive structure over
F_{R}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
B\in\mathcal{B}_{n}(\iota,E)
. If
n=0
, we have that

G_{B}(t,k)=M^{E,\iota}(k).

Otherwise,
B
writes
(N_{*}(B_{1},B_{2}),\iota,E)
and if
*=\mathtt{left}
, setting
E=(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
, we have that

G_{B}(t,k)=-\iota i\int_{0}^{t}G_{B_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)q_{-\iota\varphi(\mathtt{root}(B_{2})}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)G_{B_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})\mathrm{d}\tau

and if
*=\mathtt{right}
, we have that

G_{B}(t,k)=\iota i\int_{0}^{t}G_{B_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)q_{\iota\varphi(\mathtt{root}(B_{2})}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)G_{B_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})\mathrm{d}\tau.

Proof.

The case
n=0
follows from the definition of the different objects. Indeed, if
\iota=+
, then by definition

G_{B}(t,k)=M^{E}(k)=M^{E,+}(k)

and if
\iota=-
, we have

G_{B}(t,k)=\overline{M^{E}(-k)}=M^{E,-}(k).

If
n>0
, let
\rho
be any element of
\mathfrak{M}(B)
and
R
be such that there exists
\rho_{R}
such that
(R,\rho_{R})=\mathtt{BtoR}(B,\rho)
. We have that

G_{B}=\#\mathfrak{M}(B)G_{B}^{\rho}=\#\mathfrak{M}(B)F_{R}^{\rho_{R}},

and

F_{R}^{\rho_{R}}=\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\tilde{F}_{R}.

We set

G_{B}=\#\mathfrak{M}(B)\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\tilde{G}_{B}.

We therefore have that
\tilde{G}_{B}=\tilde{F}_{R}
. Assume that
B=(N_{\mathtt{left}}(B_{1},B_{2}),\iota,E)
. Then
R
writes
(A,A^{\prime}_{1},\sigma)
where
A=N_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A_{2},A^{\prime}_{2},A_{1})
and where
R_{i}:=(A_{i},A_{i}^{\prime},\sigma_{|\mathcal{L}(A_{i})\cup\mathcal{L}(A^{\prime}_{i})})=\mathtt{BtoR(B_{i})}
. As we have already seen, we have that

\tilde{F}_{R}(k)=-i\tilde{F}_{R_{1}}(k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}q_{\mathtt{root}(A)}\tilde{F}_{R_{2}}(k_{2})

where we used that
\mathcal{D}_{k}(R)
is the set of maps
\kappa:\mathcal{L}(R)\cup\mathcal{N}(R)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
such that
\kappa_{|\mathcal{L}(R_{1})\cup\mathcal{N}(R_{1})}\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(R_{1})
and such that there exists
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
such that
\kappa_{|\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\cup\mathcal{L}(R_{2})}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}}(R_{2})
. With these notations, we have

q_{\mathtt{root}(A)}=\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A))\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)q^{c(\mathtt{root}(A)}_{\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A))\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}_{2}))}(k_{2},-k_{2},k).

Thanks to Proposition
5.3
, we have that
\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A))=\iota
,
\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}_{2}))=-\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A_{2}))=-\varphi(\mathtt{root}(B_{2}))
and
c(\mathtt{root}(A))=\eta
.
We therefore have

\tilde{G}_{B}(k)=-\iota i\tilde{G}_{B_{1}}(k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)q^{\eta}_{-\iota\varphi(\mathtt{root}(B_{2}))}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\tilde{G}_{B_{2}}(k_{2}).

We now use that
\#\mathfrak{M}(B)\frac{t^{n}}{n!}=\int_{I_{B}(t)}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}(B)}dt_{j}
where

I_{B}(t)=\{(t_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}(B)}\;|\;j<j^{\prime}\Longrightarrow t_{j}<t_{j^{\prime}}\}

with the order of parentality on the nodes of
B
to get the induction relation

G_{B}(t,k)=-\iota i\int_{0}^{t}G_{B_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)q_{-\iota\varphi(\mathtt{root}(B_{2}))}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)G_{B_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})\mathrm{d}\tau

We turn to the case
B=(N_{\mathtt{right}}(B_{1},B_{2}),\iota,E)
. Then
R=(A_{1},A^{\prime},\sigma)
where
A^{\prime}=N_{\mathrm{low-}r}(A_{2},A^{\prime}_{2},A^{\prime}_{1})
and where
R_{i}:=(A_{i},A_{i}^{\prime},\sigma_{|\mathcal{L}(A_{i})\cup\mathcal{L}(A^{\prime}_{i})})=\mathtt{BtoR(B_{i})}
. We have that

\tilde{F}_{R}(k)=-i\tilde{F}_{R_{1}}(k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}q_{\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime})}\tilde{F}_{R_{2}}(k_{2})

where we used that
\mathcal{D}_{k}(R)
is the set of maps
\kappa:\mathcal{L}(R)\cup\mathcal{N}(R)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
such that
\kappa_{|\mathcal{L}(R_{1})\cup\mathcal{N}(R_{1})}\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(R_{1})
and such that there exists
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
such that
\kappa_{|\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\cup\mathcal{L}(R_{2})}\in\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}}(R_{2})
. With these notations, we have

q_{\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime})}=\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)q^{c(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))}_{\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}_{2}))}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k).

Thanks to Proposition
5.3
, we have that
\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))=-\iota
,
\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}_{2}))=-\varphi(\mathtt{root}(A_{2}))=-\varphi(\mathtt{root}(B_{2}))
and
c(\mathtt{root}(A^{\prime}))=\eta^{\prime}
.
We therefore have

\tilde{G}_{B}(k)=\iota i\tilde{G}_{B_{1}}(k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)q^{\eta^{\prime}}_{\iota\varphi(\mathtt{root}(B_{2}))}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)\tilde{G}_{B_{2}}(k_{2}),

and we conclude as before.
∎

Notation 5.1
.

We introduce the notation

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}(t)=\sum_{C\in\mathtt{Res}_{n}(\iota,-\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\hat{F}_{C}(\varepsilon^{-2}t).

Proposition 5.7
.

We have that
X_{\mathtt{res},0}^{\iota,E}(t,k)=M^{E,\iota}(k)
and if
n>0
, we have

\displaystyle X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,E}(t,k)

\displaystyle=-2\iota i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{\iota,E}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\Big(q_{-\iota\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}-q_{\iota\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})\Big)\mathrm{d}\tau.

where
E=(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
and
E_{2}^{*}
is the color of the right child of a
*
node with color
E
.

Proof.

By definition

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,E}(t)=\sum_{C\in\mathtt{Res}_{n}(\iota,-\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\hat{F}_{C}(\varepsilon^{-2}t).

According to Proposition
5.2
, we have that

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,E}(t)=2^{n}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(\iota,-\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\hat{F}_{R}(\varepsilon^{-2}t).

We may chose to order the nodes in each
R
and get

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,E}=2^{n}\sum_{(R,\rho_{R})\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{ord}}(\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime})}F_{R}^{\rho_{R}}.

We use that
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\mathtt{ord}}(\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{ord}}(\iota,E)
are in bijection to get

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,E}=2^{n}\sum_{(B,\rho_{B})\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\mathtt{ord}}(\iota,E)}G_{B}^{\rho_{B}}.

We may sum back on
\rho_{B}
and get

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,E}=2^{n}\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{n}(\iota,E)}G_{B}.

(5.13)

For
n=0
, we use that
\mathcal{B}_{0}(\iota,E)
is reduced to one element and we use Proposition
5.6
.

If
n>0
, we use the induction property in Proposition
5.6
. For this, we use that
B\in\mathcal{B}_{n}(\iota,E)
write
B=(N_{*}(B_{1},B_{2}),\iota,E)
in a unique way, with
B_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}}(\iota_{i},E_{i}^{*})
where
n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1
, where
\iota_{1}=\iota
and
(E_{1}^{*},E_{2}^{*})
are the colors prescribed by Table
5.12
. The sign
\iota_{2}
may be chosen freely. We note that according to the table, we have that
E_{1}^{*}=E
. We deduce

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,E}=\\
2^{n}\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\Big(\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}}(\iota,E)\\
B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}}(\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}})\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.69046pt(-\iota i)\int_{0}^{t}G_{B_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)q_{-\iota\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)G_{B_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})d\tau\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}}(\iota,E)\\
B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}}(\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}})\end{subarray}}\iota i\int_{0}^{t}G_{B_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)q_{\iota\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)G_{B_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})d\tau\Big).

Using Equation (
5.13
), this becomes

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,E}=2\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\Big(-\iota i\int_{0}^{t}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{\iota,E}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)q_{-\iota\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}d\tau\\
\iota i\int_{0}^{t}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{\iota,E}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)q_{\iota\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})d\tau\Big).

Using the fact that

\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)=(1-\chi)(|k-k_{2}|\varepsilon^{-\gamma})(1-\chi)(|k+k_{2}|\varepsilon^{-\gamma})=\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)

and factorising yields

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{\iota,E}=-2\iota i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{\iota,E}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(q_{-\iota\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}(\tau,k_{2})-q_{\iota\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})d\tau\Big),

which concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

For all
E=(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
we set
\bar{E}=(\eta^{\prime},\eta)
.
We have that

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,\bar{E}}(t,k)=X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{-,E}(t,-k)=\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}.

Proof.

We prove this lemma by induction on
n
. For
n=0
, we have

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,\bar{E}}(t,k)=\overline{M^{\bar{E}}(k)}=M^{E}(k)=\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}

and

X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{-,E}(t,-k)=\overline{M^{E,-}(-k)}=M^{E}(k).

By the induction relation of Proposition
5.7
, we have that

\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}=2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{+,E}(\tau,k)}L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(\overline{q_{-\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)}\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}}(\tau,k_{2})-\overline{q_{\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)}\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})}d\tau\Big).

We can use the induction hypothesis and obtain
\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}}(\tau,k_{2})=X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{-\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}(\tau,-k_{2})
. We get

\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}=2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{+,E}(\tau,k)}L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(\overline{q_{-\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{-\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}(\tau,-k_{2})-\overline{q_{\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{-\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,-k_{2})d\tau\Big).

We perform the change of variable
\iota_{2}\leftarrow-\iota_{2}
and
k_{2}\leftarrow-k_{2}
and get

\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}=2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{+,E}(\tau,k)}L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(-k_{2},k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(\overline{q_{\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(-k_{2},k_{2},k)}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}(\tau,k_{2})-\overline{q_{-\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(-k_{2},k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})d\tau\Big).

(5.14)

The induction hypothesis then implies
\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{+,E}(\tau,k)}=X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{-,E}(\tau,-k)
and we use
\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(-k_{2},k_{2},k)=\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)
and
\overline{q_{\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(-k_{2},k_{2},k)}=q_{\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)
to obtain

\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}=2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{-,E}(\tau,-k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)\\
\times\Big(q_{\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}(\tau,k_{2})-q_{-\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})d\tau\Big).

We recognize

\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}=X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{-,E}(t,-k).

We now go back to (
5.14
), use the induction hypothesis to get that
\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{+,E}(\tau,k)}=X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{+,\bar{E}}(\tau,k)
, use that
\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(-k_{2},k_{2},k)=\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)
and rearrange the sum to get

\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{+,\bar{E}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(\overline{q_{-\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(-k_{2},k_{2},-k)}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})-\overline{q_{\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(-k_{2},k_{2},k)}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}(\tau,k_{2})d\tau\Big).

Using now
\overline{q_{-\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(-k_{2},k_{2},-k)}=q_{-\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)
and
E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}=(\bar{E})_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}
for any
E
(according to Table (
5.12
)) we obtain

\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{+,\bar{E}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(q_{-\iota_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},(\bar{E})_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}(\tau,k_{2})-q_{\iota_{2}}^{\eta}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},(\bar{E})_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})d\tau\Big),

and finally recognize

\overline{X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,E}(t,k)}=X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,\bar{E}}(t,k).

This concludes the proof of the induction.
∎

This lemma implies that a lot of quantities are redondant and we define the main ones:

\displaystyle\rho^{\eta}_{L,n}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,(\eta,\eta)},

\displaystyle\rho^{\times}_{L,n}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,\times}.

The following proposition gathers key recursive properties of
\rho^{\eta}_{L,n}
and
\rho^{\times}{L,n}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

The function
\rho^{\eta}_{L,n}
is real-valued and
\left(\rho^{0}_{L,n},\rho^{1}_{L,n},\rho^{\times}_{L,n}\right)
satisfies the following induction property: if
n=0
we have
\rho^{\eta}_{L,0}=M^{\eta}
and
\rho^{\times}_{L,0}=M^{\times}
and if
n>0
we have

\displaystyle\rho^{0}_{L,n}(t,k)=

\displaystyle 4\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{0}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{0}(k,k_{2})\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\right)\mathrm{d}\tau,

\displaystyle\rho^{1}_{L,n}(t,k)=

\displaystyle 4\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{1}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{1}(k,k_{2})\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})\right)\mathrm{d}\tau,

\displaystyle\rho^{\times}_{L,n}=

\displaystyle 2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{\times}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\left(P(k,k_{2})\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})+M(k,k_{2})\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\right)\mathrm{d}\tau.

Proof.

We start with
\rho^{0}
. We have

\rho^{0}_{L,n}(t,k)=X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,(0,0)}=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n}^{0}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(q_{-\iota_{2}}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}(\tau,k_{2})-q_{\iota_{2}}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})\mathrm{d}\tau\Big).

We remark that
E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}=E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}=\times
and get

\rho^{0}_{L,n}(t,k)=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n}^{0}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(q_{-\iota_{2}}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)-q_{\iota_{2}}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)\Big)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},\times}(\tau,k_{2})\mathrm{d}\tau.

We develop the sum in
\iota_{2}
and get

\rho^{0}_{L,n}(t,k)=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n}^{0}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big[\Big(q_{-}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)-q_{+}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)\Big)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{+,\times}(\tau,k_{2})+\Big(q_{+}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)-q_{-}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)\Big)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{-,\times}(\tau,k_{2})\Big]\mathrm{d}\tau.

We remark that
X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{+,\times}(\tau,k_{2})=\rho^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})
and that
X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{-,\times}(\tau,k_{2})=\overline{\rho^{\times}(\tau,-k_{2})}
and we perform a change of variables into the second part of the sum to get

\rho^{0}_{L,n}(t,k)=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{0}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big[\Big(q_{-}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)-q_{+}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)\Big)\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})+\Big(q_{+}^{0}(-k_{2},k_{2},k)-q_{-}^{0}(-k_{2},k_{2},-k)\Big)\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\Big]\mathrm{d}\tau.

We rearrange the sum using that
q(-k_{1},-k_{2},-k_{3})=\overline{q(k_{3},k_{2},k_{1})}
and obtain

\rho^{0}_{L,n}(t,k)=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{0}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big[\Big(q_{-}^{0}(k,-k_{2},k_{2})-\overline{q_{+}^{0}(k,k_{2},-k_{2})}\Big)\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})+\Big(q_{+}^{0}(k,k_{2},-k_{2})-\overline{q_{-}^{0}(k,-k_{2},k_{2})}\Big)\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\Big]\mathrm{d}\tau.

that is to say, using the definition of
N^{0}(k,k_{2})
,

\rho^{0}_{L,n}(t,k)=2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{0}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\left(\overline{N^{0}(k,k_{2})}\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})-N^{0}(k,k_{2})\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\right)\mathrm{d}\tau,

which may be further expressed as

\rho^{0}_{L,n}(t,k)=4\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{0}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{0}(k,k_{2})\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\right)\mathrm{d}\tau.

The computation for
\rho^{1}
is similar.

We now turn to
\rho^{\times}
. We have

\rho^{\times}_{L,n}=X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,\times}-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}X_{\mathtt{res},n_{1}}^{+,\bar{\times}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(q_{-\iota_{2}}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}}(\tau,k_{2})-q_{\iota_{2}}^{1}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}}(\tau,k_{2})\mathrm{d}\tau\Big).

We use that
E_{2}^{\mathtt{left}}=\times
and that
E_{2}^{\mathtt{right}}=\bar{\times}
and get

\rho^{\times}_{L,n}=X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,\bar{\times}}=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{\times}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(q_{-\iota_{2}}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},\times}(\tau,k_{2})-q_{\iota_{2}}^{1}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{\iota_{2},\bar{\times}}(\tau,k_{2})\Big)\mathrm{d}\tau.

We develop the sum in
\iota_{2}
and get

\rho^{\times}_{L,n}=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{\times}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(q_{-}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{+,\times}(\tau,k_{2})-q_{+}^{1}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{+,\bar{\times}}(\tau,k_{2})\\
q_{+}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{-,\times}(\tau,k_{2})-q_{-}^{1}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)X_{\mathtt{res},n_{2}}^{-,\bar{\times}}(\tau,k_{2})\Big)\mathrm{d}\tau.

We write everything in terms of
\rho^{\times}
and get

\rho^{\times}_{L,n}=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{\times}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(q_{-}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})-q_{+}^{1}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\\
q_{+}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,-k_{2})}-q_{-}^{1}(k_{2},-k_{2},-k)\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,-k_{2})\Big)\mathrm{d}\tau.

We perform a change of variable in the second part of the sum and use the symmetries of
q
to get

\rho^{\times}_{L,n}=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{\times}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(q_{-}^{0}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})-\overline{q_{+}^{1}(k,k_{2},-k_{2})}\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\\
q_{+}^{0}(k,k_{2},-k_{2})\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}-\overline{q_{-}^{1}(k,-k_{2},k_{2})}\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})\Big)\mathrm{d}\tau.

We finally obtain

\rho^{\times}_{L,n}=-2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{\times}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big[\Big(q_{-}^{0}(k,-k_{2},k_{2})-\overline{q_{-}^{1}(k,-k_{2},k_{2})}\Big)\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})+\Big(q_{+}^{0}(k,k_{2},-k_{2})-\overline{q_{+}^{1}(k,k_{2},-k_{2})}\Big)\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\Big].

Using the definitions of
P_{\pm}
this becomes

\rho^{\times}_{L,n}=2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{L,n_{1}}^{\times}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r}(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\\
\times\Big(P_{-}(k,k_{2})\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})+P_{+}(k,k_{2})\overline{\rho_{L,n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,k_{2})}\Big)\mathrm{d}\tau,

which concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

5.3
Analysis of the resonant system

We consider the Cauchy problem for (
1.15
) with initial data
(\rho^{\eta},\rho^{\times})_{|_{t=0}}=(M^{\eta},M^{\times})
. Note that by definition, for all
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the maps
\xi\longmapsto N^{\eta}(\xi,\zeta)
and
\xi\longmapsto P_{\pm}(\xi,\zeta)
are continuous and bounded. The following proposition in particular implies the second part of Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 5.9
.

The Cauchy problem (
1.15
) is locally well-posed in
W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C})\cap W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C})
. What is more, defining
(\rho_{n}^{\eta},\rho_{n}^{\times})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
by induction as

\rho_{0}^{\eta}=M^{\eta},\quad\rho_{0}^{\times}=M^{\times}

and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\rho^{\eta}_{n+1}(t,\xi)=

\displaystyle 4\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\rho_{n_{1}}^{\eta}(\tau,\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}d\zeta\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{\eta}(\xi,\zeta)\rho_{n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)^{\iota(\eta)}\right)

(5.15)

\displaystyle\rho^{\times}_{n+1}(t,\xi)=

\displaystyle 2i\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\rho_{n_{1}}^{\times}(\tau,\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}d\zeta\Big[P_{-}(\xi,\zeta)\rho_{n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)+P_{+}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{\rho_{n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)}\Big],

(5.16)

where
\iota(\eta)
is the sign of
(-1)^{\eta+1}
, we get that there exists
\Lambda>0
such that for all
n
and for

\displaystyle\delta<\left(64\Lambda\left(\max_{\eta}\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}\right)\right)^{-1}

we have

\displaystyle\|\rho_{n}^{*}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\delta],W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{n+1}\delta^{n}c_{n},

(5.17)

\displaystyle\left\|\rho^{*}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\rho_{k}^{*}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\delta],W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle\leq(\Lambda\delta)^{n},

(5.18)

where
c_{n}
is the
n
-th Catalan number and
(\rho^{0},\rho^{1},\rho^{\times})
is the unique maximal solution to (
1.15
).

Proof.

The local well-posedness of (
1.15
) follows from the fact that the quadratic function

F(\rho^{\eta},\rho^{\times}):=\begin{pmatrix}4\rho^{\eta}\int d\zeta\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{\eta}(\cdot,\zeta)\rho^{\times}(\zeta)^{\iota(\eta)}\right)\\
2i\rho^{\times}\int d\zeta\Big[P_{-}(\cdot,\zeta)\rho^{\times}(\zeta)+P_{+}(\cdot,\zeta)\overline{\rho^{\times}(\zeta)}\Big]\end{pmatrix}

is Lipschitz continuous on
W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular, we observe that

\displaystyle\|F(\rho^{\eta},\rho^{\times})^{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\leq 8\|\rho^{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}\|\rho^{\times}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{1}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\|F(\rho^{\eta},\rho^{\times})^{\times}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\leq 8\|\rho^{\times}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}\|\rho^{\times}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{1}_{\xi}}.

There exists then a unique maximal solution
(\rho^{0},\rho^{1},\rho^{\times})\in L^{\infty}([0,\delta];W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, where
\delta>0
depends on the size of the data.

As concerns the estimate for
\rho^{*}_{n}
, we prove by induction that

\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\|\rho^{*}_{n}(t)\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\leq c_{n}8^{n}\delta^{n}\left(\max_{\eta}\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}\right)^{n}\max_{*}\left(\|\rho^{*}_{0}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\right)^{n+1}

where
c_{n}
is the n-th Catalan number. Estimate (
5.17
) will follow by recalling that
c_{n}\leq 4^{n}
and choosing
\Lambda
any fixed constant grater than
32(\max_{\eta}\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}})\max_{*}(\|\rho^{*}_{0}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}})
. The above inequality is trivially true for
n=0
and we suppose it holds true for any
\rho^{*}_{m}
with
0\leq m\leq n
. From definitions (
5.15
), (
5.16
) and the above estimates on
F
, we deduce that for
t\in[0,\delta]

\displaystyle\|\rho^{\eta}_{n+1}(t)\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\leq 8t\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n}\|\rho^{\eta}_{n_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\|\rho^{\times}_{n_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{1}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\leq 8^{n+1}t\delta^{n}\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}(\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}})^{n}\max_{*}(\|\rho^{*}_{0}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}})^{n+2}\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n}c_{n_{1}}c_{n_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{n+1}(8\delta)^{n+1}(\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}})^{n+1}\max_{*}(\|\rho^{*}_{0}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}})^{n+2}

and similarly

\displaystyle\|\rho^{\times}_{n+1}(t)\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\leq 8t\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n}\|\rho^{\times}_{n_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\|\rho^{\times}_{n_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{1}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{n+1}(8\delta)^{n+1}(\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}})^{n+1}\max_{*}(\|\rho^{*}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\xi}\cap L^{1}_{\xi}})^{n+2}.

Up to shortening the time interval to
[0,\delta]
with
\delta<1/(2\Lambda)
, we immediately obtain from (
5.17
) that the series
\sum_{n}\rho^{*}_{n}
converges in
L^{\infty}_{t}([0,\delta],W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. It remains to show that it converges to
\rho^{*}
solution of (
1.15
) and to prove (
5.18
). Let us define
v=(v^{0},v^{1},v^{\times})
as follows

v^{*}:=\rho^{*}-\sum_{n\leq N}\rho^{*}_{n},\quad*\in\{0,1,\times\}

From the equations in (
1.15
) and Definitions (
5.15
), (
5.16
), we derive that
v
solves the following system of equations

v^{*}=\mathfrak{S}^{*}+\mathfrak{L}^{*}(v)+\mathfrak{Q}^{*}(v,v),\quad*\in\{0,1,\times\}

with source term

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}^{\eta}=4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{1},n_{2}\leq N\\
N\leq n_{1}+n_{2}\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\,\rho_{n_{1}}^{\eta}(\tau,\xi)\int d\zeta\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{\eta}(\xi,\zeta)\rho_{n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)^{\iota(\eta)}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}^{\times}=2i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{1},n_{2}\leq N\\
N\leq n_{1}+n_{2}\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\rho_{n_{1}}^{\times}(\tau,\xi)\int d\zeta\Big[P_{-}(\xi,\zeta)\rho_{n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)+P_{+}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{\rho_{n_{2}}^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)}\Big],

linear term

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{\eta}(v)=

\displaystyle 4\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\Big[\rho^{\eta}_{n}(\tau,\xi)\int d\zeta\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{\eta}(\xi,\zeta)v^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)^{\iota(\eta)}\right)+v^{\eta}(\tau,\xi)\int d\zeta\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{\eta}(\xi,\zeta)\rho^{\times}_{n}(\tau,\zeta)^{\iota(\eta)}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{\times}(v)=

\displaystyle 2i\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\rho^{\times}_{n}(\tau,\xi)\int d\zeta\Big[P_{-}(\xi,\zeta)v^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)+P_{+}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{v^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)}\Big]

\displaystyle+2i\sum_{n\leq N}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau v^{\times}(\tau,\xi)\int d\zeta\Big[P_{-}(\xi,\zeta)\rho^{\times}_{n}(\tau,\zeta)+P_{+}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{\rho^{\times}_{n}(\tau,\zeta)}\Big],

and quadratic term

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}^{\eta}(v,v)

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{t}v^{\eta}(\tau,\xi)\int d\zeta\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{\eta}(\xi,\zeta)v^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)^{\iota(\eta)}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}^{\times}(v,v)

\displaystyle=2i\int_{0}^{t}v^{\times}(\tau,\xi)\int d\zeta\Big[P_{-}(\xi,\zeta)v^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)+P_{+}(\xi,\zeta)\overline{v^{\times}(\tau,\zeta)}\Big].

From (
5.17
) and the fact that
\Lambda\delta<1/2
, we immediately derive that

\|\mathfrak{S}^{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}+\|\mathfrak{S}^{\times}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}_{\xi}\cap L^{1}_{\xi}}\leq 8(\|N^{\eta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}})(\Lambda\delta)^{N+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\Lambda\delta)^{n}

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{L}^{\eta}(v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}+\|\mathfrak{L}^{\times}(v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\leq 8(\|N^{\eta}\|_{{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}})\|v\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}(\Lambda\delta)^{n+1}

\displaystyle\leq 16\Lambda\delta(\|N^{\eta}\|_{{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}})\|v\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}

\|\mathfrak{Q}^{\eta}(v,v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}+\|\mathfrak{Q}^{\times}(v,v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}\leq 8\delta(\|N^{\eta}\|_{{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}}+\max_{\pm}\|P_{\pm}\|_{{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}})\|v\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi}}.

Up to choosing a smaller
\delta
as in the statement, we see that the norm of
\mathfrak{L}
as an operator from
L^{\infty}_{t}(W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}\cap W^{1,1}_{\xi})
to itself is smaller than
1/2
. Therefore
\text{Id}-\mathfrak{L}
is invertible, the norm of its inverse is bounded by 2 and the equation for
v
turns into

v=(\text{Id}-\mathfrak{L})^{-1}(\mathfrak{S}+\mathfrak{Q}(v,v)).

Using the estimates recovered above, one can easily check that the operator in the above right hand side is a contraction on the ball

\displaystyle\left\{v\in L^{\infty}_{t}([0,\delta],W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))~\middle|~\|v\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}([0,\delta],W^{1,\infty}\cap W^{1,1})}\leq(\Lambda\delta)^{N}\right\},

which implies (
5.18
) and concludes the proof.
∎

5.4
Final comparison

In this section, we conclude the proof of Theorem
1.1
by proving its third point.

For
A>0
and
L>0
, we denote by
\mathcal{E}_{L,A}
the set of measure bigger than
1-L^{-A}
on which the results of Proposition
4.1
hold. Restricting ourselves to this set, the solution
X^{\eta}
of (
1.7
) is (uniquely) well-defined up to times of order
\delta\varepsilon^{-2}
. We compare

\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\overline{\widehat{X^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right)

to

\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(t,k)

where
[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]=\eta
if
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
or
[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]=(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\times
if
(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=(0,1)=\times
, and where
t\in[0,\delta]
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
. We remark that
\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(t,k)
is well-defined since
\rho^{*}
is continuous in time and phase space. More precisely, we prove that there exists
\nu>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\overline{\widehat{X^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\Big)-\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(t,k)\right|\lesssim L^{-\nu},

for all
t\in[0,\delta]
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
.

We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}

\displaystyle\overline{\widehat{X^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\Big)-\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(t,k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\overline{\widehat{X^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right)-\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}\overline{\widehat{X_{n_{1}}^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X_{n_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right)

(5.19)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}\overline{\widehat{X_{n_{1}}^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X_{n_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right)-\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}\overline{\widehat{X_{n_{1}}^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X_{n_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right)

(5.20)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}\overline{\widehat{X_{n_{1}}^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X_{n_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right)-\sum_{n\leq N(L)}X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}(t,k)

(5.21)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq N(L)}\rho_{L,n}^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(t,k)-\sum_{n\leq N(L)}\rho_{n}^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(t,k)

(5.22)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq N(L)}\rho_{n}^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(t,k)-\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(t,k)

(5.23)

with
X_{\mathtt{res},n}^{+,(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}=\rho_{L,n}^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}
is introduced in Notation
5.1
and expressed recursively in Proposition
5.8
, and
\rho_{n}^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}
is defined in (
5.15
), (
5.16
). We discuss how to bound each of the above lines.

Line (
5.19
): smallness due to the remainder.

Using the result of Theorem
4.1
, it corresponds to

\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\overline{\widehat{{W}^{\eta}_{1}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{{W}_{2}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right)

where
W_{1},W_{2}\in\{X_{\leq N(L)},v\}
, with at least one of the two equal to
v
. Using the estimates derived in Corollary
3.1
and Theorem
4.1
, together with the fact that
H^{s}
is an algebra when
s>d/2
, we derive that

\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{L,A}}\overline{\widehat{{W}^{\eta}_{1}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{{W}_{2}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\right)\lesssim L^{-\left(d+\frac{1}{4}+2A\right)},

for some implicit constant independent of
L
.

Line (
5.20
): smallness due to high probability.

It corresponds to

\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}^{\complement}_{L,A}}\overline{\widehat{X_{n_{1}}^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X_{n_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\Big)

where
\mathcal{E}^{\complement}_{L,A}
denotes the complement of
\mathcal{E}_{L,A}
. We estimate the above terms using Hölder’s inequality twice and Gaussian hypercontractivity. Recalling also estimate (
3.80
), we get that the above sum is

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}L^{-\frac{A}{2}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big|\overline{\widehat{X_{n_{1}}^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X_{n_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}L^{-\frac{A}{2}}(\mathbb{E}|\widehat{X^{\eta}_{n_{1}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)|^{4})^{\frac{1}{4}}(\mathbb{E}|\widehat{X^{\eta}_{n_{2}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)|^{4})^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle\leq L^{-\frac{A}{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}c^{n_{1}}c^{n_{2}}(\mathbb{E}|\hat{X}^{\eta}_{n_{1}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{E}|\hat{X}^{\eta}_{n_{2}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda L^{-\frac{A}{2}}\left(\sum_{n\leq N(L)}(\Lambda\delta)^{\frac{n}{4}}c^{n}\right)^{2}

for some
\Lambda,c>0
. If
\delta
is small enough so that
\Lambda c^{4}\delta<1/2
, we conclude that

\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}^{\complement}_{L,A}}\overline{\widehat{X_{n_{1}}^{\eta}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)}\widehat{X_{n_{2}}^{\eta^{\prime}}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)\Big)\leq\Lambda L^{-\frac{A}{2}},

for some new
\Lambda>0
.

Line (
5.21
): smallness due to resonances.

From (
2.3
), it corresponds to

\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta,\eta^{\prime},-,+}_{n_{1},n_{2}}}\widehat{{F}_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)-\sum_{n\leq N(L)}\sum_{C\in\mathtt{Res}_{n}(\iota,-\iota,\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)

where we recall that
\mathtt{Res}_{n}(\iota,\iota^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\mathtt{Res}_{n}\cap\cup_{n_{1}n_{2}}\mathcal{C}^{\iota,\iota^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
and
\mathtt{Res}_{n}
is the set of couplings with
n
nodes, all resonant. The above difference can be rearranged as follows

\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta,\eta^{\prime},-,+}_{n_{1},n_{2}}\\
C\text{ has a non-resonant node}\end{subarray}}\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}\leq 2N(L)\\
n_{1}>N(L)\text{ or }n_{2}>N(L)\end{subarray}}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta,\eta^{\prime},-,+}_{n_{1},n_{2}}\cap\cup_{n}\mathtt{Res}_{n}}\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k).

(5.24)

On the one hand, the first sum in (
5.24
) can be split into three additional terms:

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}<80}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta,\eta^{\prime},-,+}_{n_{1},n_{2}}\\
C\text{ has a non-resonant node}\end{subarray}}\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k),

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)\\
n_{1}+n_{2}\geq 80\end{subarray}}\sum_{\lfloor\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}\rfloor-40<q\leq\lfloor\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}\rfloor}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta,\eta^{\prime},-,+}_{n_{1},n_{2}}\\
C\text{ has a non-resonant node}\\
n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)=q\end{subarray}}\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k),

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\leq N(L)\\
n_{1}+n_{2}\geq 80\end{subarray}}\sum_{0\leq q\leq\lfloor\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}\rfloor-40}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta,\eta^{\prime},-,+}_{n_{1},n_{2}}\\
C\text{ has a non-resonant node}\\
n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)=q\end{subarray}}\widehat{F_{C}}(\varepsilon^{-2}t,k)

We use Proposition
5.1
and Lemma
2.4
(which counts the couplings with a given number of self-coupled bushes) to deduce that
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are bounded by
\Lambda\varepsilon^{\alpha}
for some large constant
\Lambda
, while we use (
3.2
) and again Lemma
2.4
to get the same bound for
S_{3}
(cf. the same estimate in the proof of Corollary
3.1
).

On the other hand, the second sum in (
5.24
) may be estimated using that if
C\in\mathtt{Res}_{n}
then
n_{\mathrm{scb}}(C)=\frac{n}{2}
and therefore the cardinal of
\mathtt{Res}_{n}(\iota,\iota^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime})
is bounded by some
\Lambda^{n}
. We deduce that it can be estimated using (
3.1
) and the fact that either
n_{1}
or
n_{2}
are bigger than
N(L)
as less than

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}\leq 2N(L)\\
n_{1}>N(L)\text{ or }n_{2}>N(L)\end{subarray}}\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{2}}\leq\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{\frac{N(L)}{2}}

Given that
\Lambda\delta<1
and that
N(L)=\lfloor{(\log L)\rfloor}
,
(\Lambda\delta)^{\frac{N(L)}{2}}
is smaller than a negative power of
L
.

Line (
5.22
): smallness due to recursive properties of resonances.

We recall the recursive expression obtained for
\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}_{L,n}
in Proposition
5.8
as well as the definition (
5.15
)-(
5.16
) of
\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}_{n}
. It can be easily proved by induction that the maps
\xi\mapsto\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}_{L,n}(t,\xi)
and
\xi\mapsto\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}_{n}(t,\xi)
are compactly supported in a ball
B(0,R)
. Furthermore, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
L>0

\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}|\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}_{L,n}(t,k)|\leq\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{n}c_{n},

(5.25)

where
\Lambda\gg 1
is some fixed constant and
c_{n}
denotes the
n
th Catalan number.
Such estimate is the analogue of (
5.17
) and obtained using the same type of argument.

We next prove by induction on
n\leq N(L)
that there exists some
\Lambda,\alpha>0
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}|\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}_{L,n}(t,k)-\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}_{n}(t,k)|\leq\Lambda L^{-\alpha}c_{n}(\Lambda\delta)^{n}.

(5.26)

From this estimate it will follow that line (
5.22
) is bounded by
\Lambda L^{-\alpha}(\log L)
, for some new
\Lambda>0
.
For
n=0
, the two quantities coincide so let us assume that the above statement is true for any
0\leq m\leq n-1
. First, we observe that

\displaystyle\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{L,n}(t,k)=4\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{L,n_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{[\eta,\eta]}(k_{2},k)\rho^{\times}_{n_{2},L}(\tau,k_{2})^{\iota(\eta)}\right)\ d\tau

\displaystyle+4\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{L,n_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}(1-\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r})(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{[\eta,\eta]}(k_{2},k)\rho^{\times}_{L,n_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})^{\iota(\eta)}\right)\ d\tau

and that
(1-\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r})(k_{2},-k_{2},k)
vanishes when
|k\pm k_{2}|\geq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma}
. Therefore, by triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{L,n}(t,k)-\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{n}(t,k)\Big|\leq

\displaystyle 4\Big|\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}(\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{L,n_{1}}(\tau,k)-\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{n_{1}}(\tau,k))L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{[\eta,\eta]}(k_{2},k)\rho^{\times}_{n_{2},L}(\tau,k_{2})^{\iota(\eta)}\right)\ d\tau\Big|

(5.27)

\displaystyle+4\Big|\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{n_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{[\eta,\eta]}(k_{2},k)(\rho^{\times}_{n_{2},L}(\tau,k_{2})^{\iota(\eta)}-\rho^{\times}_{n_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})^{\iota(\eta)})\right)\ d\tau\Big|

(5.28)

\displaystyle+4\Big|\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{n_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{[\eta,\eta]}(k_{2},k)\rho^{\times}_{n_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})^{\iota(\eta)}\right)\ d\tau

(5.29)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{n_{1}}(\tau,k)\int d\zeta\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{[\eta,\eta]}(\zeta,k)\rho^{\times}_{n_{2}}(\tau,\zeta)^{\iota(\eta)}\right)\ d\tau\Big|

\displaystyle+4\Big|\hskip-11.38092pt\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{[\eta,\eta]}_{L,n_{1}}(\tau,k)L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}(1-\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r})(k_{2},-k_{2},k)\mathrm{Im}\left(N^{[\eta,\eta]}(k_{2},k)\rho^{\times}_{L,n_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})^{\iota(\eta)}\right)\ d\tau\Big|

(5.30)

To bound (
5.27
) and (
5.28
) we use the induction hypothesis, the fact that
\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}_{L,m}
and
\rho^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}_{m}
are bounded by
\Lambda^{m+1}\delta^{m}c_{m}
(cf. (
5.25
) and (
5.17
)) and that the sum over
k_{2}
is restricted to a ball
B(0,R)
of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}
. For (
5.29
), we remark that
\zeta\mapsto N^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(\zeta,k)\mathrm{Im}\rho^{\times}_{n}(t,\zeta)
satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
3.11
, hence using (
5.17
) we deduce that for
n_{2}\leq n-1

\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}\left|L^{-d}\sum_{k_{2}}\mathrm{Im}\Big(N^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(k_{2},k)\rho^{\times}_{n_{2}}(\tau,k_{2})\Big)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}d\zeta\ \mathrm{Im}\Big(N^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(\zeta,k)\rho^{\times}_{n_{2}}(\tau,\zeta)\Big)\right|\\
\lesssim L^{-d-1}\sum_{k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}\sup_{C_{k_{2},L}}|\nabla\Big(N^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(\cdot,k)\rho^{\times}_{n_{2}}(\cdot)\Big)|\leq L^{-1}\|N^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{n_{2}}c_{n_{2}}R^{d}.

Plugging this into (
5.29
), we find it is bounded by

4\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n-1}\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{n_{1}+n_{2}+1}c_{n_{1}}c_{n_{2}}L^{-1}\|N^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}R^{d}

and finally by
\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{n}c_{n}L^{-\alpha}
given the fact that
4L^{-1+\alpha}\|N^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{\xi}L^{\infty}_{\zeta}}R^{d}\leq 1
if
L
is sufficiently large and
\alpha<1
.

Finally, to bound (
5.30
) we use estimate (
5.25
) on
\rho^{*}_{L,n}
and the fact that
(1-\chi_{\mathrm{low-}r})(k_{2},-k_{2},k)=0
whenever
|k\pm k_{2}|\geq 2\varepsilon^{\gamma d}
and derive that for
L
sufficiently large

\leq\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{n}2^{d}\varepsilon^{\gamma d}c_{n}\|N^{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\Lambda(\Lambda\delta)^{n}L^{-\alpha},

for some
\alpha>0
. This concludes the proof of (
5.26
).

Line (
5.23
): smallness due to the iterative scheme.

It follows from (
5.18
) that this line is bounded by
(\Lambda\delta)^{2N(L)+2}
. Since
\Lambda\delta<1/2
, this tends to zero uniformly in
t,k
as
L\rightarrow\infty
.

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
3.4

We give the different steps in the proof of the Section 5 of
[
LS11
]
to get this result.

Construction of the momentum graph of
C
.
Given a coupling
C
and an order
\rho
, we build a
momentum graph

G
in the following way:

•

The vertices of
G
are the vertices of
C
(its nodes and leaf vertices), to which we add a vertex that we call a
root vertex
and
n
vertices that we call
fusion vertex
.

•

The edges of
G
are the edges of
C
to which we add two edges from the roots of
C
to the root vertex and
n+2
edges build in the following way: we associate to each minus leaf
\ell
a fusion vertex
v_{\ell}
in a bijective way, and we add the edges
\{\ell,v_{\ell}\}
and
\{\sigma(\ell),v_{\ell}\}
.

Note that
G
is a connected graph. We write its vertex set
V
and its edges set
E
.

Signed vertices of the momentum graph.
For each vertex
v\in V
, we write
E_{+}(v)
and
E_{-}(v)
the following collections of edges:

•

If
v
is the root vertex,
E_{+}(v)=\emptyset
and
E_{-}(v)
are the edges from
v
to the roots of
C
.

•

If
v
is one of the roots of
C
,
E_{+}(v)
contains only the edge connecting
v
to the root vertex and
E_{-}(v)
is the collection of the edges connecting
v
to its children in
C
.

•

If
v
is a node vertex that is not a root of
C
,
E_{+}(v)
is the set containing only the edge connecting
v
to its parent in
C
and
E_{-}(v)
is the collection of edges connecting
v
to its children in
C
.

•

If
v
is a leaf vertex,
E_{+}(v)
is the set containing only the edge connecting
v
to its parent in
C
and
E_{-}(v)
contains only the unique edge connecting
v
to its fusion vertex.

•

If
v
is a fusion vertex,
E_{+}(v)
is the collection of edges connecting
v
to its two leaves and
E_{-}(v)=\emptyset
.

Kirchoff rule.
If
j
is a node or a leaf vertex of
C
, we set
e(j)
the unique edge in
E_{+}(j)
. Note that each edge of
G
is either a
e(j)
or an edge connecting a leaf vertex to a fusion vertex. We associate elements of
V
to the edges of
G
in the following way:

•

If the edge
e\in E
is of the form
e=e(j)
for some
j
then define
\mathfrak{K}(e)=K(j)
.

•

If
e
is connecting a leaf
\ell
to its fusion vertex then define
\mathfrak{K}(e)=K(\ell)
.

The relationships between the
(K(j))_{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)}
of Definition
3.1
can be expressed as a
Kirchhoff rule
at each vertex
v\in V
:

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in E_{+}(v)}\mathfrak{K}(e)=\sum_{e\in E_{-}(v)}\mathfrak{K}(e).

Finally, we define an order on the set of edges
E
of
G
. Define the map
\rho_{G}
in the following way

•

If
e\in E
is of the form
e(j)
set
\rho_{G}(e)=\rho^{-1}(j)
.

•

Denote
E_{fusion}
the set of edges connecting a leaf vertex of
C
to a fusion vertex, note that
|E_{fusion}|=n+2
. Define
\rho_{G|_{E_{fusion}}}
to be any bijection from
E_{fusion}
to
\llbracket-n-1,0\rrbracket
.

This defines a bijection
\rho_{G}:E\rightarrow\llbracket-n-1,n(C)+n+2\rrbracket
and thus an order denoted
<_{G}
with the following properties:

(i)

if
e\in E_{fusion}
then
e<_{G}e(j)
for all
j
which is not the root vertex or any fusion vertex,

(ii)

if
j,j^{\prime}
are not the root vertex nor any fusion vertices then

\displaystyle j<_{\rho}j^{\prime}\Longrightarrow j<_{G}j^{\prime}.

Spanning tree of the momentum graph.
We build the
spanning tree
of
G
. For this, we enumerate the edges of
G
, in the appropriate order

e_{1}<_{G}e_{2}<_{G}\ldots<_{G}e_{R}

where
R=n(C)+2n+4
is the cardinal of
E
. We build a sequence of connected graphs
G_{0}=(V_{0},E_{0})
,
G_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1})
up to
G_{R}=(V_{R},E_{R})
such that
E_{0}=V_{0}=\emptyset
and
G_{r+1}
is obtained from
G_{r}
in the following way:

•

If adding the edge
e_{r+1}
to
G_{r}
creates a loop in
G_{r}
, we set
G_{r+1}=G_{r}
.

•

If adding the edge
e_{r+1}
to
G_{r}
does not create a loop in
G_{r}
, we set
E_{r+1}=E_{r}\sqcup\{e_{r+1}\}
and
V_{r+1}=V_{r}\cup\{v,v^{\prime}\}
where
e_{r+1}=\{v,v^{\prime}\}
in
G
.

This builds a graph
G_{R}=(E_{R},V_{R})
with the following properties:

(i)

By definition,
G_{R}
contains no loop. Moreover, since
G
is connected, so is
G_{R}
. Therefore,
G_{R}
is a tree.

(ii)

Since
G
is connected, we have
V_{R}=V
.

(iii)

The property
V_{R}=V
implies that
G_{R}
is a maximal (for the inclusion order) tree included in
G
. The number of vertices of
G_{R}
is by definition
4+n(C)+\frac{3}{2}n
. Since it is the tree, its number of edges, the cardinal of
E_{R}
is
3+n(C)+\frac{3}{2}n
. Finally, the cardinal of
E
is by definition
R=n(C)+2n+4
. Therefore, if
F\vcentcolon=E\setminus E_{R}
then
|F|=\frac{n+2}{2}
.

We root
G_{R}
by choosing as its root the root vertex. This induces a (partial) order of parentality on
G_{R}
(
a priori
not similar to the order of parentality in
C
or the order that we put on the edges). We denote this order on
V
by
<_{G_{R}}
.

Generating family for
(\mathfrak{K}(e))_{e\in E}
.

We claim that for any
e\in E
, the linear map
\mathfrak{K}(e)
is a signed combination of
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F}
. For this, we set
E(v)=E_{+}(v)\sqcup E_{-}(v)
and define
\sigma_{v}(e)
for
e\in E(v)
as
e\in E_{\sigma_{v}(e)}(v)
. This allows to write the Kirchoff rule as

\displaystyle\forall v\in V,\quad\sum_{e\in E(v)}\sigma_{v}(e)\mathfrak{K}(e)=0.

(A.1)

For all
v\in V
, we set
\mathcal{P}(v)=\{v^{\prime}\in V\;|\;v^{\prime}\leq_{G_{R}}v\}
. If
e\in E_{R}
then
e
is an edge in
G_{R}
therefore
e
writes
\{v,u\}
with
v<_{G_{R}}u
, we claim that

\mathfrak{K}(e)=-\sigma_{v}(e)\sum_{v^{\prime}\leq_{G_{R}}v}\;\sum_{f\in E(v^{\prime})\cap F}\sigma_{v^{\prime}}(f)\mathfrak{K}(f).

(A.2)

This is proved by induction on the cardinal of
\mathcal{P}(v)
:

•

If
\mathcal{P}(v)
is reduced to
\{v\}
, this means that
v
is a leaf in
G_{R}
. Therefore we have that
E(v)\cap E_{R}=\{e\}
. In other words,
E(v)\cap F=E(v)\smallsetminus\{e\}
. The formula (
A.2
) then simply follows from the Kirchoff rule (
A.1
) at the vertex
v
.

•

Otherwise, the Kirchoff rule at
v
yields

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}(e)=-\sigma_{v}(e)\sum_{e^{\prime}\in E(v)\setminus\{e\}}\sigma_{v}(e^{\prime})\mathfrak{K}(e^{\prime}).

Since
e\in E_{R}
, we can divide this sum as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}(e)=-\sigma_{v}(e)\sum_{e^{\prime}\in(E(v)\cap E_{R})\setminus\{e\}}\sigma_{v}(e^{\prime})\mathfrak{K}(e^{\prime})-\sigma_{v}(e)\sum_{f\in E(v)\cap F}\sigma_{v}(f)\mathfrak{K}(f).

If
e^{\prime}
belongs to
E(v)\smallsetminus(F\sqcup\{e\})=E(v)\cap E_{R}
, since
v
can only have one parent in
G_{R}
, this implies that
e^{\prime}=\{v^{\prime},v\}
with
v^{\prime}<_{G_{R}}v
. We deduce by transitivity of
<_{G_{R}}
, that
\mathcal{P}(v^{\prime})\subsetneq\mathcal{P}(v)
and thus we can apply the induction hypothesis to
e^{\prime}
. We deduce

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}(e)

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{v}(e)\sum_{e^{\prime}\in(E(v)\cap E_{R})\setminus\{e\}}\sigma_{v}(e^{\prime})\left(-\sigma_{v^{\prime}}(e^{\prime})\sum_{v^{\prime\prime}\leq_{G_{R}}v^{\prime}}\;\sum_{f\in E(v^{\prime\prime})\cap F}\sigma_{v^{\prime\prime}}(f)\mathfrak{K}(f)\right)

(A.3)

\displaystyle\quad-\sigma_{v}(e)\sum_{f\in E(v)\cap F}\sigma_{v}(f)\mathfrak{K}(f).

Note that by definition we have
\sigma_{v}(e^{\prime})=-\sigma_{v^{\prime}}(e^{\prime})
and thus
\sigma_{v}(e^{\prime})(-\sigma_{v^{\prime}}(e^{\prime}))=1
. Finally, note that summing over the
v^{\prime\prime}\leq_{G_{R}}v^{\prime}
for each child
v^{\prime}
of
v
is equivalent to summing over the
v^{\prime\prime}<_{G_{R}}v
. This implies

\displaystyle\sum_{e^{\prime}\in(E(v)\cap E_{R})\setminus\{e\}}\sigma_{v}(e^{\prime})\left(-\sigma_{v^{\prime}}(e^{\prime})\sum_{v^{\prime\prime}\leq_{G_{R}}v^{\prime}}\;\sum_{f\in E(v^{\prime\prime})\cap F}\sigma_{v^{\prime\prime}}(f)\mathfrak{K}(f)\right)=\sum_{v^{\prime}<_{G_{R}}v}\;\sum_{f\in E(v^{\prime})\cap F}\sigma_{v^{\prime}}(f)\mathfrak{K}(f).

Plugging this into (
A.3
) gives the desired formula.

We have proved that (
A.2
) holds, i.e that each
\mathfrak{K}(e)
is a linear combination of the
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F}
. We can prove in addition that each
\mathfrak{K}(e)
is a signed combination of the
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F}
. For this, let us prove that a given
\mathfrak{K}(f)
can only appear once in (
A.2
). If
f\in F
belongs to
E(v^{\prime})\cap E(v^{\prime\prime})
for some
v^{\prime}\leq_{G_{R}}v
and
v^{\prime\prime}\leq_{G_{R}}v
with
v^{\prime}\neq v^{\prime\prime}
then without loss of generality we can assume that
f_{0}\in E_{+}(v^{\prime})\cap E_{-}(v^{\prime\prime})
. This implies that
\sigma_{v^{\prime}}(f)=-\sigma_{v^{\prime\prime}}(f)
so that the contributions of
\mathfrak{K}(f)
in (
A.2
) cancel. This proves indeed that each
\mathfrak{K}(e)
is a signed combination of the
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F}
.

Triangular structure for
(\mathfrak{K}(e))_{e\in E}
.

We claim that for any
e\in E
, the linear map
\mathfrak{K}(e)
is a signed combination of
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F,f>_{G}e}
. Assume
e\in E_{R}
, write
e=\{v,u\}
with
v<_{G_{R}}u
and take
f<_{G}e
such that
\mathfrak{K}(f)
appears in the formula (
A.2
), that is, that there exists
v^{\prime}\leq_{G_{R}}v<_{G_{R}}u
such that
v^{\prime}
is an extremity of
f
. Write
f=\{v^{\prime},u^{\prime}\}
, the vertex
v^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}
are not necessarily comparable in
G_{R}
. There exists a unique path between
u^{\prime}
and
v
in
G_{R}
. If
u^{\prime}>_{G_{R}}v
or if
u^{\prime}
is incomparable in
G_{R}
to
v
then the path between
u^{\prime}
and
v
necessarily pass by their smallest common ancestor and thus by
u
. This implies that the path from
u^{\prime}
to
v
concatenated with the path from
v
to
v^{\prime}
and
f
forms a loop that contains
e
. But this is not possible because
e>_{G}f
, which means that when we decided whether to add
f
in the spanning tree,
e
was not in the graph and thus
f
was not closing this loop (and not any other, otherwise there would be a loop in
G_{R}
). Therefore
u^{\prime}\leq_{G_{R}}v
. But since it is so,
\mathfrak{K}(f)
appears twice in the formula (
A.2
). As we have already noted, in this case, the contributions where
\mathfrak{K}(f)
is involved cancel each other. We deduce that
\mathfrak{K}(e)
is a signed combination of
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F,f>_{G}e}
.

Final properties of
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F}
.
Since the cardinal of
F
is
\frac{n+2}{2}
, since the family
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F}
generates the vector subspace
V
of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d(n+2)/2},\mathbb{R}^{d})
of dimension
\frac{n+2}{2}
, we deduce that
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F}
is a basis of
V
and thus the
(\mathfrak{K}(f))_{f\in F}
are linearly independant.

It remains to prove that if
f
belongs to
F
, then there exists
j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\sqcup\mathcal{L}(C)
such that
f=e(j)
. This is due to the construction of
G_{R}
. Since the edges that are not of this form are smaller than all the other, we start by deciding whether to add them to the spanning tree. But they are edges connecting a leaf to a fusion vertex and those by themselves can never form a loop. This means that they all belong to
E_{R}
and thus all the
f\in F
are of the form
e(j)
. We deduce that for all
j
, the linear map
K(j)=\mathfrak{K}(e(j))
is a signed combination of the family
(K(q))_{e(q)\in F,q>_{\rho}j}
. Setting
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(C)=\{j\in\mathcal{N}(C)\cup\mathcal{L}(C)\;|\;e(j)\in F\}
concludes the proof of Proposition
3.4
.
